First, there is the period of the contract, namely, 12 months; secondly, the moment in time when each fixed and spot price is determined, that is, for each period of 30 minutes; thirdly, the obligations of PP to pay the floating price, that is, the spot price of electricity fixed each 30 minutes and PCA to pay the fixed price stated in a document which was attached to the confirmation, called a load profile or load shape; fourthly, a statement that the commodity is electricity; fifthly, that the unit of power is megawatt hours (MWh); sixthly, the price source being an electricity pool, that is, the Victorian Power Exchange; seventhly, the settlement date and finally, that the pool price is that determined under the applicable Victorian State code.

  260. The parties had agreed on the volume of notional electricity which is not specified in the confirmation document but can be calculated from the load shape.

  261. The confirmation is to be read with the Master Agreement which, inter alia, provides for payment on netting.

  262. After the contract commenced, each month settlement of the movement of money took place and payment was made.

  263. The collar contracts differed in that there was a price range band so that there was an upper and lower fixed price and the movement of money between the parties did not occur until the spot price exceeded the higher price (cap) or was less than the lower price (floor).

  264. Commodity futures trading has operated for many, many years and some organisations have drafted standard form agreements to apply to over the counter trading, that is, trading not on an exchange. By way of example, the British Bankers Association established a standard form contract known as the British Bankers Association interest rate swap terms in 1987 and in 1992 the International Swaps and Derivatives Association published the standard form contract called the ISDA Multicurrency Master Agreement 1992. There were earlier versions.

  265. This case is concerned with the ISDA Multicurrency-Cross Border Master Agreement ("Master Agreement").

  266. In August 1996, at the first meeting between representatives of the parties, PP stated that it wished their dealings to be governed by the Master Agreement and thereafter negotiations took place between the parties culminating in the execution of a bundle of documents. It comprised the ISDA Master Agreement and a number of additional documents. I will refer to the total bundle as "the Master Agreement".

  267. The Master Agreement is dated 21 November 1996. However, it was executed by Dr Lang on behalf of PP some time between 21 November 1996 and 10 December 1996 when PP's executed copy was handed to PCA which executed it through its company secretary, Mr Shane Breheny, on or about 13 December 1996. The executed copy was forwarded to PP on or about 16 December 1996.

  268. The agreement was varied on 8 October 1997.

  269. It is common ground that the parties executed the bundle of documents comprising the Master Agreement and further, that it was varied in 1997. There is no dispute as to what constitutes the Master Agreement between the parties. The parties disagree as to its application, its meaning and the effect of the confirmation document.

  270. The Master Agreement comprises the following:

    271. (i) ISDA Master Agreement dated 21 November 1996;

    (ii) the 1991 ISDA definitions;

    (iii) the 1993 ISDA commodity derivatives definitions;

    (iv) Schedule to the Master Agreement dated 21 November 1996;

    (v) September 1992 Australian addendum No. 7 (as amended in March 1994 - netting);

    (vi) March 1996 NSW State Electricity Market addendum No. 1;

    (vii) Telephone Dealing Supplement;

    (viii) a six page document in the form of a letter addressed to Mr Doug Telford, Powercor Australia Limited, executed by the parties on or about 7 October 1997;

    (ix) June 1997 Australian addendum No. 13 - Electricity Transactions, which replaced item (vi) above being the March 1996 NSW State Electricity Market addendum No. 1.

  271. The main areas of dispute between the parties concern the application of the agreement to negotiations, the meaning and effect of some of the terms in relation to contracting, intention to contract and authority, and the operation and effect of the Telephone Dealing Supplement.

  272. Another important issue is at what point did the parties conclude an agreement which was binding upon them?

  273. PP contends that no contract came into being until the confirmation document was signed by the parties and exchanged. PCA contends that a contract came into being when the parties agreed on the terms. It submits the contract could be in writing, or partly in writing, oral or partly oral, and implied.

  274. The issues raised involve the construction of the Master Agreement to determine the common intention of the parties.

  275. The court is dealing with a standard form contract that has been drafted and settled by experts in the field of over the counter derivative transactions. It is clear from the many terms in the agreement that the object of the agreement is to regulate and control the transactions, to remove uncertainty and to provide for controls and regulation of the risks involved in the trading. The object is to standardise the manner in which the risks are allocated and managed by appropriate contractual terms.

  276. The court's object in interpreting a contract is to determine the common intention of the parties.

  277. It is trite law that the common intention is to be determined at the date the contract was concluded. On the evidence this is 13 December 1996 and at the date of the variation being 7 October 1997.

  278. The primary source of the common intention is the words of the contract. They are to be considered in context after taking into account the whole document.

  279. The words are to be given their normal every day meaning unless the words bear a special meaning because, they are technical or because of trade usage or custom or the admissible surrounding circumstances show that the parties used a word in a particular sense.

  280. If the language used is clear and definite there is no necessity to resort to any aids to interpret the agreement. The clear language must be given effect to. But if the language is obscure, uncertain or ambiguous, then the court must seek to determine what the parties intended by resort to admissible evidence and aids to construction.

  281. Sometimes linguistic mistakes are made. If it is clear the parties have failed to properly record their intention, then the latter must prevail over the language.

    282. "On the other hand, if one would nevertheless conclude from the background that something must have gone wrong with the language, the law does not require judges to attribute to the parties an intention which they plainly could not have had. Lord Diplock made this point more vigorously when he said in Antaiso Compania Naviera S.A. v Salen Rederierna A.B. (1985) A.C. 191 at 201: 'if detailed semantic and syntactical analysis of words in a commercial context is going to lead to a conclusion that flouts business common sense, it must be made to yield to business commonsense'

    per Lord Hoffman in I.C.S. Ltd v West Bromwich B.S. (1998) 1 WLR at p.913".

    See also Schenker & Co (Aust) Pty Ltd v Maplas Equipment and Services Pty Ltd [1990] VicRp 74; (1990) VR 834 at 837, 840 and 848.

  282. The court avoids a result which is unreasonable and absurd when viewed in a commercial setting. Clearly, parties do not intend an absurd or unjust result. See L. Schuler A.G. v Wickham Machine Tool Sales Ltd [1973] UKHL 2; (1974) AC 235 at pp.251, 255-6, 264 and 272.

  283. But that is not to say the court has a licence to construe the words to achieve a result. Sometimes on a plain meaning of the words the meaning is clear but it is thought that the parties were unlikely to have had that result in mind.

  284. The law is clear. Parties are bound by their contract whether they read it or not and whether they appreciated the significance of its terms. If a term had been drawn to their attention at the time and the likelihood is that the parties would have deleted a term but did not do so, in the absence of compelling evidence of error, the parties are bound.

  285. The court's function is to read the words intelligently in the meaning the parties understood and give effect to them and only if the meaning of the words is open to reasonable doubt does the court consider the consequences. See Total Gas Marketing Ltd v Arco British Ltd (1998) 2 LL. Rep. 209 at 223.

  286. But it is important to construe the words to give effect to what the parties meant. This is important when considering the negotiations in the present case. The parties employed their own language.

    287. "The meaning of words is a matter of dictionaries and grammars: the meaning of the document is what the parties using those words against the relevant background would reasonably have been understood to mean".

    Per Lord Hoffman in I.C.S. Ltd case ibid at p.913.

  287. As his Lordship said at p.912:

    288. "Interpretation is the ascertainment of the meaning which the document would convey to a reasonable person having all the background knowledge which would reasonably have been available to the parties in the situation in which they were at the time of the contract" - ibid at p.912.

  288. The emphasis on giving effect to what the parties as commercial men intended to achieve is not new.

  289. Lord Tomlin in Hillas & Co v Arcos Ltd [1932] UKHL 2; (1932) 38 Com. Cas. 23 at p.29 said -

    290. "The problem for a court of construction must always be so to balance matters that, without the violation of essential principle, the dealings of men may as far as possible be treated as effective, and that the law may not incur the reproach of being the destroyer of bargains".

  290. The dicta encapsulates the philosophy of law of contract: that the reasonable expectations of honest men should be protected.

  291. Steyn L.J. summarised the theme as he called it in First Energy (UK) Ltd v Hungarian International Bank Ltd (1993) 2 LL. L. Rep. 194 at 196 when he said -

    292. "A theme that runs through our law of contract is that the reasonable expectations of honest men must be protected. It is not a rule or a principle of law. It is the objective which has been and still is the principal moulding force of our law of contract. It affords no licence to a judge to depart from binding precedent. On the other hand, if the prima facie solution to a problem runs counter to the reasonable expectations of honest men, this criterion sometimes requires a rigorous re-examination of the problem to ascertain whether the law does indeed compel demonstrable unfairness."

  292. Lord Hoffman summarised the modern approach to interpretation of contracts in I.C. S. Ltd case supra at p.912 when he said after referring to the speeches of Lord Wilberforce in Prenn v Simmonds and Reardon Smith Line Ltd v Yjngvar Hansen-Tangen -

    293. "The result has been subject to one important exception, to assimilate the way in which such documents are interpreted by judges to the common sense principles by which any serious utterance would be interpreted in ordinary life. Almost all the old intellectual baggage of 'legal' interpretation has been discarded."

  293. The exception he was referring to is the situation when the parties have made an error in use of language.

  294. I respectfully agree. Too often lawyers spend inordinate time focusing on words suggesting ambiguity and uncertainty to deny or bring down a contract; an exercise more appropriate to the old days of construing a will on an originating summons.

  295. In carrying out the task of construction the court is not confined to the words of the agreement. Gone are the days when the court construed a contract in a vacuum.

  296. "The time has long passed when agreements, even those under seal, were isolated from the matrix of facts in which they were set and interpreted purely on internal linguistic considerations. There is no need to appeal here to any modern, anti-literal tendencies for Lord Blackburn's well-known judgment in River Wear Commissioners v Adamson (1877) 2 App. Cas. 743 at 763, provides ample warrant for a liberal approach. We must, as he said, inquire beyond the language to see what the circumstances were with reference to which the words were used and the object, appearing from those circumstances which the person using them had in view." Per Lord Wilberforce in Prenn v Simmonds (1971) 1 WLR 1381 at 1383-4.

  297. His Lordship, who wrote the leading speech, held that the evidence concerning the matrix of facts "should be restricted to evidence of the factual background known to the parties at or before the date of the contract, including evidence of the genesis and objectively the 'aim' of the transaction."

  298. The resort to background circumstances and negotiations was discussed in the leading judgment of Mason, J in Codelfa Construction Pty Ltd v State Rail Authority of N.S.W. [1982] HCA 24; (1982) 149 CLR 337 at pp.347 et seq.

  299. With respect to negotiations and surrounding circumstances, his Honour said this at p.352 -

    300. "The true rule is that evidence of surrounding circumstances is admissible to assist in the interpretation of the contract if the language is ambiguous or susceptible of more than one meaning. But it is not admissible to contradict the language of the contract when it has a plain meaning. Generally speaking facts existing when the contract was made will not be receivable as part of the surrounding circumstances as an aid to construction, unless they were known to both parties, although, as we have seen, if the facts are notorious knowledge of them will be presumed. It is here that a difficulty arises with respect to the evidence of prior negotiations. Obviously the prior negotiations will tend to establish objective background facts which were known to both parties and the subject matter of the contract. To the extent to which they have this tendency they are admissible. But in so far as they consist of statements and actions of the parties which are reflective of their actual intentions and expectations they are not receivable. The point is that such statements and actions reveal the terms of the contract which the parties intended or hoped to make. They are superseded by, and merged in, the contract itself. The object of the parol evidence rule is to exclude them, the prior oral agreement of the parties being inadmissible in aid of construction, though admissible in an action for rectification." (Emphasis added.)

  300. Lord Hoffman more recently stated the principle in the I.C.S. Ltd case, supra at p.912 when he stated -

    301. "The background was famously referred to by Lord Wilberforce as 'the matrix of facts', but this phrase is, if anything, an understated description of what the background may include. Subject to the requirements that it should have been reasonably available to the parties and to the exception to be mentioned next, it includes absolutely anything which would have affected the way in which the language of the document would have been understood by reasonable men. The exception is exclusion of negotiation and declarations of subjective intent." (Emphasis added.)

  301. The quest of the court to determine the common intention of the parties requires the court to approach the task on an objective basis. The function of the court is to ascertain the intention from the words. The court is not concerned with what a party actually intended.

    302. "What a man intends and the expression of his intention are two different things. He is bound, and those who take after him are bound, by his expressed intention." - see Simpson v Foxon (1907) p.54 at 57.

  302. The court does not decide what was probably intended to be or what ought to have been or what the parties would have thought about the matter if they had given further consideration to the topic or had been properly advised. The court interprets the document and has no authority to re-write it.

  303. Mason J in Codelfa summarised the approach at p.352 when he said -

    304. "Consequently when the issue is which of two or more possible meanings is to be given to a contractual provision we look, not to the actual intentions, aspirations or expectations of the parties before or at the time of the contract, except in so far as they are expressed in the contract, but to the objective framework of facts within which the contract came into existence, and to the parties' presumed intention in this setting. We do not take into account the actual intentions of the parties and for the very good reason that an investigation of those matters would not only be time consuming but it would also be unrewarding as it would tend to give too much weight to these factors at the expense of the actual language of the written contract."

  304. His Honour, however, expressed the view that there may be a situation when evidence of actual intention of the parties should be allowed to prevail over presumed intention. If the evidence shows that the parties considered a matter and then excluded it from their contract then in those circumstances, "it may be proper to receive evidence of that refusal".

  305. The common sense of his Honour's approach is beyond doubt and I respectfully agree.

  306. Lord Wilberforce expressed the court's approach in the Reardon Smith case (1976) 1 WLR 989 at 997 when he stated -

    307. "What the court must do must be to place itself in thought in the same factual matrix as that in which the parties were. All of these opinions seem to be implicitly to recognise that, in the search for the relevant background, there may be facts which form part of the circumstances in which the parties contract and in which one, or both, may take no particular interest, their minds being addressed to or concentrated on other facts so that if asked they would assert that they did not have those facts in the forefront of their mind, but that will not prevent those facts from forming part of an objective setting in which the contract is to be construed."

  307. It is important to always bears in mind that it is the final document which records the consensus of the parties. The parties, in negotiating a contract, pursue their hopes and expectations with different emphasis and hope to achieve their goal in a certain way. Often words which are used by one party mean something different to the other party and yet, in the end result, the parties use a formula to effect their agreement.

  308. Lord Wilberforce's speech in Prenn v Simmonds supra at p.1385 is a timely reminder with respect to the approach in this case. His Lordship said -

    309. "Far more, and indeed totally, dangerous it is to admit evidence of one party's objective - even if this is known to the other party. However strongly pursued this may be, the other party may only be willing to give it partial recognition, in a world of give and take, men often have to be satisfied with less than they want. So again, it would be a matter of speculation how far the common intention was that the particular objective should be realised."

  309. Further, a court does not construe an agreement in the light of what the parties did or said after the date of the contract. See L. Schuler A.G. v Wickham Machine Tools Sales Ltd, supra. Such evidence is irrelevant to the issue of construction.

  310. Lord Morris, in the Wickham Tools case underlined the point when he said at p.260 -

    311. "If on the true construction of a contract a right is given to a party, that right is not diminished because during some period either the existence of the right or its full extent was not appreciated."

  311. Where the common intention of the parties has been reduced to writing, external evidence is not as a general rule admitted either to show what the actual intention was or to contradict, vary, add to or subtract from the wording of the document.

  312. The broad purpose of the rule was explained by Mason J in Codelfa Construction Pty Ltd supra, at 347 when he said -

    313. "The broad purpose of the parole evidence rule is to exclude extrinsic evidence (except as to surrounding circumstances) including direct statements of intention (except in cases of latent ambiguity) and antecedent negotiation, to subtract from, add to vary or contradict the language of a written instrument."

  313. There are a number of exceptions to that general rule, one of which is surrounding circumstances to which I have referred above.

  314. In construing the provisions of the Master Agreement the starting point to determine the common intention, is the language of the agreement itself.

  315. Lord Mustill has recently reminded the court of the importance of that approach when he said -

    316. "There comes a point at which the court should remind itself that the task is to discover what the parties meant from what they have said, and that to force upon the words a meaning which they cannot fairly bear is to substitute for the bargain actually made one which the court believes could better have been made. This is an illegitimate role for a court. Particularly in the field of commerce, where the parties need to know what they must do and what they can insist on not doing it, is essential for them to be confident that they can rely on the court to enforce their bargain according to its terms." (Emphasis added.) Charter Reinsurance Co Ltd v Fagan (1997) AC 313 at 388.

  316. The ISDA Master Agreement is a standard form document that is used throughout the world in respect of commodity and financial derivatives transactions. It is the product of the labours of many experts in the field of futures trading. Despite its wide use the parties did not refer me to any case which discussed the issues presently in dispute as to its meaning and application.

  317. PCA referred me to two unreported cases in which reference is made to the ISDA agreement but they do not address the issues that arise in this case.

  318. The ISDA Master Agreement is the culmination of the efforts of those experienced in the derivative transaction markets to provide a standard contract to govern their trading relationship.

  319. Dr Alastair Hudson in his work, The Law on Financial Derivatives, 2nd ed. at p.90 states -

    320. "There is a drift towards market standards of documentation. The foremost in this field is the ISDA contract (produced by the International Swaps and Derivatives Association) which has slowly adapted to cover the breadth of derivatives contracts which are arranged between private counter-parties on a bi-lateral basis. Most of these documents are based upon the English and American systems of law ... ".

  320. At p.91 the author wrote -

    321. "Derivatives documentation is structured by organising the way in which the parties will deal with one another across all applicable derivatives of products, under one master agreement. The ISDA structure has formed itself into something close to an ideology: the master agreement provides that the master agreement together with all of the other documents entered into between the parties across all transactions are to be read as being a single agreement. (See cl.1(c)). The purpose behind this provision in the master agreement's terms is to seek to achieve netting across all payments made under all transactions between the parties. The master agreement contains all the central terms about what the parties are to do if one of them defaults, or if tax regulations change, or if there is a dispute as to the manner in which amounts owed reciprocally can be set off against one another, and so forth. ... It sets the parameters for all derivatives transactions. To create a metaphor, the master agreement sets out the rules of the game which the parties are to play, as those rules are understood by the market place, whereas each transaction is a separate playing of that game." (Emphasis added.)

  321. "Netting" means the process of setting off obligations under transactions one against another so that payments between parties are made net rather than gross. See ibid at p.421.

  322. I agree with the observations of Dr Hudson concerning the standard form agreement but it is necessary in the present case to consider the Master Agreement entered into between the parties which the parties negotiated and amended. The schedule to it and the other documents represents the negotiations of the parties and the modification of the Master Agreement.

  323. The evidence reveals that the Master Agreement was the subject of discussion amongst the participants in the electricity industry in New South Wales in 1996 and a committee of representatives was formed to consider the standard documentation with the assistance of a firm of solicitors. Representatives of PP served on that committee, including Mr Murphy.

  324. At the first meeting between Messrs Treacy and Murphy on 13 August 1996 Mr Murphy raised the question of the documentation evidencing their proposed dealings and it was agreed in principle that the dealings would be governed by the ISDA documentation.

  325. Thereafter negotiations took place, drafts were exchanged, the schedule was negotiated and settled and eventually the parties agreed on the form of the documentation. They finally exchanged the executed parts on or about 16 December 1996.

  326. By this time the parties had transacted the first hedge contract and were in the process of negotiating five more.

  327. The signed confirmation document in respect of the first contract had been exchanged and in a form acceptable to the parties. It was prepared by PP.

  328. The confirmation was in the form of a letter prepared by PP and addressed to "Powercor Australia Ltd". It is dated 4 November 1996.

  329. The opening words of the confirmation were -

    330. "Forward commodity contract. The purpose of this letter is to confirm the terms and conditions of the forward commodity contract for electricity entered into between us on the trade date specified below ("Transaction")." (Emphasis added.)

  330. The confirmation went on to provide that the parties agreed to negotiate and sign the Master Agreement and that all provisions contained in the Master Agreement governed the confirmation.

  331. The trade date is noted as "4 November 1996". By definition this is the date of the transaction.

  332. It is noted that the confirmation letter was to confirm a pre-existing transaction.

  333. This form of confirmation was acceptable to the parties prior to the execution of the Master Agreement and all confirmations thereafter used the same verbiage.

  334. Contract No. 1 was a two-way hedge contract for the notional supply of 181 GWhs of electrical energy for the calendar year of 1997. It is pertinent to note the substantial quantity of notional energy and the fact that the period was for one year, matters of importance on the issue of authority. It was negotiated by Mr Murphy on behalf of PP with Mr Treacy of PCA.

  335. The opening words of the confirmation letter were in accordance with a standard form that was accepted by the industry in 1996 where transactions were documented according to the ISDA Agreement.

  336. The rival contentions of the parties concerning the meaning and application of the Master Agreement raised four issues for consideration and determination, namely:

    337. (i) What effect does the Master Agreement have on the dealings between the parties?

    (ii) What constitutes a binding contract in respect of the dealings between the parties?

    (iii) To what dealings did the Telephone Dealings Supplement apply?

    (iv) What effect did the Master Agreement have on the issue of authority?

    The effect

  337. According to the schedule to the Agreement the governing law of the Agreement is the laws in force in New South Wales. However, neither party submitted that the laws in force in New South Wales differed in any way from the laws in this State in respect to the issues raised in this case.

  338. It was common ground between the parties that once a transaction was concluded and binding on the parties that it incorporated the terms of the master agreement.

  339. This is made clear by s.1(c) of the Master Agreement which was substituted by the September 1992 Australian addendum No. 10 and which reads in its amended form -

    340. "All transactions are entered into in reliance on the fact that this Master Agreement and all Transactions (as evidenced by their Confirmations) form a single contract (collectively referred to as this 'Agreement') and the parties would not otherwise enter into any Transactions. The entering into of each Transaction takes effect as an amendment to this Agreement." (Emphasis added.)

  340. It is noted that the Master Agreement was the primary document between the parties and as each dealing was concluded it operated as an amendment to the Master Agreement.

  341. However, the parties disagreed as to the role of the Master Agreement in the negotiations leading to a transaction being concluded.

  342. In my opinion it was the common intention of the parties that the Master Agreement was to govern the negotiations between the parties leading to a transaction as well as the transaction itself. It is a charter with respect to the future dealings between the counter-parties.

  343. The Master Agreement is a stand alone agreement which fulfils a dual purpose.

  344. First, it governs the negotiations between the parties and provides for the moment when the contract is concluded and binding. Secondly, each transaction when concluded took effect as an amendment to the Master Agreement so that the latter's terms were incorporated into the transaction.

  345. It is a charter which guides the parties and determines issues between them during the course of their negotiations and provides for the effect of their negotiations.

  346. This is made clear by the purpose for executing the Master Agreement and secondly, by the terms of the Agreement.

  347. The parties from the very beginning of their relationship agreed in principle that their transactions should be governed by the Master Agreement.

  348. The opening words of the agreement provided -

    349. " ISDA MASTER AGREEMENT dated as of 21st November, 1996
    Pacific Power (for Eraring Power Setation and Hydro and Gas Turbines)
    POWERCOR Australia Limited

    ACN 064 651 109

    ........................................... and .......................................... have entered and/or anticipate entering into one or more transactions (each a 'Transaction') that are or will be governed by this Master Agreement, which includes the schedule (the 'Schedule') and the documents and other confirming evidence (each a 'Confirmation') exchanged between the parties confirming those Transactions."

  349. Section 9 is concerned with miscellaneous provisions.

  350. Section 9(e) provides -

    351. "9(e)(i) This agreement (and each amendment modification and waiver in respect of it) may be executed and delivered in counter-parts (including by facsimile transmission) each of which will be deemed an original. (ii) The parties intend that they are legally bound by the terms of each Transaction from the moment they agree to those terms (whether orally or otherwise). A Confirmation shall be entered into as soon as practicable and may be executed and delivered in counter-parts (including by facsimile transmission) or be created by an exchange of telexes or by an exchange of electronic messages on an electronic messaging system which in each case will be sufficient for all purposes to evidence a binding supplement to this Agreement. The parties will specify therein or through another effective means that any such counter-part, telex or electronic message constitutes a Confirmation." (Emphases added.)

  351. Section 9(e)(ii) makes it clear that the Master Agreement does govern the negotiations and provides when the parties are legally bound by the terms of a transaction. In my opinion the phrase "the terms of each Transaction" means the terms agreed for that transaction. What constitutes the terms of a particular transaction will depend on the circumstances and this may involve not only the essential terms to give effect to the commercial object of the transaction but other terms negotiated by the parties.

  352. Mr Downes QC did not dispute the proposition that the terms of the Master Agreement are to be considered on the question of intention to enter into a contract but disagreed with the proposition that the Master Agreement was to govern the negotiations.

  353. In my opinion it is clear that the parties entered into the Master Agreement to provide for the issues, inter alia, of when the parties reached a binding agreement and intended to be bound.

    354. When a binding contract?

  354. Section 9(e)(ii) makes clear the common intention of the parties that in respect of each transaction the parties are legally bound when they agree to the terms of the transaction. The transaction can be oral or otherwise. There is no requirement that the agreement is to be in any particular form.

  355. PP submitted that s.9(e)(ii) means that it is still necessary to prove as a fact a concluded and binding contract in law before the parties are bound.

  356. At common law in order to establish a contract it is necessary to show agreement of the terms, and an intention to be bound.

  357. It is necessary to prove agreement of the terms under clause 9(e)(ii) but once agreed intention is presumed. That is what the opening words provide.

  358. In my opinion s.9(e)(ii) forecloses the argument on intention once the parties have agreed to the terms "of each transaction". Once this occurs the parties have already agreed that they intend to be legally bound.

  359. Despite the clear language it would be open to the parties to vary this provision but the evidence would have to be certain and compelling to establish a variation. The plain words are that once there is agreement to the terms of the transaction, "the parties intend that they are legally bound".

  360. There is no suggestion the parties by express agreement varied that provision. But Mr Downes QC on behalf of PP submitted that the conduct of the parties demonstrated a variation leading to the conclusion that the parties did not intend to be bound having reached agreement to the terms. It will be necessary to consider the evidence hereafter.

  361. PP further submitted that no contract came into being until a confirmation document was executed and exchanged between the parties confirming the transaction.

  362. In my opinion the opening words of the Master Agreement and the provisions of s.9(e)(ii) make it clear that there is a difference between the contract constituting the transaction and confirmation which is evidence of the transaction.

  363. The word "confirmation" means the verification of a matter.

  364. Further, the wording of the Master Agreement draws a clear distinction between the Agreement and the confirmation.

  365. The confirmation letter in contract No. 1 which is a standard form recommended by industry makes it clear that it is to record the pre-existing binding transaction. This transaction was concluded whilst the parties were negotiating the terms of the Master Agreement and is part of the factual matrix objectively known to the parties.

  366. Dr Hudson op cit when discussing the ISDA Master Agreement wrote this at p.91 -

    367. "Each individual transaction is connected to this master agreement by entering into a confirmation which is, as its name suggests, a confirmation of the trade which two parties have entered into by telephone fax or telex on the dealing floors."

  367. He continued on the same page when he wrote -

    368. "The confirmation is, indeed, a written confirmation of the trade which two parties have already entered into by telephone, fax or telex."

  368. In my respectful opinion the learned author is saying no more than what appears in the words in the Master Agreement and I agree with his observations.

  369. The confirmation is a binding supplement to the Master Agreement.

  370. I should add the provisions of s.1(b) which provides -

    371. "In the event of any inconsistency between the provisions of the Schedule and other provisions of this Master Agreement the Schedule will prevail. In the event of any inconsistency between the provisions of any Confirmation and this Master Agreement (including the schedule), such Confirmation will prevail for the purpose of the relevant Transaction."

  371. By reason of the June 1997 Australian addendum No. 13, the definitions and provisions contained in the 1991 and 1993 ISDA definitions are incorporated into each confirmation.

  372. Going to the 1993 ISDA commodity derivatives definitions they define "confirmation" as being

    373. "with respect to a Transaction one or more documents or other confirming evidence exchanged between the parties which, taken together, confirm all of the terms of the transaction."

  373. The provisions also support my decision.

  374. Further, the said definitions defines "trade date" meaning -

    375. "In respect of a transaction, the date on which the parties enter into the transaction."

  375. This pre-dates the preparation of the confirmation which records the trade date.

  376. As at 10 October 1997 (the date of the Master Agreement variation) the parties had transacted some 25 transactions in which the trade date was specified at a date earlier than the confirmation document and each confirmation document had as its opening words the words which I have set out above which show that the confirmation was evidence of a pre-existing transaction.

  377. It was in those circumstances that the June 1997 Australian addendum No. 13 was applied to the Master Agreement by the agreed variation.

  378. In my opinion the common intention of the parties was that once all terms of a particular transaction were agreed the parties intended to be bound and were bound and the confirmation document was as the description plainly shows, evidence of a transaction which had already been concluded.

  379. The final binding nature of the agreement did not depend upon the exchange of a signed confirmation. However it was a term of their agreement that a confirmation document would be signed.

    380. Telephone Dealing Supplement

  380. The Telephone Dealing Supplement is a standard form contract issued by three financial institutions including the Australian Bankers Association.

  381. Clause 1 provides -

    382. "1. This supplement applies to transactions of the type specified in an appendix which are entered into by telephone after the parties have exchanged appendixes (sic) for that type of transaction. This supplement and any appendix prevails if there is an inconsistency between it and either:

    (a) the master agreement in which it is incorporated;

    (b) or any list of authorised officers issued under that Master Agreement."

  382. PP submits that the supplement only applies to a transaction which is wholly entered into over the telephone.

  383. PCA submits that it is not so confined and that as long as the deal is finally agreed over the telephone then it applies. Further, PCA submits that the document has general application because of the reference to authority of personnel to transact a dealing.

  384. The Supplement is relied upon by PCA because it specifies who has the authority to conclude a transaction. The authority of Mr Murphy provided in the document is greater than what PP submits was his authority to enter into the disputed transactions. Hence the issue between the parties over the Supplement's application.

  385. Clause 2 provides -

    386. "2. Each party must take all reasonable steps to ensure that:

    (a) only its authorised employees enter into transactions by telephone; and

    (b) transactions are initiated by telephoning the other party on one of the numbers or dedicated lines specified in Part B of the appendix."

    (Emphasis added.)

  386. Further, the clause goes on to provide -

    387. "The parties intend they are legally bound by the terms of each transaction from the moment they agree to those terms over the telephone."

  387. Each of the two appendices attached to the supplement is completed by one party.

  388. The appendices show that the supplement is to apply to "NSW and Vic electricity market transactions". The one completed by PCA sets out the authorised employees who are permitted to enter into transactions and those who can confirm transactions. Telephone numbers are indicated.

  389. In my opinion it was the common intention of the parties that the telephone dealings supplement should only apply to dealings done over the telephone.

  390. The wording of the telephone supplement makes that clear. Further, it covers the same matters as the ISDA Master Agreement and schedules and was intended to apply to particular transactions, namely, those done over the telephone.

  391. Mr Myers QC on behalf of PCA submitted that the terms of the supplement were relevant to the question of authority.

  392. I will consider that submission when I consider the question of authority.

    393. Authority

  393. Each party accepts that the parties had the necessary authority to enter into the 11 disputed contracts.

  394. The issue between the parties concerns the defence raised by PP that the officers in question, namely Mr Murphy and Mr Trethewy of PP and Mr Treacy of PCA were not authorised to enter into the 11 disputed transactions.

  395. The acceptance by both parties of their authority to enter into the types of transactions involved in the 11 disputed contracts avoids the problems that have plagued local authorities in England who entered into swap contracts for hedging purposes and in so doing exceeded their powers. See Hazell v Hammersmith and Fulham (1992) 2 AC 1.

  396. As PP has raised these defences it is appropriate to consider and determine the issues after the Court has considered the question of contract or no contract in respect to each disputed transaction.

  397. It will be necessary to come back to the Master Agreement which includes the Telephone Dealing Supplement to consider the issues raised.

    398. Estoppel by convention

  398. PP has pleaded that if the court comes to the conclusion that on the interpretation of the Master Agreement, an agreement is concluded and binding prior to the signing

    and exchange of the confirmation then the dealings of the parties established that it was their common intention at the date of each of the disputed contracts that no contract was concluded and binding until a confirmation document was executed and exchanged.

  399. PP submits that the dealings of the parties established a convention to that effect and relying upon the doctrine of estoppel by convention contends that PCA is estopped from denying that a contract came into being only after the signing and exchange of the confirmation.

  400. I have held that the Master Agreement between the parties makes it clear that the transaction was concluded and binding when the parties agreed to the terms and the confirmation document was evidence of the pre-existing agreement.

  401. PP submits that the conduct of the parties from 17 December 1996 up to 13 November 1997 established a convention that the contract did not bind until the signing of the confirmation and its exchange. The 13th November 1997 is the date of the first disputed contract.

  402. The plea of estoppel by convention is pleaded in paragraph 6E of PP's amended defence which reads -

    403. "Further as to paragraph 6 of the statement of claim, PP says, in the event that it is held in respect of any one or more of the alleged commodity transaction agreements that PCA and PP reached agreement on all the essential terms and intended to make a concluded bargain upon those alleged terms and that Bob Murphy had authority to bind PP in relation to that Commodity Transaction Agreement or those Commodity Transaction Agreements and insofar as that Commodity Transaction Agreement or those Commodity Transaction Agreements included one or more of those referred to in paragraphs 6(a), 6(b), 6(e), 6(f) and 6(j) of the Statement of Claim that Chris Treacy had authority to bind PCA in relation to that Commodity Transaction Agreement or those Commodity Transaction Agreements -

    (a) That each of PP and PCA assumed as the conventional basis of the Commodity Transaction Agreements governed by the Master Agreement as varied that each such Commodity Transaction Agreement would not be binding until such time as a letter confirming its terms had been signed by a person referred to in paragraphs 4(h)(i), (ii), (iii) or (iv) having authority for that Commodity Transaction Agreement on behalf of PP and by a person having authority to sign a letter confirming the terms of that Commodity Transaction Agreement on behalf of PCA and had been exchanged between PP and PCA."

  403. The reference to paragraph 4(h) is a reference to a previous paragraph in the amended defence which sets out the names of persons who were authorised.

  404. The pleading goes on to assert in particulars that PCA was informed by PP as to who could sign the confirmation documents prior to 18 November 1997 and that in respect of the 35 undisputed contracts the date when the first settlement of the amounts owing between the parties took place it was after the date of signature and exchange of each confirmation letter. It was contended that no settlement could take place until after the exchange of the signed confirmation document.

  405. The evidence did show that settlements did not take place until the confirmation letter had been executed and exchanged. The fact was that settlement could not take place until the load shape was prepared in accordance with the agreement in those transactions where the shape was not flat.

  406. The confirmation letter was evidence of the terms which had been agreed between the parties in respect of the transaction and the determination of moneys due and owing between the parties could only be made when the load profile had been prepared. This document set out at each relevant time period, the quantity of volume of notional electricity and the cost per megawatt hour. The load profile was prepared in accordance with the terms of the transaction which provided for the total volume and a formula to enable the load profile to be prepared.

  407. It follows that what was done was in accordance with the agreed terms of the particular agreement and pursuant to the Master Agreement.

  408. In other words, it was the expectation of the parties and their common intention that settlement would not take place until the load profile document was provided. This created no difficulties in relation to the undisputed contracts. Settlement often had to be calculated well after the due date. The parties accepted this course of conduct.

  409. In most instances the load profile document was prepared by PCA. However, PP on occasions prepared their own.

  410. The Master Agreement contemplated that there would be an attachment to the confirmation which set out the period in question, the calculation period which was 48 half hour periods in a day, the capacity in megawatts, the notional quantity in megawatts per hour and the fixed price in A$/MWh.

  411. Until that document was completed it was not possible to settle the transaction.

  412. This is made clear by the evidence of Mr Murphy who stated that since the commencement of trading on the Exempt Futures Market, most transactions were governed by the ISDA Master Agreement. He referred to the Australian guide to the Agreement contained in AFMA's 1997 guide to over the counter transactions which was published by the Australian Financial Markets Association. He referred to part 20 which he tendered in evidence and this sets out sample confirmation documents with attachments thereto.

  413. The guide was evidently prepared some time in the latter half of 1997.

  414. What the parties did in relation to the 35 undisputed agreements accorded with the practice set out in the guide industry practice and the Master Agreement.

  415. Nevertheless, PP contends the trading established a convention between the parties to the effect that no agreement was binding until the confirmation was signed and exchanged.

    416. Estoppel by convention - principles

  416. What constitutes estoppel by convention was stated by the High Court in Grundt v The Great Boulder Proprietary Gold Mines Ltd [1937] HCA 58; (1938) 59 CLR 641.

  417. In that case, according to a tribute agreement, a portion of a mine was to be worked by the tributers and this was defined. Under the agreement the ore won by the tributers was to be delivered to the mine owner for treatment and in return the latter was to supply air for the drilling and other equipment and to account to the tributers for portion of the profits of the sale of the treated ore.

  418. The tributers won ore from part of the mine outside the limits defined by the agreement. Even though the owner complained it still processed the ore, provided the air and paid the share of the proceeds to the tributers as if the ore was won within the boundaries of the tribute.

  419. A dispute arose between the parties and proceedings were instituted. The mine owner sued, inter alia, for recovery of the proceeds paid over in respect of the ore won outside the boundaries during the period when it was aware that the ore was being extracted outside the boundaries and when it processed the ore, supplied the air and accounted for the proceeds.

  420. The High Court held that the mine owner was estopped from recovering the proceeds which had been paid over after discovering the tributers were mining outside the boundaries.

  421. The issue came down to whether or not the mining company was precluded by estoppel from asserting that the tributers had committed a wrongful act?

  422. At p.674 Dixon J said -

    423. "But the estoppel set up by the tributers goes to the first step, the winning of the ore. The principle upon which estoppel in pais is founded is that the law should not permit an unjust departure by a party from an assumption of fact which he has caused another party to adopt or accept for the purpose of their legal relations. This is, of course, a very general statement. But it is the basis of the rules governing estoppel. Those rules work out the more precise grounds upon which the law holds the party disentitled to depart from an assumption in the assertion of rights against another. One condition appears always to be indispensable. That other must have so acted or abstained from acting upon the footing of the state of affairs assumed that he would suffer a detriment if the opposite party were afterwards allowed to set up rights against him inconsistent with the assumption."

    (Emphasis added.)

  423. His Honour went on to explain the nature of the detriment. He said at p.674 -

    424. "His complaint is that when afterwards the other party makes a different state of affairs the basis of an assertion of right against him then, if it is allowed, his own original change in position will operate as a detriment. His action or inaction must be such that, if the assumption upon which he proceeded were shown to be wrong and an inconsistent state of affairs were accepted as a foundation of the rights and duties of himself and the opposite party, the consequence would be to make his original act or failure to act a source of prejudice."

    (Emphasis added.)

  424. But the departure from an assumed state of fact and detriment is not enough to establish the estoppel. It is necessary to prove the part played by the party to be estopped and the lack of fairness or injustice which would result if the party was free to ignore the assumed facts.

  425. Dixon J said at p.675 after referring to the "justice of an estoppel" -

    426. "Before anyone can be estopped, he must have played such a part in the adoption of the assumption that it would be unfair or unjust if he were left free to ignore it."

  426. His Honour then noted a warning that the question was not left at large and referred to recognised grounds of preclusion he discussed in Thompson v Palmer [1933] HCA 61; (1933) 49 CLR 507 at 547. The focus is primarily on the conduct of the party to be estopped.

  427. His Honour emphasised the broad basis of the application of the principle when he said at p.676 -

    428. "It is important to notice that belief in the correctness of the facts or state of affairs assumed is not always necessary. Parties may adopt as the conventional basis of a transaction between them an assumption which they know to be contrary to the actual state of affairs."

  428. PP submits that despite the common intention of the parties found in the ISDA Master Agreement that a contract was concluded and binding once the terms of the transaction were agreed and that the confirmation document was evidence of the contract, the trading conduct of the parties thereafter established a conventional basis to the effect that the parties treated the signing and exchange of the confirmation document as the moment in time when the contract was concluded and bound the parties.

  429. It is contended that the conventional basis had the effect of varying the terms of the Master Agreement. There was no confirmation document signed in respect to any of the 11 disputed contracts although draft confirmation documents were prepared by PP at various times and it forwarded a number in respect to contracts numbered 33, 39, 40 and 41. PP relies on the pattern of trading between the parties in respect to the other 35 contracts which were the subject of signed and exchanged confirmations.

  430. It is well established that a court in construing a contract is not permitted to look at the subsequent conduct of the parties.

  431. In Whitworth Street Estates (Manchester) Ltd v James Miller and Partners Ltd (1970) AC 583, Lord Reid said at p.603 -

    432. "It is not legitimate to use as an aid in the construction of the contract anything which the parties said or did after it was made. Otherwise one might have the result that a contract meant one thing the day that it was signed, but by reason of subsequent events meant something different a month or a year later."

  432. Whilst that is the general rule, it has been recognised that the parties may by their conduct put a particular meaning on a contract or indeed, vary it. This is not a question of construction.

  433. This principle was taken up by Lord Denning MR in Amalgamated Investment and Property Co Ltd v Texas Commerce International Bank Ltd (1982) 1 QB 84 at p.120 when he said -

    434. "Although subsequent conduct cannot be used for the purpose of interpreting a contract retrospectively, yet it is often convincing evidence of a course of dealing after it. There are many cases to show that a course of dealing may give rise to legal obligations. It may be used to complete a contract which would otherwise be incomplete. It may be used so as to introduce terms and conditions into a contract which would not otherwise be there ...

    If it can be used to introduce terms which were not already there, it must also be available to add to, or vary, terms which are there already, or to interpret them. If parties to a contract by their course of dealing put a particular interpretation on the terms of it - on the faith of which each of them - to the knowledge of the other - acts and conducts their mutual affairs - they are bound by that interpretation just as much as if they had written it down as being a variation of the contract. There is no need to inquire whether their particular interpretation is correct or not - or whether they were mistaken or not - or whether they had in mind the original terms or not. Suffice it that they have, by their course of dealing, put their own interpretation on their contract, and cannot be allowed to go back on it."

  434. His Lordship referred to the judgments of Latham CJ and Dixon J in the Grundt's case and then said -

    435. "I care not whether this is put as an agreed variation of the contract or as a species of estoppel. They are bound by the 'conventional basis' on which they conducted their affairs. The reason is because it would be altogether unjust to allow either party to insist on the strict interpretation of the original terms of the contract - when it would be inequitable to do so having regard to dealings which have taken place between the parties."

  435. His Lordship then concluded at p.122 -

    436. "When the parties to a transaction proceed on the basis of an underlying assumption - either of fact or of law - whether due to misrepresentation or mistake makes no difference - on which they have conducted the dealings between them - neither of them will be allowed to go back on that assumption when it would be unfair or unjust to allow him to do so. If one of them does seek to go back on it, the courts will give the other such remedy as the equity of the case demands."

    (Emphasis added.)

  436. The other two judges quoted with approval the statement in Spencer Bower and Turner, Estoppel by Representation 3rd ed at p.157 -

    437. "This form of estoppel is founded, not on a representation of fact made by a representor and believed by a representee, but on an agreed statement of facts the truth of which has been assumed, by the convention of the parties, as the basis of a transaction into which they are about to enter. When the parties have acted in their transaction upon the agreed assumption that a given state of facts is to be accepted between them as true, then as regards that transaction each will be estopped as against the other from questioning the truth of the statement of facts so assumed."

  437. Whether the doctrine only covers an assumed statement of facts, or extends to an assumed state of law, or a mixed assumption of fact and law is the subject of some controversy.

  438. In Con-Stan Industries of Australia Pty Ltd v Norwich Winterthur Insurance (Australia) Ltd [1986] HCA 14; (1986) 160 CLR 226, the High Court of Australia appeared to confine the principle to an assumed state of fact.

  439. In that case in the joint judgment of Gibbs CJ, Mason, Wilson, Brennan and Dawson JJ their Honours said at p.244 -

    440. "Estoppel by convention is a form of estoppel founded not on a representation of fact made by a representor and acted on by a representee to his detriment, but on the conduct or relations between the parties on the basis of an agreed or assumed state of facts which both will be estopped from denying. The existence of an estoppel based on a convention between the parties has often been recognised."

  440. Their Honours referred to a number of cases including the Texas Commerce case.

  441. But their Honours then went on to say that the doctrine did not apply in the case before them for two reasons and their second reason was expressed as follows -

    442. "Secondly, just as estoppel by representation requires a representation of fact, so too estoppel by convention requires the assumed state of affairs to be an assumed state of fact. ... The state of affairs relied on by Con-Stan is that the parties conducted their business relationship on the basis that the broker was alone liable to the insurer for the premiums. That is clearly an assumption as to the legal effect of their conduct and not an assumption of fact."

  442. In Eslea Holdings Ltd v Butts (1986) 6 NSWLR 175 Samuels JA with whom Kirby P agreed stated that what the High Court said in respect to the second reason was obiter dictum and accordingly did not bind the New South Wales Court of Appeal.

  443. He also noted that the High Court quoted a number of cases in support of its earlier propositions and concluded -

    444. "It seems to me that the authorities to which the judgment in Con-Stan refers are not inconsistent with the proposition that a conventional estoppel might rest upon a foundation of assumed law as well as of assumed fact."

    See at p.188.

  444. His Honour referred at p.188 to what Kerr LJ in the English Court of Appeal said to the effect that the conventional basis could amount to "a binding contractual nexus between them which did not in fact exist" and then went on to say -

    445. "In other words they dealt upon the basis of an assumption as to the legal effect of their engagement. Granted the necessity of a conventional basis constituted by an existing state of affairs, and a requirement that there must be some statement or conduct by one party which induces some belief or expectation in the other, I can see no reason in principle or utility why that belief or expectation should not concern the legal rights of the parties."

  445. In Commonwealth v Verwayen (1990) 170 CLR 394 the High Court exhaustively considered the doctrine of estoppel.

  446. Many representations made and many assumed states involve questions of fact and law and an assumed state of legal rights are in many cases inextricably bound up with the facts.

  447. This led Mason CJ in Commonwealth v Verwayen at p.413 to state -

    448. " ... The distinction between assumptions as to fact and assumptions as to law is artificial and elusive ... So it would be productive only of confusion and arid technicality to restrict the operation of the doctrine so as to exclude from its scope an assumption as to a purely legal state of affairs. It is therefore not surprising that long ago the Judicial Committee recognised that a representation as to the legal effect of an agreement can give rise to an estoppel."

    See also the observations of Deane J in Foren v Whight [1989] HCA 51; (1989) 168 CLR 385 at 433-435.

  448. I respectfully agree. The important element is the assumed state of affairs which formed the conventional basis for the mutual dealings of the parties. To compartmentalise the assumed basis into fact or law defeats the purpose of the rule not to mention the effort and time spent in litigation seeking to establish the distinction.

  449. In the unreported decision of Riseda Nominees Pty Ltd v St Vincent's Hospital (Melbourne) Ltd, of the Court of Appeal, delivered 12 September 1997, Callaway JA speaking for the Court stated obiter at p.10 -

    450. "Secondly, in the August Leonhardt (1985) 2 LL.Rep. 28 at p.35, Kerr LJ delivering the judgment of a strong Court of Appeal, treated the leading English case Amalgamated Investment and Property Co Ltd v Texas Commercial International Bank Ltd (1982) QB 84 as an instance of parties having acted on 'their common assumption of a binding contractual nexus between them which did not in fact exist.' That is perhaps a narrowest view that may be taken of the Amalgamated Investment case, but it does suggest that the existence of a binding contract, or by parity of reasoning the validity of an assignment, may be an assumption of fact giving rise to a conventional estoppel. A 'fact' in this context includes the existence of at least some private legal rights. The law cannot be deformed by reference to considerations of commercial convenience, but the view that there cannot be a conventional estoppel with respect to the existence of a binding contract or the validity of an assignment is so plainly inconvenient as to cast doubt on whether it is indeed the law. Thirdly, the Con-Stan Industries case was not concerned with an assumption about either the existence of a contract or the validity of an assignment. A relevant assumption was that the broker alone, and not the insured, was liable to the insurer for the premiums. Their Honours did not say, at p.245, that that was an assumption as to the legal efficacy of the parties' conduct but as to its legal effect ie the result in law that it had produced."

  450. In my respectful opinion, parties to a contract who by their conduct establish a state of affairs which forms the conventional basis of their dealing and which may be factual or legal or a mixture of both may be estopped from denying the conventional basis by the application of the doctrine of conventional estoppel.

  451. Based upon the principles expounded by Dixon J in the Grundt case, in order to establish estoppel by convention, the party relying upon the doctrine has to establish -

    452. (i) that the parties have assumed a state of affairs whether it be factual, legal or both;

    (ii) that the assumed state of affairs is the basis for action or inaction in the dealings between the parties;

    (iii) that the departure by one party from the assumption would result in a detriment being suffered by the other party;

  452. Sir Owen Dixon went on to give examples of what constitutes unfairness or injustice.

  453. His Honour said at p.675 -

    454. "But the law does not leave such a question of fairness or justice at large. It defines with more or less completeness the kinds of participation in the making or acceptance of the assumption that will suffice to preclude the party if other requirements for estoppel are satisfied."

  454. He referred to what he said in Thompson v Palmer (1933) 49 CLR at p.547.

  455. One of the examples he gave was that the party may be required to abide by the assumption because it formed the conventional basis upon which the parties entered into contractual or other mutual relations such as bailment.

  456. I would add also because the assumption formed the conventional basis of the trading between parties which was contrary to their strict contractual rights.

    457. Estoppel - Conclusion

  457. PP contends that the parties had assumed in their dealings that a contract was not concluded and binding until the confirmation document was signed and exchanged.

  458. This is contrary to the terms of the Master Agreement as found by the Court. But it is said that PCA is estopped from denying that assumed state of affairs.

  459. It followed therefore, according to PP's argument that in respect of each disputed contract there was no contract until the confirmation document was signed and exchanged.

  460. PP submitted that the evidence to support the assumed state of affairs is found in the trading of the parties in respect to the 35 undisputed contracts.

  461. The first was agreed on 4 November 1996 (the trade date), the start date (effective date) was 1 January 1997 and the signed confirmation document was exchanged on 8 November 1996.

  462. The last undisputed contract No. 42 was agreed on the trade date of 27 May 1998, the effective date is 1 June 1998 and the confirmation was signed and exchanged on 29 May 1998.

  463. However, the pattern in respect of the majority of the other undisputed contracts is different. The transaction was concluded on the trade date, the contract period commenced and the signed confirmation was exchanged after commencement of the period.

  464. In respect of all the contracts save for contracts numbered 1, 17, 19, 24, 25, 27, 28, 30 and 42 the same pattern was followed, that is, agreement was reached on the trade date, the period commenced on the effective date and the confirmation was signed and exchanged subsequently to the trade date.

  465. The important piece of evidence relied upon by PP is the fact that in respect of all the contracts other than No. 21 no settlement of funds between the parties occurred until the confirmation document was signed and exchanged.

  466. It therefore followed that the parties had accepted that the contract was not concluded and binding until after the signed confirmation was exchanged.

  467. Contracts numbered 2-16 (inclusive), 18, 20, 21, 26, 29, 31, 32, 34, 36 and 37 all commenced before the signed confirmation was exchanged. This is powerful evidence against a finding of estoppel.

  468. By way of example, in contract No. 31, the trade date was 8 October 1997, the effective date was 1 January 1998, the contract was to run for five years, and the signed confirmation was not exchanged until 27 February 1998. Despite the fact that settlement was monthly, settlement did not take place until after 27 February 1998.

  469. In respect to contract 21 PP in fact paid out $880,000 in settlement of that contract prior to PCA receiving the signed confirmation letter. In fact, PCA had already signed its copy of the confirmation and sent it to PP but PP had not sent back its copy. The evidence showed that the parties got together and agreed that contract 15 was to be cancelled out by contract 20 and contract 21 was entered into as a replacement. There were already obligations under contract 15 and the payment was the difference between the two contracts.

  470. I agree with the submission of PP that these unusual circumstances are not inconsistent with the alleged conventional basis of dealing.

  471. PP submits that it is clear that since settlement never took place - save for the unusual circumstances concerning contract 21 - prior to the receipt of the signed confirmation that it followed that there was no binding and concluded contract in law between the parties. If the effect of the Master Agreement was to the contrary then the conventional basis of dealing estopped PCA from relying upon the terms of the Master Agreement.

  472. In my opinion the so-called conventional basis for dealing was in fact in accordance with the terms of the Master Agreement and the common intention of the parties.

  473. The step by step procedure contemplated by the Master Agreement was as follows -

    474. (i) Agreement is concluded when the parties agree to the terms of the transaction - see s.9(e)(ii).

    (ii) The confirmation of the agreement was to be entered into as soon as practicable and could be executed and delivered in counterparts - see ibid.

    (iii) The confirmation is to record the agreed terms of the transaction - see definition of confirmation in the 1991 and 1993 ISDA definitions which form part of the Master Agreement.

    Section 1.2 of the Definitions provided -

    "Confirmation means, with respect to a swap transaction, one or more documents exchanged between the parties which, taken together, confirm all of the terms of a swap transaction."

    (iv) The actual payment between the parties was specified in the confirmation - see s.2(a)(i) and payments were to be made on the date specified in the confirmation - see s.2(a)(ii) and netting, ie the process of setting off obligations under transactions one against the other was provided for in s.2(c) of the Master Agreement.

  474. It follows in my opinion that settlement was to take place once the confirmation document had been signed and exchanged. This procedure was in accordance with the common intention of the parties as evidenced in the Master Agreement.

  475. Further, it was contemplated by the parties that settlement would not take place until the load profile was prepared in accordance with the terms of the transaction. This was attached to the confirmation document in accordance with the Master Agreement. But more importantly the parties contemplated and adopted the procedure of settling after the confirmation was signed in respect to the undisputed contracts, more often after the contract was commenced.

  476. The latter fact is weighty evidence that the contract was concluded and binding once the terms were agreed.

  477. It follows that in my opinion that the alleged assumed state of affairs was in accordance with what the parties had agreed in accordance with the Master Agreement and each transaction.

  478. PP cannot prove that as a result of the state of affairs so established that it did something or omitted to do something which was in any way different to what it had contractually agreed to do. The parties did precisely what they had agreed to do.

  479. It follows that reliance on the terms of the Master Agreement by PCA that the agreement was concluded and binding prior to confirmation would not result in any detriment to PP and further, PCA would not be guilty of any unfair or unjust conduct in seeking to enforce what it was contractually entitled to do.

  480. In my opinion PP suffers no detriment in the estoppel sense by being required to comply with the terms of the Master Agreement. The so-called conventional basis of dealing was not contrary to what the parties had agreed.

  481. It follows that PP fails to establish its plea of estoppel based upon convention.

    482. Contract or no contract?

  482. PP contends that the parties had not reached a concluded and binding contract in respect of the 11 disputed contracts.

  483. Whether or not the parties have entered into a concluded and binding contract is a question of fact. PP submits that the facts show that the parties had not reached the point of a concluded and binding contract in respect to each of the disputed contracts.

  484. PP also submitted that it was the common intention of the parties that no contract would come into being until the exchange of the signed confirmation. I have already held that it was the common intention of the parties expressed in the Master Agreement that a contract could come into being and bind the parties prior to the exchange of the signed confirmation document. I have also found that PP has not established estoppel by convention to estop PCA from relying upon the terms of the Master Agreement in this respect.

  485. PP also submits that the parties did not intend to be bound when they had reached agreement on all the terms. It faces the formidable task of proving that the parties had varied the effect of the express provision of the Master Agreement concerning intention.

  486. The court now has to consider with respect to each of the 11 disputed contracts whether or not the parties had reached a final concluded and binding contract.

  487. PCA relies upon the terms of the Master Agreement. In my opinion it is entitled to do so.

  488. PP also submits that in determining whether or not a contract came into being it is also necessary to take into account the question which is much in dispute between the parties concerning the authorities of Mr Treacy on one hand and Mr Murphy and Trethewy on the other to enter into such agreements binding their respective employers.

  489. PP relies on the events between November 1997 and June 1998 to show that it was not the intention of PCA to be bound by any alleged agreements.

  490. PP has the heavy burden of establishing that the terms of the Master Agreement concerning intention to contract has been varied by the actions of the parties.

    491. A. Contract - Principles

  491. In determining whether parties have entered into a final concluded and binding contract in law it is necessary to consider and determine three questions. In Air Great Lakes Pty Ltd v KS Easter (Holdings) Pty Ltd (1985) 2 NSWLR 309 Mahoney, JA at p.326 said -

    492. "In considering this question, in a context such as the present, it is of assistance to distinguish between three questions: did the parties arrive at a consensus?; (if they did) was it such a consensus that was capable of forming a binding contract?; and (if it was) did the parties intend that the consensus at which they arrived should constitute a binding contract?; cf Barrier Wharfs Ltd v W. Scott Fell & Co Ltd [1908] HCA 88; (1908) 5 CLR 647 at 650.)"

  492. In regard to each of these questions his Honour said -

    493. "The first question looks to the existence of a common intention ... this question does not arise in the present case in the sense that, in respect of such terms as were agreed upon, the parties were ad idem." - at p.327 line A." "The second question looks to what the parties have agreed and to whether what they have agreed is capable of forming a binding contract. There are some forms of agreement which, because of (as it is conveniently described) uncertainty, are not capable of constituting such a contract." - at p.327 line B. "The third question looks to whether, the parties intention being congruent as to the terms of their agreement, they intend that agreement to be a binding contract in the sense of being legally enforceable as such. It is, of course, open to them to agree that it shall not." - at p.329 line F.

  493. On the question of whether or not the parties have agreed, it is necessary to consider whether the parties have reached an agreement i.e. were the parties consensus ad idem, had there been a meeting of the minds?

  494. In the present matter the court is concerned with bi-lateral contracts. The evidence showed negotiation, offer and counter-offer and plainly an intention on both sides to do "business", in other words an intention to bring into existence a contract binding in law.

  495. The first question is, did they reach an agreement on all necessary terms? In determining the general issue and this particular question, the law in this country is concerned with the external manifestation of the parties agreement and intention to enter into an agreement. That is, considering what they said, wrote and did or not do. It is an objective test.

  496. In the New South Wales Court of Appeal decision of ABC v XIVth Commonwealth Games Ltd (1988) 18 NSWLR 540 the court was concerned with the issue of whether a contract was concluded and binding in circumstances where there were protracted negotiations, a letter of intent, exchanges of draft agreements and discussions.

  497. Gleeson CJ with whom the other judges agreed referred to what the majority of the High Court said in Masters v Cameron [1954] HCA 72; (1954) 91 CLR 353 at p.362, namely -

    498. "The question depends upon the intention disclosed by the language the parties have employed, and no special form of words is essential to be used in order that there shall be no contract binding upon the parties before the execution of their agreement in its ultimate shape."

  498. His Honour then said -

    499. "Although there are qualifications to it, this general test of objectivity is of pervasive influence in the law of contract." - at p.549.

  499. His Honour stated that two passages from speeches of Lord Diplock illustrated the point. He referred to his Lordship's statement in Gissing v Gissing [1970] UKHL 3; (1971) AC 886 at 906 as follows -

    500. "As in so many branches of English Law in which legal rights and obligations depend upon the intentions of the parties to a transaction, the relevant intention of each party is the intention which was reasonably understood by the other party to be manifested by that party's words or conduct notwithstanding that he did not consciously formulate that intention in his own mind or even acted with some different intention which he did not communicate to the other party."

  500. His Honour also referred to what Lord Diplock said in Ashington Piggeries Ltd v Christopher Hill Ltd (1972) AC 441 at 502. See also Toyota Motor Corporation v Ken Morgan Motors [1994] VicRp 55; (1994) 2 VR 106 at 134.
