  501. Intention to contract is a necessary element in proving a binding contract. The issue was discussed in the New South Wales Court of Appeal in the Air Great Lakes case, supra. At p.337, McHugh JA said -

    502. "The intention to create a legally binding contract although a matter to be proved objectively, may, nevertheless, in my opinion, be proved by what the parties said and did as well as by what they wrote. The intention may be proved in that way even in a case where the document is intended to comprise all the terms of their bargain. This is because the intention to be bound is a jural act separate and distinct from the terms of their bargain."

  502. His Honour went on to say at p.338 -

    503. "In the current state of authority I think that it is open to this court to hold that the intention to create a legally binding contract may be proved by what the parties said and did as well as what they wrote, even where there is a signed document which is intended to comprise the terms of their bargain."

  503. Often it is a case of drawing inferences from what the parties said, did and wrote. In Allen v Corbone [1975] HCA 14; (1975) 132 CLR 528, Stephen, Mason and Murphy JJ at p.532 said -

    504. "Where parties reach an agreement which is expressed informally, whether in writing or orally, the terms of their bargain are not ordinarily recorded in meticulous detail in the words which they use. To ascertain their relevant intention it is often necessary to resort to inference, a process for which there is little or no scope when the parties have taken care to comprehensively record the terms of their agreement in written form."

  504. In my opinion where the parties objectively manifest agreement to all necessary terms to effect the transaction, this is weighty evidence in support of the conclusion that they intended to make a final and binding contract in law.

  505. Nevertheless there may be evidence to rebut the conclusion.

  506. In the present case the Master Agreement provided that the parties intended to be bound legally once the terms of the transaction had been agreed.

  507. As I have said, it would take compelling evidence to establish that despite what they had agreed to as regulating their future transactions, the parties nevertheless did not intend to be bound when they agreed to the terms of the transaction.

  508. In considering the issues the court looks at the relevant evidence not only of prior negotiations, and the words and actions of the parties but also relevant evidence post alleged contract date.

  509. This is well established by authority.

  510. In the ABC case, supra at p.547 Gleeson, CJ said -

    511. "There is ample authority for the proposition that reference may be made to the correspondence between the parties subsequent to 13 June 1986 for the purpose of showing that 'it was not in the contemplation of either party that they were to be bound until all the essential preliminaries had been agreed to, nor until a formal contract had been drawn up embodying all the matters incidental to a transaction of such a nature.: Barrier Wharfs Ltd v W. Scott Fell and Co Ltd [1908] HCA 88; (1908) 5 CLR 647 at 669."

  511. Later His Honour said at p.550-

    512. "Furthermore as was noted earlier, it is proper to have regard to communications between the parties subsequent to the date of the alleged contract to the extent to which those communications throw light upon the meaning of the language which is being considered for the purpose of determining whether it expresses an intention one way or the other upon the critical matter. At the least, such subsequent communications will often form part of the context to which the particular exchanges in question are to be evaluated."

  512. In addition, it is necessary to consider the purpose of the transaction and also its commercial context.

  513. In the ABC case, supra Gleeson, CJ said at p.548 -

    514. "In a case where a court is required to make a judgment concerning the intention of the parties in relation to what might be broadly described as a Masters v Cameron dispute, it will normally be of importance that the court have an understanding of the commercial context in which the dispute arises, and a most significant feature of that context will relate to the subject which the parties regard, or would ordinarily be expected to regard, as matters to be covered by their contract."

  514. Later, he summarised the approach at p.550 when he said -

    515. "The case involves the objective determination of the intention of the parties from a consideration of a series of communications exchanged by them in the context of their dealings over a period of time. In those circumstances it is both appropriate and necessary to have regard to the commercial circumstances surrounding the exchange of communications and in particular to the subject matter of those communications."

  515. In approaching the question of determining whether or not a contract binding in law was made between the parties, the court once satisfied that the parties intended to enter into legal relations should strive to uphold bargains made and not suffer the reproach stated by Lord Tomlin in Hillas and Co v Arcos Ltd [1932] UKHL 2; (1932) 38 Com. Cas. 23 at p.29, quoted above.

  516. Whilst His Lordship was talking about construction of a contract in my opinion his observations equally apply to the question of "contract or no contract".

  517. Gleeson, CJ in the ABC case, supra made the same point at p.548 when he said -

    518. "To say that parties to negotiations have agreed upon sufficient matters to produce the consequence that, perhaps by reference to implied terms or by resort to considerations of reasonableness, the court will treat their consensus as sufficiently comprehensive to be legally binding, is not the same thing as to say that a court will decide that they intended to make a concluded bargain. Nevertheless, in the ordinary case, as a matter of fact and common sense, other things being equal, the more numerous and significant the areas in respect of which the parties have failed to reach agreement, the slower a court will be to conclude that they had the requisite contractual intention."

  518. In the Toyota Motor Corporation case, supra at p.131 Brooking, J, after referring to the two varieties of uncertainty, namely, uncertainty of an agreed term and incompleteness went on to say -

    519. "Courts are reluctant to strike down an agreement on the ground of uncertainty or incompleteness."

  519. He later said -

    520. "If the court comes to the conclusion that parties intended to make a contract it will if possible give effect to their intention by overcoming difficulties said to result from uncertainty or incompleteness."

  520. Uncertainty or vagueness concerning the meaning of an agreed term or incompleteness in the sense that the parties apparently did not finally agree on a particular term leading the way to further negotiation may provide some evidence that the parties had not agreed or if they had, they did not intend to be bound.

  521. But in considering this question a number of principles should be kept in mind.

  522. First, even if it is impossible to assign a certain meaning to a provision this does not mean that the contract is void if it could be severed leaving the rest of the contract good. In other words a term of little importance.

  523. Secondly, any questions of uncertainty, vagueness or ambiguity may be resolved on a proper construction and if necessary by the implication of terms.

  524. The principles were summarised by Brooking J in Toyota Motor Corporation case, supra, at p.130-131. His Honour said -

    525. "Vagueness or incompleteness will bring a supposed contract down because there has been a failure to agree upon such terms as are in the circumstances legally necessary to constitute a contract: although the parties intended to make a contract, the law says that they failed to do so. But a suggested contract may fail for a different and more fundamental reason: that the parties did not intend that it should be a binding contract. The presence of vagueness or incompleteness, or of a provision which gives rise to doubt about the certainty or completeness of the suggested contract, may lead the court to conclude, or help it in concluding, that the necessary intention to make a binding contract was absent. It is well accepted that lack of clarity in the terms of an agreement and the leaving of questions to be settled by subsequent agreement may suggest that there is no intention to make an immediately binding contract." (Emphasis added.)

  525. His Honour went on to point out that the failure to reach agreement on matters not necessarily essential to the contract but nonetheless matters normally agreed to in a transaction of that class has the same tendency to lead to the conclusion of no contract or no intention to be bound. Further, large transactions usually command a degree of certainty and formality. The absence of either may lead to the conclusion of no contract.

  526. But as his Honour pointed out at p.132 "each case is different". He went on to say -

    527. "A relatively short letter may be held to record a binding agreement in a matter of great consequence and considerable complexity, in which numerous formal documents will have to be prepared and executed to give effect to the transaction."

  527. In this case the parties agreed through the Master Agreement to provide for future transactions. They appreciated the necessity of certainty, finality and expedition. They expressly provided as to their intention once the terms of the transaction were agreed.

  528. The parties in transacting each deal used their own shorthand and jargon to express their offers and acceptances. Much time was spent in this case by counsel for PP focussing on the words that had been agreed to by the parties and suggesting incompleteness, uncertainty and vagueness and therefore no contract. But the question is what did the parties intend by their discussions? One has to be on guard interpreting what they said in accordance with normal every day speech.

  529. The parties expressly provided in the Master Agreement in respect of intention to be bound and effect must be given to what they agreed to.

  530. It is important to bear in mind the difference between incompleteness and uncertainty. Incompleteness in respect to a term of a proposed contract tends towards a conclusion that the parties have not finally agreed although that as a general proposition is subject to exception. The term may be of such little importance that the court would find the agreement even though the term was vague or uncertain.

  531. Uncertainty of a term is used in two senses in the law of contract. First, as being some evidence that the parties have not finally agreed, in other words they are still in a state of negotiation, and secondly, as a matter of construction the term is so uncertain as to be incapable of operation.

  532. Whether or not a vague uncertain term shows that the parties had not finally agreed or is purely a question of construction depends upon the circumstances.

  533. But a court should be slow to conclude that the parties have not agreed because a term is vague or uncertain. Further, the court on a matter of construction will decide what the term means and once the court does that, there is no longer uncertainty.

  534. The point is graphically made by Barwick CJ in Upper Hunter County District Council v Australian Chilling and Freezing Co Ltd [1968] HCA 8; (1968) 118 CLR 429 at 436 when he said -

    535. "But a contract of which there can be more than one possible meaning or which when construed can produce in its application more than one result is not therefore void for uncertainty. As long as it is capable of a meaning, it will ultimately bear that meaning which the courts, or in an appropriate case, an arbitrator, decides is its proper construction; and the court or arbitrator will decide its application. The question becomes one of construction, of ascertaining the intention of the parties, and of applying it."

  535. Much time was spent in the present case by counsel for PP dissecting the words of a term agreed to by the parties and seeking to show that it was vague, uncertain and/or ambiguous in order to show the parties had not reached final agreement. Each alleged term will have to be considered, but in most instances it is a question of construction of an agreed term. A term expressed in language used by, and acceptable to the parties. The arguments provided little support for the conclusion that the parties were still negotiating.

  536. The principle of upholding contracts was stated by Professor Corbin in Corbin on Contracts, 1 Volume edition 1952 at p.144 as follows -

    537. "In considering expressions of agreement, the court must not hold the parties to some impossible, or ideal, or unusual standard. It must take language as it is and people as they are. All agreements have some degree of indefiniteness and some degree of uncertainty. In spite of its defects, language renders a practical service. In spite of ignorance as to the language they speak and write, with resulting error and misunderstanding, people must be held to the promises they

    make. The court must not be overly fearful of error; it must not

    be pedantic or meticulous in interpretation of expressions.

    Although courts have often said that they do not make contracts for the parties, it is nevertheless true that in the process of interpretation and construction both court and jury must make findings in doubtful cases and cannot avoid the occasional enforcement of a contract some of the terms and effects of which were not in the minds of the parties.

    If the parties have concluded a transaction in which it appears that they intend to make a contract, the court should not frustrate their intention if it is possible to reach a fair and just result, even though this requires a choice among conflicting meanings and the filling of some gaps that the parties have left. Their expressions should be interpreted ut res magis valeat. The application of such a rule as this is believed to come nearer to attaining the purpose of the contracting parties than any other, to give more business satisfaction and to make contract a workable instrument. As between merchants, their usage and customary modes of expression and of doing business must be taken into account; their expressions often leave conscious gaps to be filled in this manner." (Emphases added.)

  537. I respectfully agree. The learned author's observations are pertinent to the issue of formation of a binding contract. Often the issue involves interpreting what the parties meant in their dealings.

  538. Although evidence of the actual intention of a party to an alleged contract is not generally admissible, nevertheless it may be relevant and admissible to the overall issue of whether the parties had reached agreement and were legally bound. But in considering such evidence the court has to exercise considerable care. The topic was discussed by Gleeson, CJ in the ABC case, supra and at p.550 His Honour said -

    539. "The position is by no means so clear, however, in connection with internal memoranda, communications by one or other of the parties with some third party, or statements as to subjective intention made by individuals in the course of giving evidence."

  539. His Honour went on to refer to the substantial body of evidence given before the trial judge and opined the view that the evidence supported the conclusion that the parties themselves were in considerable confusion about the issues that ultimately emerged as determining their rights. His Honour went on to say it was not one of those exceptional cases where subjective intention is directly in issue such as mistake, misrepresentation, duress or undue influence. He then went on to say this -

    540. "Insofar as acts or statements of the kind referred to, not involving communications between the parties, are claimed to be relevant in a case such as the present upon the ground that they constitute an admission, it seems to me it will often be necessary to identify with some care the fact which is said to have been admitted. As was noted, there may be cases in which the issue is such that the fact of the subjective state of mind of one or other of the parties is relevant. Normally, however, what is in issue is not the subjective state of mind but their 'intention as expressed' and caution may need to be exercised in relating a fact which is said to be admitted to the fact which is legally relevant." (Emphasis added.)

  540. Other factors of some importance to determine whether or not there was a legally binding contract is the subject matter of the contract, what was involved and in particular the value of the contract? Contracts of complexity involving property of considerable value are normally not concluded without clear expression of intention or a formal contract. The fact that no formal contract was actually executed may be some evidence against a finding of contract. See Sinclair Scott & Co Ltd v Naughton [1929] HCA 34; (1929) 43 CLR 310.

  541. On the other hand, if a party embarks upon performance of the contract with knowledge by the other party or with active participation of the other party that is weighty evidence that the parties intended to enter into a binding concluded agreement.

  542. Whether or not any evidence of a subjective intention is admissible and in particular whether it may be an admission against interest of an intention to be or not to be bound, the court must be careful in using and weighing the evidence. The evidence may throw light on what the parties meant when they communicated with each other. But in the end such evidence would carry little weight if there was evidence which strongly led to a conclusion of contract or no contract as an objective fact. Nevertheless, there may be evidence amounting to an admission by a party that it did not intend to be bound.

  543. The issue facing the court was stated by the Privy Council in Harichand Mancharam v Govind Luxman Gokhale (1922) LR 50 Ind. App. 27 at p.30 -

    544. "Whether an agreement is a completed bargain or merely a provisional arrangement depends on the intention of the parties, as deducible from the language used by the parties on the occasion when the negotiations take concrete shape. As was observed by the Lord Chancellor in Ridgway v Wharton the fact of a subsequent agreement being prepared may be evidence that the previous negotiations did not amount to an agreement, but the mere fact that persons wish to have a formal agreement drawn up does not establish the proposition that they cannot be bound by a previous agreement."

    Quoted by Isaacs, J in The Sinclair Scott case, supra at p.235.

    See also Masters v Cameron (1991) CLR 353 at 360 et seq.

  544. Before considering the facts it is convenient to make two observations. First, the parties negotiated the disputed contracts in their own language and style and used language consistent with agreement in the background of negotiating and concluding many similar contracts.

  545. Secondly they provided in the Master Agreement for their intention to contract.

    546. B. Was there a contract?

  546. PP raised a number of issues on the question of whether in respect of each of the 11 disputed contracts there was a contract concluded and binding in law.

  547. The main contention put forward was that no agreement came into existence and bound the parties until there was an exchange of a signed written record of their agreement, that is, the confirmation.

  548. I have found to the contrary. An agreement came into being when all terms of the transaction were agreed between the parties.

  549. Mr Downes QC frankly conceded that if the Court came to that decision, PP's case on the issue whether the contract was concluded was not strong.

  550. PP contended that the evidence established that it was not the intention of the parties to be bound until (1) there was a written confirmation and (2) that in any event the evidence demonstrated objectively that the parties had not reached the point where they intended to be bound.

  551. I have ruled against PP's submission in relation to the confirmation and with respect to the question of intention to contract I have held that once there was an agreement to the terms of the transaction the parties had by the Master Agreement expressed their common intention that at that moment they intended to contract.

  552. Nevertheless, it is still open to PP to argue that on the evidence in respect of each transaction the parties had not agreed to the terms and did not intend to contract. PP relies upon the conduct of PCA in respect of each of the November transactions and in particular the delay between the alleged agreement and the steps taken to prepare and sign the confirmation as showing an intention not to contract. In the face of the express term PP carries a substantial burden.

  553. But as I have already held, the parties evinced their common intention in the Master Agreement and there would have to be clear, unequivocal evidence in respect to a particular transaction to defeat the common intention already expressed.

  554. PP submits that in respect of each of the 11 disputed contracts the parties did not reach a consensus on all terms, some terms were inconclusive or uncertain and hence the parties were still in a state of negotiation and that the parties did not intend to reach a concluded contract.

  555. Mr Downes QC submits that the issues in relation to the authority of the employees of the parties bears upon the question of concluded and binding contract.

  556. I reject that submission. It is a separate issue altogether. Either the parties had the necessary authority or were estopped from denying it, or they did not.

  557. One of the purposes of the Master Agreement was to provide for the moment in time when a contract was concluded and binding.

  558. According to s.9(e)(ii) the parties were "legally bound by the terms of each transaction from the moment they agreed to those terms".

  559. The parties in executing the Master Agreement agreed that in respect of any transaction negotiated thereafter, the agreement was concluded and binding from the moment when they agreed to the terms. Once reached, the agreement amended the Master Agreement.

  560. The issue then comes down to a question of determining the terms of each transaction and whether the parties had agreed to them. This is a question of fact. The court looks at all circumstances pre and post alleged contract date in determining the question.

  561. The provisions in the Master Agreement to that extent add nothing to the common law in the question of agreement or no agreement.

  562. However, the opening words of s.9(e)(ii) are important. They are concerned with the question of intention.

  563. Section 9(e)(ii) in its opening words states -

    564. "(ii) The parties intend that they are legally bound by the terms of each Transaction from the moment they agree to those terms (whether orally or otherwise ... " (Emphasis added.)

  564. As I have found in respect of each transaction once the terms of each transaction are agreed the parties have agreed that they intend to be bound.

  565. This construction gives effect to the purpose of the Master Agreement and the commercial purpose and common sense of the transaction.

  566. The need for certainty in a transaction concerning a derivative contract is obvious. The spot prices of electricity in the pools in New South Wales and Victoria respectively are fixed every half hour, are subject to change, are volatile and on occasions can rise and fall by substantial sums. It is of the utmost importance to those transacting the business to know precisely the time when the contract is made. The amounts of money involved in any derivative contract involving the notional supply of electricity can be enormous. A substantial movement in the spot market can result in the transfer of very large sums of money between the parties.

  567. That is not to say that the parties could not in respect of a particular transaction override what they agreed to in the Master Agreement by providing that they did not intend to be bound until a certain event occurred but as I have said it would take cogent and clear evidence to establish that the common intention stated in the Master Agreement had been displaced by the parties.

  568. What are the "terms of each transaction" will depend upon the particular circumstances.

  569. Clearly in respect to each transaction there are minimum terms which must be agreed to give effect to the commercial purpose of the transaction. But in addition to the minimum essential terms the parties may also introduce additional terms and accordingly it will be necessary to consider the circumstances to determine whether those additional terms have been agreed or abandoned by the parties. In the course of negotiations terms may be floated, discussed, deferred for further consideration and may even be abandoned expressly or by implication. It will be necessary to closely scrutinise the evidence in respect to each transaction because PP submits that the evidence shows that certain terms were the subject of negotiation and were never agreed prior to the moment in time when PP unequivocally indicated to PCA that it was no longer interested in transacting the 11 disputed contracts. It followed according to the argument that not all terms were agreed. But in considering the evidence it must not be overlooked that a term discussed in negotiations may be abandoned especially where it is to benefit PCA.

    570. C. Essential minimum terms

  570. The first disputed contracted was originally No. 22 and became contract No. PP/PC0033. It was negotiated from about March 1997 and PCA alleges that it was concluded and binding on or about 13 November 1997 and varied in March 1998.

  571. By 13 November 1997 the parties had negotiated, concluded and exchanged signed confirmations in respect to 26 contracts, the majority of which were two-way swap contracts.

  572. The course of their trading and the commercial purpose of the transactions established the minimum essential terms for a two-way swap transaction.

  573. Of the 11 disputed contracts, nine are two-way swap contracts and two are collar contracts.

  574. The two-way swap contracts required agreement to the following essential terms -

    575. (i) The period of contract - from the effective date to date of termination;

    (ii) The period when the spot price was to be determined; invariably every 30 minutes for every day of the period of the contract;

    (iii) The settlement date for payment - invariably the twentieth business day after the end of the billing period;

    (iv) Identification of a fixed price payer - that is, party B - PCA;

    (v) Identity of the floating price payer - that is, party A - PP;

    (vi) The fixed price to be paid for a particular period which is expressed to be peak or off-peak and in addition in NSW, shoulder;

    (vii) Determination of the floating price which was specified as a unit of volume, namely MWh, reference to the particular electricity pool, for example, Victorian Power Exchange or New South Wales pool and to be calculated by reference to the applicable State code.

  575. By November 1997 certain terms were agreed in every transaction, namely, period of calculation, settlement date, and identification of parties A and B. Further, peak hours and off-peak hours were known to the parties depending on which State. The parties could describe a contract covering all essential terms, such as No. 24 as follows -

    576. "Contract 24 - VPX 50 MWh peak at $24 for the period from 1 November 1997 to 30 June 1998."

  576. The reference to VPX is a reference to the Victorian Power Exchange. This brief description encapsulated what was agreed and their course of trading incorporated all other essential terms.

  577. By 13 November 1997, the parties had adopted the practice of attaching to the confirmation a summary setting out for each half hour the volume of energy and the fixed price. This summary was in accordance with the agreed essential terms. The attachment facilitated settlement. The attachment was to accord with what was agreed as to the load profile or shape.

  578. Prior to 13 November 1997 only one collar contract had been agreed and the confirmation was signed shortly prior to 9 November 1997. This was contract No. 26.

  579. The first of the two disputed contracts which is a collar is No. 38 which PCA alleges was agreed on 28 November 1997.

  580. The essential terms of a collar are the same as the two-way swap. By way of example the confirmation document for contract No. 26 sets out the minimum essential terms and in that contract it is spelt out how the settlement was to take place. This depended upon whether the spot price was higher or lower than $16.15 and $13.85 respectively and if it was not then there was no flow of money between the parties.

  581. It is now necessary to consider each disputed contract to determine whether the parties agreed to the terms of the transaction and whether they intended to be bound.

    582. (I) Contract No. 33 (formerly 22)

  582. This alleged contract was formally allotted number 22 but subsequently was allotted contract No. 33. It was known as the Visy/Air Liquide contract because the terms of the derivative contract were negotiated by PCA as a hedge to proposed substantial retail contracts for the supply of electricity to the two very large trading organisations known as Visy Board and Air Liquide respectively.

  583. PP was aware that PCA was negotiating substantial retail contracts with the two organisations in question but they were not apprised of the negotiations or the terms of any proposed retail contracts.

  584. PCA some time prior to March 1997 commenced negotiations with Visy Board to supply the entity with a large volume of electricity to commence on 1 July 1998 for a long term.

  585. The retail staff informed Mr Treacy of the circumstances and Mr Treacy opened negotiations with PP in March 1997 aware that Visy wanted a Meet the Market clause ("MTM") in its retail contract, it did not want a force majeure clause and the contract for the supply of electricity was to be over a long term, namely, in the vicinity of ten years. At the outset PCA wished to hedge in respect of the peak supply of electricity to Visy and proposed to source the hedge against off-peak supply elsewhere.

  586. A MTM clause was a clause that a consumer of electricity wanted in the retail contract which enabled the consumer to require a review of the price periodically, usually annually, and agreement to a revised price or termination of the retail contract.

  587. If a retail customer had such a clause it was necessary for PCA in hedging the risk to have a similar type clause with the other party to the hedge transaction. In general terms the clause enabled a review of the fixed price periodically, usually annually, and if there was an expected movement in the wholesale price, the fixed price could be adjusted and if it was, PCA was given the option to continue with the contract or terminate it.

  588. The negotiations between PCA and PP commenced at a meeting on 5 March 1997 involving Messrs Petch and Trethewy of PP and Dr Nelson and Mr Treacy on behalf of PCA. The early discussions concerned a price of $24/MWh using an escalation of CPI minus one for future years and at that stage PP made it clear that there would be no MTM clause, no rebate pricing option which was applicable in relation to other hedge contracts, and that the deal proposed was only for Visy.

  589. Thereafter Messrs Treacy and Murphy negotiated the terms of the proposed transaction at meetings, facsimiles were sent and discussions took place concerning the terms and including the insertion of a MTM clause.

  590. This was not the first time there had been discussions between the parties concerning a MTM clause.

  591. In earlier discussions PP and PCA had discussed a form of MTM clause and in respect of contract No. 2 had agreed to what was described as "Customer Out-Clause" which was drafted by PP and was in the following terms -

    592. "For a customer who has a contract for greater than one year, he has the option that, providing giving a one month's notice prior to the anniversary date to PCA, he can go back into the market for new prices."

  592. Taken out of context and without the knowledge of the negotiating parties, this clause does not make much sense. However, in context and taking into account the factual circumstances the parties did intend that if the retail customer could go back into the market for new prices then the parties to the hedge contract were entitled to do likewise in respect of their transaction.

  593. On 19 May 1997 Mr Treacy sent a facsimile to Mr Murphy which summarised offers made by Mr Murphy and which were open to 31 May 1997.

  594. By 30 May 1997 Mr Treacy had raised the question of the terminology of the MTM clause and also raised the query of taking up the balance of power that had been offered by Mr Murphy.

  595. On 2 June 1997 Mr Murphy sent a facsimile to PCA stating that the contents of Mr Treacy's facsimile of 30 May 1997 "are acceptable in principle" and went on to state that he needed "to see the actual profiles before agreeing".

  596. On 1 July 1997 Mr Treacy sent a copy of the Visy load profile.

  597. On 4 June 1997 Mr Treacy sent a draft of a MTM clause for discussion.

  598. The load shape sent on 1 July 1997 satisfied the term discussed in the course of negotiations that the load was to be not less than 38% of the annual load.

  599. By 28 July 1997 PCA had formed the opinion that the parties had reached agreement and Mr Treacy requested Mr Trethewy to inform him as to the status of the contract.

  600. Clearly, Mr Trethewy on behalf of PP must have thought that the contract had been finalised because on that day he sent a draft confirmation of what was then contract No. 22 to PCA.

  601. It is pertinent to note that the confirmation is dated 1 July 1997 and the Trade Date is noted as the same date.

  602. I interpolate here to draw attention to the definition of "Trade Date" which for the purposes of the Master Agreement meant -

    603. "In respect of a swap transaction the date on which the parties enter into the swap transaction."

  603. Despite the apparent agreement, Mr Treacy in a facsimile raised questions concerning the subject of automatic triggering, CPI index escalation price formula and the GST tax effect on future prices.

  604. A discussion took place on 26 August 1997 between Mr Murphy and Mr Treacy and by facsimile of the same date Mr Treacy wrote that the parties had agreed that the MTM clause would be linked to the retail customer with the hedge so that if the MTM clause was triggered by the retail customer then it would flow through to the hedge contract. He said that he would organise some words to "wrap around this for the contracts in question".

  605. He also noted that PP was going to check on the Bureau of Statistics formula to incorporate the adjustment to any CPI escalation by the effect of a GST.

  606. Further discussion ensued. On 11 September 1997 PP forwarded a signed confirmation of contract No. 22 to PCA.

  607. It was noted at that stage by PCA that a number of matters in the confirmation were not in accordance with what was agreed.

  608. Both parties managed to lose the original contract No. 22 which was signed by PP. It had attached to it the load profile and it is clear from the evidence that Messrs Murphy and Trethewy had initialled the load profile as being acceptable.

  609. At a meeting held on 23 October 1997 the Visy contract confirmation was discussed and it was agreed that PCA had to prepare the wording for the MTM and CPI escalation clauses.

  610. On 10 November 1997 Mr Treacy sent a copy of a summary of the revisions to contract No. 22 with changes highlighted.

  611. Attached was a revised MTM clause.

  612. On 12 November 1997 Mr Treacy sent a facsimile detailing the issues which were to be discussed at a meeting to be held on 13 November 1997.

  613. One of the items was -

    614. "Visy deal - for discussion on 13.11.97 (details already sent)."

  614. The reference to details was to the facsimile of 10 November 1997. Mr Murphy gave evidence that he had a conversation with Mr Treacy on 10 or 12 November 1997 in which Mr Treacy stated that the draft MTM clause needed further revision. Mr Treacy denied such conversation.

  615. Mr Murphy's version was based on his memory. He has no note of it, he is unsure when the conversation took place and his version in cross-examination was hardly convincing. Also the alleged admission by Mr Treacy is hardly consistent with what occurred at the meeting which occurred on 13 November 1997. I reject his evidence.

  616. On 13 November 1997 a meeting took place in Melbourne.

  617. During the meeting in which PCA was represented by Messrs Daley and Treacy and PP by Messrs Murphy and Lindsay Oates, the Visy contract was discussed.

  618. What was said is disputed.

  619. I accept the version of Messrs Daley and Treacy to Mr Murphy's version for a number of reasons.

  620. First, he had no notes of the meeting, and was relying on memory and documents produced by PCA; secondly, he was uncertain whether the Visy details set out in Mr Treacy's facsimile of 7 November 1997 were discussed but he was prepared to state in oral evidence categorically that the question of monthly flex applying to the Visy contract was not discussed. In his witness statement he used the phrase "I deny that there was any discussion at this meeting" concerning flex relating to Visy. This is ambiguous.

  621. His evidence lacked credibility and was vague in respect to most details except those he thought were important to PP's case.

  622. Finally, Mr Treacy, on 21 November 1997, sent a facsimile which contained a summary of the "Outcome of Discussions" which recorded -

    623. "*BM confirmed the Visy details as faxed to PP as acceptable. BM was still awaiting final confirmation on the wording of the MtM clause from PP's solicitors."

  623. Mr Oats did not give evidence because of ill-health.

  624. I accept the version of Mr Treacy as he has notes, an agenda, a note to prompt him and his summary of the outcome. Further, his memory of the meeting was good.

  625. His evidence was to the effect that he put to Mr Murphy at the meeting whether the details that had been sent on the Visy contract were acceptable and Mr Murphy advised "yes", that everything was fine in regard to his facts, however, he wished to have his solicitors check on the Meet the Market clause.

  626. According to Mr Treacy, Mr Murphy did come back later that month saying that the Meet the Market clause was acceptable. Mr Murphy denied this.

  627. I accept the evidence of Mr Treacy. It is supported by the fact that the Meet the Market clause in the same terms was in contracts 36 and 37 which were confirmed and performed from 1 January 1998. No query was raised by Mr Murphy concerning the wording of the MTM clause. I accept the evidence of Mr Treacy that Mr Murphy also said at the meeting that the flex arrangement was acceptable to PP.

  628. Subsequently to that meeting concluding Mr Treacy made out a trade ticket in respect to the transaction. This was part of the internal procedure within PCA to record the details of a transaction which had been agreed.

  629. That piece of evidence in itself does not prove that the terms of the transaction had been agreed but it does throw light upon the evidence given by Mr Treacy with respect to this meeting held on 13 November 1997 and supports his version. As far as he was concerned the parties had an agreement on 13 November 1997.

  630. The later correspondence passing between the parties also confirms that the parties had agreed on the terms.

  631. On 2 December 1997 Mr Treacy sent to Mr Murphy a facsimile of proposed agenda items for a visit on 4 December 1997. Under the heading "contract status" the sixth dot point refers to - "Visy/Air Liquide".

  632. The meeting did take place on 4 December 1997 and it is common ground that Visy was noted but nothing was discussed. This supports the view that the parties were satisfied with what had been agreed.

  633. On 8 December 1997 Mr Treacy sent another facsimile to Mr Murphy in which reference was made to the meeting on 4 December and it was noted that certain items had to be actioned.

  634. Under the heading "* outstanding contracts and their priorities are as follows: ...

    * Visy/Air Liquide starts 1.7.98."

  635. There was a reference to all told seven contracts. What was required was the exchange of signed confirmations.

  636. On 23 December 1997 Mr Treacy sent a facsimile to Mr Murphy listing the outstanding contracts and noting the key ones which had to be actioned as they started on 1 January 1998. He noted that there would be a difficulty in signing the contracts and stated "when most of the key people finish tomorrow and are not due back until January 5 1999.

    Can you advise the timing of these key contracts so that I can ensure we have the relevant authorised personnel to sign them."

  637. Attached was a list of outstanding contracts and reference is made to the Visy/Air Liquide contract which has a date for the deal being done of 13 November 1997, a start date of 1 July 1998 and determination date of 8 March 2007. In the comments column it was noted - "PP to raise contract."

  638. PP did not at any time respond to any of the correspondence either in writing or verbally to suggest that the deal had not been done. All the parties were waiting for was the preparation, signature and exchange of the confirmation document.

  639. PP's attitude to the deal is exemplified by their account manager, Mr Trethewy, sending a facsimile to Mrs Sim on 13 January 1998 which relevantly reads in respect to the Visy contract as follows -

    640. "Lena, CONTRACT NUMBERS The proposed new contract numbers and details are as follows:-
    PPN
    PC No.
    Contract Details
    Data
    Flex
    MtM
    Hol Rest
    33
    40-13-N
    Visy/Air, 1 Jul98 to 8 Mar 07
    QTEN1

    Y
    Y
    ...

  640. The reference to "QTEN1" is a reference to the load profile which had been sent.

  641. The facsimile concluded by requesting Mrs Sim "to advise of any mistakes in the details". In fact there was an error. The details omitted reference to the flex term. This facsimile emanating from PP is weighty evidence of a contract.

  642. On 4 February 1998 or thereabouts Mrs Sim sent off a document headed "Outstanding Contracts as at 4 February 1998" and it is in similar form to the table that was sent by Mr Treacy in late December and again it is noted that PP to raise contract.

  643. On 28 May 1998 Mrs Sim forwarded a facsimile to Mr Trethewy in which she indicated that PCA was still waiting for PP to raise a contract in respect to the Visy agreement.

  644. PP did not respond in any way to any of the documents sent by PCA suggesting that the deal had not been done.

  645. Mr Murphy in his evidence-in-chief said that he had thought about the Visy commodity transaction from time to time during early 1998 and said - "I regarded it as being incomplete and requiring further negotiation. As the initiative lay with Powercor to provide a revised Meet the Market clause I did not raise the matter with Mr Treacy. At some point which I cannot now recall I forgot about this proposed commodity transaction agreement until it was raised by Mr Treacy on 11 June 1998."

  646. I do not accept his evidence that he regarded the transaction as being incomplete and requiring further negotiation. His evidence is an afterthought and is false.

  647. In my opinion the parties had agreed to the transaction by 13 November 1997. The terms of the MTM clause were to be checked by PP's solicitors. The later events showed that PP accepted the terms.

  648. In his opening Mr Myers QC stated that this contract was varied by agreement between the parties in March 1998.

  649. Mr Treacy gave evidence concerning the variation and even though the pleading and particulars does not refer to it I am satisfied that PP were aware at a very early stage in the trial that that was PCA's case. Mr Downes QC did not submit to the contrary.

  650. The variation related to the application of annual flex and this was agreed at a meeting on 4 March 1998 between Messrs Treacy and Murphy. The procedure for implementing all flex mechanisms was agreed to for all contracts on 19 March 1998. Another copy of the flex rules and procedures which were discussed at the meeting were sent by PCA to PP on 23 March 1998 and on 26 March 1998 PP confirmed its agreement to the rules and procedures by a facsimile.

  651. The variation in March 1998 concerned the monthly flex arrangements and how it was to operate.

  652. Going back in time, PCA sought a term with respect to flex being included in the contract on 10 November 1997 and Mr Murphy agreed to flex applying to the transaction on or about 13 November 1997.

  653. What he agreed to was "plus or minus 10% monthly flex" could be incorporated in the transaction. The question was the appropriate wording for the flex term and Mr Trethewy drafted a suitable term some time prior to 27 February 1998 after consultation with Mr Murphy. The term that was drafted was included in the confirmation documents in contract Nos. 31, 34, 36 and 37 which confirmations were signed and exchanged by the parties by the beginning of March 1998.

  654. On 4 March 1998 at a meeting between Messrs Treacy and Murphy there was further discussion about the flex arrangements and because of a problem raised by PP, Mr Treacy agreed that the percentage change would be across all periods during a month. Mr Treacy sought something in return and he and Mr Murphy agreed that there would be an annual load shape review. The parties agreed that PCA would draft appropriate clauses to give effect to the agreement.

  655. PCA put forward a proposal and at a meeting held on 19 March 1998 Mr Murphy agreed to the terms set out on a document which was handed over. The copy of the flex rules and procedures was sent by PCA to PP on 23 March 1998 and PP confirmed its agreement in writing on 26 March 1998. The flex rules and procedure were summarised in the agreed rules as follows -

    656. "On a monthly basis PCA to advise PP the flex variations on the applicable contracts. Quantity nominated is to be in the range of plus or minus 10% for all contracts except the Alcoa contract (plus or minus 25%). The flex variation nominated is to be applied uniformly for each of the calculation periods. Notice of the flex will be done within five business days prior to the start of the relevant month except Alcoa (10 business days). PP to advise PCA of any problems with the flex details as supplied to them within five business days of the notification of the flex on the contracts."

  656. The ingredients of the flex term was raised and agreed in November 1997 and what occurred thereafter was the drafting and acceptance of a term to give effect to their agreement. In my opinion agreement had been reached in 1997 with respect to the flex arrangement and the events of March 1998 resulted in a variation to what had been agreed. The two items of variation were, the flex nomination was to be applied uniformly for each of the calculation periods and secondly, PP was to advise of any problems with the flex details within five business days of the notification of the variation.

    657. PP's submissions

  657. PP submitted that the parties had not reached a consensus, that some of the terms were incomplete and uncertain and that the parties did not intend to be bound.

  658. The latter submission is subject to what I have already decided in relation to the Master Agreement and its effect upon the issue of contract and confirmation and intention to be bound. In addition to the questions of incompleteness and uncertainty, PP also relies upon what has been described as the "holding back" argument.

  659. I will consider later the holding back argument in relation to all the alleged contracts agreed prior to the end of 1997.

  660. The submissions concerning lack of consensus, incompleteness and uncertainty overlap and focus on a number of issues including uncertainty as to meaning and alleged evidence of further negotiation.

  661. In considering these submissions it cannot be overlooked that the parties through Messrs Treacy and Murphy negotiated the terms on a term by term basis over a period of time, used language and jargon which they understood in the context of their overall negotiations and at times expressed their agreement in what appears to be uncertain and incomplete language.

  662. Neither man is a lawyer but they dealt with each other bona fide and eager and keen to do business on behalf of their respective employers. Each was concerned to get the best deal for his employer.

  663. As I have already stated and I reiterate, the court should not be the destroyer of men's bargains and in construing language they used, to give effect to their agreement, the court must make allowance for the fact that commercial men sometimes use loose imprecise language to express their agreement.

  664. In considering the submissions put it is necessary for the court to strive to give effect to the common intention of the parties as expressed in the language they used and understood.

  665. PP submitted that Mr Murphy did not agree to the Meet the Market clause because he indicated that he wished to refer it to PP's solicitors. This was in November 1997.

  666. In my opinion PP did agree to the terms of the Meet the Market clause because first, he informed Mr Treacy some time in late November 1997 that there was agreement. Secondly, the conduct of PP in relation to accepting the same Meet the Market clauses in other contracts from mid-November 1997 onwards amply demonstrates that PP was agreeable to the proposed Meet the Market clause put forward by Mr Treacy in November. I accept that the Meet the Market clause was different to that used in contract 34, but contract 34 was always treated as having a different clause.

  667. An argument was put that there is uncertainty as to what the clause means. It is suggested that there are a number of possible meanings.

  668. In my opinion that is a question of construction of the contract. The parties agreed to the term and the argument put that there is uncertainty does not establish the parties were still negotiating. In my opinion the evidence shows they were not. They had agreed to the wording of the clause. Its meaning and application is a matter of construction of the contract.

  669. In addition, PP submits that there was no agreement with respect to the reference node, volume of electricity, flex arrangements, load shapes or the March variation. It further submits that the Meet the Market clause was incomplete and uncertain, the volume of electricity was incomplete, the flex arrangements were incomplete and uncertain. It was submitted that there was no agreement to the March variation because the terms agreed were incomplete and uncertain.

  670. The argument in respect to incompleteness is, that the wording of the agreed terms show that the parties had not reached agreement.

  671. I have considered these submissions and in my opinion none of the submissions have any foundation. The parties had reached agreement. What each term means is a matter of interpretation of the term which if there is any dispute during performance can be resolved by the court construing the term.

  672. There are two pieces of evidence which bear on the question of alleged lack of consensus, incompleteness and uncertainty and they are, first the internal administration procedure of PCA treating a trade ticket as evidence of a transaction agreed and the evidence which emerged late in the trial because of the failure of PP to discover the documents, namely, draft confirmation letters created by Mr Murphy on 18, 19 and 22 June 1998 in respect to the disputed contracts. I accept the context in which these documents were prepared was subsequent to the dispute arising on 10 June 1998 but nevertheless they do indicate the views of Mr Murphy as to what had been the subject of discussion and agreement between the parties. It is pertinent to observe the pricing, the reference to the Victorian Power Exchange, the price escalator clause and the Meet the Market clause were all included in the draft and apparently caused no difficulty in drafting. The document was drafted by PP.

  673. These documents were not exchanged. I accept that the draft confirmation for contract No. 33 does not set out all the terms relied upon by PCA. Nevertheless, it is a piece of evidence which tends to support the conclusion that terms such as price, reference node, Meet the Market clause, volume of electricity and load shape were not in dispute and were not intended to be the subject of further negotiation.

  674. In my opinion the parties did agree to all the terms of the transaction concerning contract No. 33 and subject to the question of intention to be bound, based on the holding back argument and the questions of authority, the parties agreed to the terms of the transaction. In accordance with the Master Agreement the parties intended to be bound once they had agreed and it follows were bound. The latter conclusion is subject to the "holding back" submission but there is no other evidence which in my opinion establishes that the parties intended the Master Agreement did not apply to the issue.

  675. Another piece of evidence which cogently reinforces my decision is a telephone conversation which took place between Mr Treacy and Mr Trethewy on 12 June 1998. The statements made by Mr Trethewy in that conversation rebut any suggestion that the terms of contract 33 were still subject to negotiation or were incomplete or vague and uncertain.

  676. The conversation must be understood in context. It was on 29 May 1998 that Mr Murphy fired the first salvo across the bows of PCA suggesting that deals transacted were not binding. The evidence of Mr Murphy in relation to why he wrote the facsimile on that day was, to say the least, evasive, shuffling and unconvincing. I am satisfied that he realised on or about that day that he, Mr Murphy, on behalf of PP had placed PP in a position where it was about to over-commit its supply of electricity on futures contracts. He was looking for reasons why he could renege on transactions made not only in May 1998 but in late 1997. His letter of 10 June reinforces that conclusion.

  677. Despite this Mr Trethewy and Mr Treacy had a friendly telephone conversation on 12 June 1998. Mr Trethewy accepted in evidence that he may have spoken to Mr Murphy and that he did know prior to 12 June 1998 that the parties were in dispute.

  678. The telephone call was recorded.

  679. Mr Treacy referred to a visit that he had had with Mr Murphy on 11 June at which he gave him a list of outstanding contracts and asked Mr Trethewy which ones were straightforward and could be done at the moment. He pointed out that some went back about 12 months.

  680. The conversation continued -

    681. "CT: I don't know whether the issue is only the ones that we just did now or what. I mean I felt the ones we did in November are all okay.

    DT: Sorry?

    CT: The ones we did in November are all okay?

    DT: Yeah.

    CT: The fax that Bob sent to me on 10 June Wednesday says most of the considerations apply to the proposed contracts 38, 39, 40 and 41 - but those four are all November contracts of last year.

    DT: Ah, I'm not with you. 38, 39, 40 and 41 are contracts starting in July as far as I'm aware.

    CT: Yeah, I know but they were negotiated in November last year.

    DT: Yes, yes.

    CT: And I thought everything had been agreed on those ones.

    DT: That is my understanding.

    CT: Yes. I need to find out which one's you've got a query with and which ones you haven'.

    DT: Okay, yeah. I don't think there's any issue to do with the negotiated arrangements. I think Bob was just saying that they are outstanding."

  681. The conversation continued -

    682. "CT: So of that list I've given to Bob which ones are these - you know like there's ones - like Air Liquide, Visy which has come back even longer than that like June last year.

    DT: Well, they were June last year but they don't start until July.

    CT: I know - that's what I'm saying - but I want to make sure that these contracts - when can we get them going through to us if there's not an issue with them and they're fine to go through. I just want to know which ones we have an issue with.

    DT: As far as I'm aware there's no issue with them - they just need to be documented."

    (Emphasis added.)

  682. Shortly thereafter Mr Treacy said that they had all been agreed to and Mr Trethewy said - "Well, I was under the impression that they were all agreed to as well."

  683. Mr Trethewy was the man within PP whose job it was to prepare confirmation documents concerning the contracts and he was the man responsible for the facsimile of 13 January 1998 in which he set out the details of the new contracts.

  684. Any suggestion that terms were incomplete, subject to further negotiation, uncertain, vague and incapable of operation are refuted by that telephone conversation of 12 June. Mr Trethewy would have a very good understanding of the terms of each agreement and the way they operated. He did not suggest lack of agreement, uncertainty or that further terms had to be agreed. He did not suggest that the agreement was incomplete. Prior to 19 June 1998, neither did Mr Murphy.

  685. I reject the submissions of PP. Contract 33 was agreed. So was the variation.

    686. (II) Contract No. 35

  686. This alleged contract is known as the Smorgon contract because it was negotiated between the parties with the proposed PCA retail contract with Smorgon being the contract the hedge was to support.

  687. PCA contend that the agreement was concluded on or about 13 November 1997.

  688. PP contend that the parties did not agree to all the terms, that there was incompleteness and uncertainty and further, the parties never intended to be legally bound.

  689. By way of background, in 1996 PCA had entered into an agreement with Smorgon to supply it with electricity, subsequently the parties varied the agreement and it was necessary for PCA to hedge against the risk of loss in relation to the varied retail supply which was to commence well into the future.

  690. On 29 July 1997 Mr Treacy spoke to Mr Murphy in which he informed him that PCA had a Victorian customer who had a contract for four years, that PCA was looking to extend it to a ten year deal and that PCA needed a financial contract to supports its retail contract for years six to ten. He followed this up with a facsimile setting out the details. The negotiations covered the period, a Meet the Market clause at the end of certain years and proposed prices with a CPI minus one escalation formulae.

  691. Mr Murphy stated the terms of an offer and by facsimile dated 30 July 1997 Mr Treacy set out in summary form the proposed terms which covered period, load, MTM clause, pricing details and that it was for peak energy. In respect of off-peak PCA was to have a month in which to source the product from some other party at which time PCA would indicate whether it would purchase off-peak from PP.

  692. On 28 August 1997, Mr Treacy had a discussion with Mr Murphy and proposed a term to the effect that if Smorgon closed down a site then the volume of the hedge contract could be reduced at PCA's option to cover the reduction. It was agreed that Mr Treacy would provide the wording to cover this term which he arranged through solicitors and faxed a draft back to Mr Murphy that day.

  693. On 29 August Mr Murphy informed Mr Treacy that the proposed terms looked "okay".

  694. In the absence of Mr Treacy on holidays, a meeting took place on 11 September 1997 involving Mr Murphy and Dr Nelson and Mr Daley. I accept the evidence of Mr Daley that Mr Murphy agreed to the off-peak supply of notional electricity for the transaction.

  695. On 8 October 1997 Mr Murphy wrote to Dr Nelson in which he indicated that prices and volumes for the peak component and the volume variation clause in the event of Smorgon closing down sites was acceptable.

  696. On 23 October 1997 a meeting took place in Sydney between Mr Duvall and Mr Treacy from PCA and Messrs Murphy and Petch on behalf of PP. At this meeting Mr Treacy informed the meeting that the retail contracts with Smorgon were ready for signing but there was a decision to be made on a holiday clause and he agreed to advise PP of Smorgon's reaction. It was agreed between the parties that there would be flex of plus or minus 10% per month clause and a one-off three months holiday foreclosure in the life of the contract. On 12 November 1997 Mr Treacy sent a facsimile to Mr Murphy setting out the issues to be covered in a meeting the next day and one of the topics was the Smorgon status report.

  697. On 13 November 1997 at a meeting in Melbourne between Messrs Treacy and Daley and Messrs Murphy and Oates, PCA agreed to take up PP's offer and PCA was to forward the load shape and summary of the deal. I reject the evidence of Mr Murphy that he did not agree to proceed with the transaction on that day.

  698. On 21 November in a facsimile Mr Treacy confirmed what had been discussed at the meeting and in reference to the Smorgon transaction recorded -

    699. "* Contracts agreed to were as follows: * Smorgon - PCA to forward load shape and summary of deal."

  699. In the facsimile Mr Treacy requested Mr Murphy to inform him if there was any variation to the details. He never did.

  700. Shortly thereafter Mr Treacy signed a trade ticket in respect of Smorgon which, as I have already indicated, is the PCA internal administration process to record a transaction when concluded.

  701. On 2 December 1997 Mr Treacy faxed an agenda of a proposed meeting to Mr Murphy and one of the matters for consideration was contract status and six contracts were noted, including Smorgon.

  702. On 8 December 1997 Mr Treacy sent a facsimile to Mr Murphy noting the items that were to be actioned as the result of the discussions had on 4 December and under the heading - "* outstanding contracts and their priorities are as follows:" and under that heading was listed seven contracts, including -

    703. "* Smorgon Starts 2001"

  703. Nothing was said by Mr Murphy to suggest that there had not been a concluded transaction. It is noted that the facsimile of 8 December referred to a number of transactions such as Alcoa, BHP and the 20 MW collar which were proceeded with and the subject of exchanged confirmations.

  704. The language was clearly that of a completed transaction.

  705. On 23 December 1997 Mr Treacy sent a facsimile to Mr Murphy listing the outstanding contracts and one contract identified concerned Smorgon with a note "PP to raise contract".

  706. I refer to the facsimile dated 13 January 1998 sent by PP to PCA setting out the new contract numbers and the Smorgon contract was listed as contract No. 35.

  707. The opening words of the the facsimile have been set out above and in relation to Smorgon appears the following -

    708. "35 340-20-N Smorgan (sic), 1 Aug 01 to 30 Jun 07" and a reference to the load shape.

  708. Mr Trethewy who sent the summary to Mrs Sim ended up by requesting -

    709. "Would you please advise of any mistakes in the details."

  709. One mistake was the omission to refer to flex. This had already been agreed as could be seen from the summary of the issues discussed at the meeting of 23 October 1997.

  710. The load shape was provided to PP by Dr Nelson by e-mail on 13 January 1998.

  711. The document forwarded around about 4 February 1998 by PCA to PP refers to outstanding contracts and it is noted in respect to contract 35 that PP was to raise the contract.

  712. Mr Murphy states that he paid no attention to the comment because, as he put it, PCA still had not provided a summary of the terms. I do not accept that evidence. It is correct that the summary of terms promised on 13 November 1997 was not sent but the failure did not undo what had been done. The terms had been agreed. Mr Murphy never asked for the summary. Mr Trethewy's facsimile refers to the Smorgon contract and the parties proceeded on the assumption that the contract had been concluded.

  713. In a facsimile dated 28 May 1998 from Mrs Sim to Mr Trethewy, Mrs Sim advised that PCA was still waiting for PP to raise the contract. No response was made to this document or the earlier documents.

  714. I do not accept that PP was waiting for PCA to send details of the agreement.

  715. Indeed, after the dispute had arisen and Mr Murphy was drafting the confirmation documents in June 1998 he drafted a document in respect of this transaction and had no difficulty in setting out the terms in relation to all matters including monthly flex, Victorian Power Exchange, Meet the Market arrangements, price escalator and attached a load profile.

  716. At no stage prior to 19 June 1998 did PP respond to any correspondence from PCA which referred to a contract suggesting there was no contract. Their conduct was consistent with agreement to the terms of the transaction.

    717. PP's submissions

  717. PP submits that there was lack of consensus with respect to price, reference pool, Meet the Market clause, volume of electricity, flex term, load shape, and the question of peak and off-peak. It was also submitted that there was incompleteness and uncertainty with respect to some of the alleged terms leading to the conclusion there was no contract.

  718. These submissions are put in support of the general assertion that the parties did not make a contract and did not intend to be bound.

  719. It is pertinent to observe that the draft document Mr Murphy prepared on 18 June 1998 had a trade date of 13 November 1997, that is, the date when the transaction was concluded and a start and termination date.

  720. In considering these submissions it is noted that on 13 January 1998 Mr Trethewy sent a summary of new contracts which included this contract. PCA sent a number of summaries to PP in which it is noted that PP was to raise the contract and at the end of May 1998 Mrs Sim pointed out to PP that it was dragging its feet on preparing the confirmation and yet at no time did PP suggest that the terms of the transaction had not been agreed. One then adds the confirmation document prepared by Mr Murphy on 18 June 1998. One has to consider the PP submissions in the context of those events occurring.

  721. I also add the telephone conversation between Mr Treacy and Mr Trethewy on 12 June 1998 part of which I reproduced in the consideration of contract No. 33. Whilst the Smorgon contract was not specifically mentioned the conversation discussed contracts made about the same time and there was no suggestion that the contract was not concluded.

  722. The first area of dispute concerns the price.

  723. PP submits that the prices for years one to four of the hedge contract were agreed for peak and off-peak energy but no agreement was reached for years five and six for peak and off-peak energy.

  724. I reject this argument. It proceeds on an unrealistic approach to what the parties were agreeing. The facsimile of 30 July 1997 on a fair reading makes it clear that the parties had agreed to the prices for years five and six on a CPI minus one escalation.

  725. That there is no doubt that PP read the transaction in the same way is clear from the draft Smorgon confirmation prepared in June 1998 which was based upon earlier drafts. This included prices for years five and six. The load shape prepared for Smorgon on 13 January 1998 was in respect to peak and off-peak hours. The clear commercial intention of the parties was to include the off-peak hours.

  726. It is noted in the 30 July 1997 facsimile that PCA was to attempt to source the off-peak product elsewhere but if not it would advise whether it wished to purchase the off-peak from PP. There is no evidence that PCA did inform PP of that fact but the other evidence including the load profile dated 13 January 1998 made it clear that PCA wished to purchase the off-peak from PP. PP never stated prior to 19 June 1998 that it did not include off-peak.

  727. I reject the argument that there is any incompleteness or uncertainty about the price.

  728. The next argument put by PP is that there was no agreement as to the reference pool but clearly the load shape forwarded by e-mail on 13 January refers to the Victorian pool by the notation - "DLS/VIC". In addition, Smorgon only had sites in Victoria and clearly it was a Victorian based transaction. I reject the submission of PP that there was no agreement with respect to the pool.

  729. The next argument is that the Meet the Market clause was not agreed, and lacked completeness and certainty. The evidence is clear that PCA sought an MTM clause at the end of the years one, three and five and Mr Murphy accepted in evidence that he agreed to such an arrangement. A revised MTM wording was provided on 10 November by PCA and I accept the evidence that Mr Murphy agreed that the wording was okay subject to confirmation from PP's solicitors.

  730. I accept the evidence that Mr Murphy did inform Mr Treacy in late November that the MTM clause was okay.

  731. Further, the evidence discloses that Mr Murphy did not at any stage object to the wording of the MTM clause which was subsequently incorporated into other contracts and the form of the Meet the Market clause was included in the draft confirmations for contracts 39, 40 and 41. It was the same as confirmed for contracts 36 and 37.

  732. I reject the evidence that PP did not accept the Meet the Market clause. Indeed, the draft of 18 June 1998 prepared by Mr Trethewy or Mr Murphy refers to a Meet the Market clause which is set out in the attachment to the document. The clause attached is the same as put forward in November 1997.

  733. Some time was spent in argument as to whether the Meet the Market clause could operate or whether it was too uncertain. In my opinion that is a matter of construction. In the circumstances the attack on the meaning does not establish that the parties were proposing to further negotiate its terms. It is clear that the parties intended to enter into such a clause and did so and in the end if there is any dispute

    as to its operation it is a matter of construction to determine the common intention of the parties.

  734. The next dispute concerns the alleged volume of electricity and it is said that there was no consensus in relation to this. Any doubt about the volume of electricity was resolved in Dr Nelson's load shape sent by e-mail on 13 January 1998. Mr Treacy confirmed the volume and that it applied to peak and off-peak. The evidence showed Messrs Murphy and Trethewy discussed the e-mail and Mr Murphy instructed Mr Trethewy to prepare the draft confirmation. Mr Trethewy confirmed the volume which covered peak and off-peak at 340 GWh that day by facsimile after discussion with Mr Murphy.

  735. Agreement was reached that volume could be reduced if Smorgon closed down one of its sites and this appears in the facsimile sent by Mr Treacy to Mr Murphy on 28 August 1997.

  736. I reject the argument that there was any lack of agreement or uncertainty about the volume of electricity.

  737. PP submits that there was lack of agreement with respect to flex, and the term was incomplete and uncertain. The evidence established agreement to a term which I have already referred to above and with respect to the alleged uncertainty as to its operation in my opinion that is a question of construction to determine the common intention of the parties to the agreed term. That alleged uncertainty does not persuade me that the parties were still in a state of negotiation.

  738. PP submits that the parties had not agreed to a formula whereby the load shape for each year of the contract was to be determined. On 13 January 1998 Dr Nelson forwarded a load shape for year one to PP. It is noted that on receipt Mr Trethewy sent a facsimile which recorded the contract, specified the numbers of each contract and the details of each contract, and with respect to Smorgon, which was to run from 1 August 2001 to 30 June 2007, Mr Trethewy acknowledged receipt of the load

    profile and requested Mrs Sim to "advise of any mistakes in the details".

  739. Thereafter there was no query with respect to the load shape being acceptable to PP.

  740. Perusal of the load shape makes it clear that the Victorian node is applicable, the energy is noted at 340 GWh and the load factor is noted at 83%.

  741. It is said in those circumstances by PP that the parties were still in a state of negotiation because there was no agreement with respect to what was to happen in the future.

  742. In order to consider this submission, it is necessary to take into account the negotiations between the parties with respect to the various types of contracts. The fact was that the parties agreed PCA was to provide a load profile in respect of the Smorgon contract. It did so. It was accepted and acknowledged as being appropriate. No query was raised in respect to it. Clearly, the parties had agreed the parameters which were to be satisfied.

  743. If one goes back in time this is so. On 21 May 1997 Mr Murphy indicated the basis upon which PP was prepared to transact in relation to long-term contracts relating to peak supply and this was put as a minimum load factor of 38% in each year being "150 Mwmax demand at 500 GWh pa". The Smorgon load profile sent on 13 January 1998 met that criteria.

  744. Clearly, the parties had by their conduct accepted an agreed formula by which the load shape would be determined. That was within agreed parameters.

  745. It is a question of construction of the contract as to what the common intention of the parties was in relation to the load shape each year.

  746. I am satisfied a formula is in place and its application is a matter of construction. I find that there was no lack of agreement with respect to the term concerning load shape.

  747. PP further submits that there was no agreement between the parties with respect to off-peak supply.

  748. As stated above, in July 1997 it was proposed that PCA would obtain off-peak cover from another source but it was indicated that if it was unable to do so PCA was to inform PP that it wished to obtain the off-peak supply from PP also. There is some dispute in the evidence as to whether Mr Treacy did exercise what has been described as an option and I accept his evidence that he did so within that month.

  749. On 11 September 1997 at a meeting Mr Daley confirmed with Mr Murphy that Mr Treacy had already accepted the off-peak because PCA could not find another party who would offer Victorian off-peak prices.

  750. Further, the load profile forwarded on 13 January 1998 to Mr Trethewy specifies the total volume of electricity covering both peak and off-peak. The load shape was accepted and at no stage was it suggested that it was not to cover off-peak.

  751. The totality of the evidence leads me to the conclusion that it was agreed that the supply was to also cover off-peak.

  752. I have considered the submissions by PP as to the certainty question in respect of a number of terms. As I have already said lack of certainty in respect of some terms may lead to the inference that the parties were in a state of negotiation. I do not draw that inference in respect to the alleged uncertainties. Any questions as to the application of a particular term is a question of construction to determine the common intention of the parties.

  753. Accordingly, subject to the holding back argument, and the question of authority, I am satisfied that the parties did agree to the terms of the transaction which subsequently was identified as contract No. 35.

  754. The application of the Master Agreement establishes the parties intended to be bound. This conclusion is subject to the holding back argument.

    755. (III) Contract No. 38

  755. Contract 38 is a collar contract and PCA alleges that it and contracts 39, 40 and 41 were all concluded on the one day, namely, 28 November 1997.

  756. This group of alleged contracts all share a common genesis but it is appropriate to deal with contract 38 separately and then to deal with the other three contracts together. PCA alleges that the other contracts were varied in June 1998.

  757. This alleged contract is a collar contract for the supply of off-peak notional electricity.

  758. PCA alleges that six transactions were finalised on 28 November 1997 and contract Nos. 36 and 37 have and are being performed. These contracts were all part of a package.

  759. The 6 November transactions resulted from PCA's plan to secure a large volume of electricity for the future at prices then prevailing in the market. The plan was to seek to incorporate Meet the Market clauses so that if the market price dropped and retail customers called on the MTM clause on PCA, PCA would not be left with a stranded hedge. This plan was devised in mid 1997 and at a meeting on 1 August 1997 Messrs Duvall and Treacy and Dr Nelson met with Mr Murphy and raised the prospect of transacting commodity derivative contracts for a total 2,000 GWh of power over ten years. This is an enormous quantity of notional electricity.

  760. Prior to that meeting Mr Treacy faxed an agenda to Mr Murphy and the agenda contained the following item for discussion -

    761. "Long Time Draw Down Product - this incorporates current terms and conditions we have negotiated for SECV/Cadia/BHP, into a single combination of items we can draw on. We would need to know the maximum volume load that PP could offer under this scenario."

  761. Terms were being finalised in relation to the three companies mentioned. The amount of notional electricity was large and the periods were lengthy.

  762. The object of the exercise was to ascertain a maximum volume load, and agree the terms and PCA would be entitled from time to time to transact a commodity derivative contract in relation to a portion of the energy load made available on common agreed terms.

  763. Discussion took place at the meeting in respect to the Long Term Draw Down Product.

  764. It will be necessary to return to this proposal hereafter.

  765. Disputed contract 38 can be dealt with separately because it is an off-peak collar transaction. At the meeting Dr Nelson gave a presentation how he wanted the collar contract to operate in respect to off-peak. This was a new arrangement as far as PCA was concerned.
