  1043. Fourthly, PCA was reluctant to finalise the agreements because the negotiations with respect to some retail contracts were still continuing. PCA was anxious to avoid being left with a stranded hedge.

  1044. There is a dispute as to the date when PP repudiated the contracts. In my opinion it repudiated the May 1998 contracts on 10 June 1998 and the November 1997 contracts on 26 June 1998.

  1045. Mr Treacy, Mr Duvall, Mrs Sim and Dr Nelson were cross-examined about various documents which suggested that finalisation of the contracts be delayed from PCA's end. On 14 April 1998 Mr Treacy sent an e-mail to a number of people within PCA in which there is reference to delaying contracts until a certain review is completed. On 19 May 1998 there is a strategy meeting action sheet prepared by Mr Daley after a meeting which suggested PCA was trying to improve its position in relation to a number of retail agreements. Further, there is evidence of a folder on which is attached a tab which reads - "Outstanding, okay for PP to raise contract 28/5." Further, there is a document which came into existence on 26 May 1998 headed 'PP VIC CONTRACT RE NEGOTIATION'."

  1046. That is a reference to Air Liquide.

  1047. On a copy of the Flex rules and Procedures dated 17 March 1998 Mrs Sim has written - "26/5 - wait for letter from Chris." On that same day Mrs Sim prepared a summary of contracts 39, 40 and 41 in which she has written in respect of 40 and 41 - "Chris to confirm before PP to raise contract." As against contract 39 she has written - "Hold back for Chris. Fax to follow."

  1048. Contracts 39, 40 and 41 were made on 28 November 1997.

  1049. Mrs Sim prepared a schedule of outstanding contracts as at 17 June 1998. In this document Mrs Sim recorded in respect of contracts 39, 40 and 41 that she was informed in April 1998 to hold back signing contracts due to terms/prices not finalised. With respect to contract 41 she noted that on the 8 May 1998, she was to ask Mr Treacy to view contracts before signing by PP. It appears that Mr Treacy told Mrs Sim to hold this contract back.

  1050. It is submitted on the part of PP that the conduct of Mr Treacy and other officers of PCA demonstrate that from about the beginning of April 1998 to 28 May 1998 Mr Treacy had formed the opinion that PCA should not proceed with contracts 39, 40 and 41.

  1051. In cross-examination Mr Treacy on a number of occasions stated he could not recall giving instructions to anyone prior to 28 May 1998 to hold back contracts or confirmations and denied saying to anyone that he wanted to confirm something in connection with contracts 40 and 41 before they were raised.

  1052. I was not impressed by the way Messrs Treacy, Duvall, Daley and McGarry gave evidence in relation to the various instructions noted in the documents. They all showed uneasiness whilst being cross-examined. So did Mrs Sim. I found their denials of any knowledge of directive of holding back finalisation of documents implausible.

  1053. With respect to Mr Duvall, he said that he saw the document prepared by Mrs Sim dated 17 June 1998, he thought that the references to holding back were errors and yet despite this he gave a copy of the document to two very senior members of the Board of the holding company Pacific Corp who were at that time in Melbourne and did not suggest any errors in the summary.

  1054. I do not accept the evidence of Mr Treacy that he did not give instructions as to holding back the documents. For some reason the confirmation documents were to be delayed.

  1055. A rejection of evidence does not establish any fact other than the rejection. On the other hand, a lie in court may be the basis for inferring a positive fact. However, the court must carefully weigh all the circumstances before reaching a positive finding because a witness may lie for a reason which is not related to the particiular issue and hence does not establish the particular relation fact.

  1056. Mr Myers QC on behalf of PCA submitted that there were a number of reasons which would explain the documentation within PCA and which did not lead to the inference that PCA was of the view that it was still negotiating and did not intend to be bound. He referred to the fact that the events during the first five months of 1998 were no different to the previous conduct of the parties, that any delay in the preparation of confirmation documents was the fault of PP, that the reference to "wait for contract" on the trade ticket for Visy was written in 1997 between 13 November and 1 December 1997 and not in 1998, and that everything that was done was consistent with the PCA wishing to proceed. The hold back references in May 1998 was an aide memoire for Mrs Sim. At that time there were some discussions about linking and possible change of prices. Further, in the context that PCA was holding back for its own reasons was inconsistent with communications in early February 1998 and May 1998 when lists were provided by PCA as to outstanding contracts and requests being made by Mrs Sim to provide documentation. Finally, it is said that PCA was not concerned about signing the confirmation documents because the conduct of the parties had been based upon trust as the prior dealings showed and upon the ISDA documentation. The confirmations were no more and no less then a record of a pre-existing agreement. The dealings in relation to the undisputed 35 contracts supported the conclusion that the confirmation documents were to be prepared to enable settlement. That was a matter for the parties to address in their own time.

  1057. Whilst I accept that there were any number of reasons which would explain the notations on the various documents nevertheless, as I put to Mr Myers QC, why did not the witnesses give one of the explanations he suggested? Yet they did not. Messrs Treacy and Duvall were evasive and prevaricated about the matter and Mrs Sim was distinctly uncomfortable in the witness box explaining the notations.

  1058. As I have said I do not accept the evidence of Mr Treacy that he did not give any instructions, or that he was not holding on to documents, but having rejected the evidence I am not prepared to infer that the documents and decisions were held up because PCA considered it was not bound and was still negotiating. The conduct of PCA towards PP was to the contrary.

  1059. PCA in final address prepared a chronology regarding the execution of non-disputed and disputed contracts from September 1997 to July 1998. I have closely considered the chronology and agree that the series of events rebut any suggestion that PCA was not prepared to proceed with the contracts made in November 1997 and considered itself not bound.

  1060. In December 1997 PCA impressed upon PP's representatives the importance of finalising the documentation with respect to agreements about to commence on 1 January 1998. On 5 December agreement was reached upon prioritising documentation of outstanding contracts. The parties agreed to proceed first with documenting the contracts commencing on 1 January 1998. Mr Treacy sent a facsimile to Mr Murphy listing the outstanding contracts and their priorities and reference was made to the eight contracts made in November 1997.

  1061. In a facsimile of 10 December 1997 Mrs Sim complained to Mr Trethewy about not receiving "signed contracts timely. Without prompt contract finalisation, settlement between our companies cannot be done promptly. Your prompt attention to this matter will be appreciated."

  1062. This request did not prompt any greater expedition from PP. On 17 December 1997 Dr Nelson sent the load profiles for contracts, 36 37, 39 and 41. On 23 December 1997 Mr Treacy sent a facsimile listing the outstanding contracts which included the November contracts and noted in a comments column - "PP to raise contract". He noted the importance of actioning the ones commencing on 1 January 1998. On 13 January 1998 PCA forwarded the Smorgon load shape which was uploaded by PP into its computer system and on the same day Mr Trethewy sent his facsimile setting out the contract numbers. On 21 January 1998 Mrs Sim sent a facsimile to Mr Trethewy with amendments to the confirmations of contracts 34, 36, and 37.

  1063. On 5 February 1998 Mrs Sim accompanied Mr Treacy to Sydney to meet Mr Trethewy for the purpose of having discussions about documentation of confirmations and to speed up the process. A list of outstanding contracts were provided to PP which included some of the November contracts.

  1064. On 9 February 1998 Mrs Sim sent a facsimile to Mr Trethewy and observed - "If there is anything I can do to help you speed up your contract paperwork please let me know."

  1065. On 18 February 1998 in another facsimile Mrs Sim pointed out in reference to contracts 36, 37, 34 and 31 - "I hope we can finalise these above contracts as soon as possible as all of them start on 1 January 1998 and settlement is coming up shortly".

  1066. Queries were raised in May 1998 concerning finalisation of the documentation.

  1067. The evidence demonstrates that PCA was expressing concern at the delays in PP preparing confirmations.

  1068. Despite rejecting the evidence of Mr Treacy and the unsatisfactory evidence given I am not prepared to make a finding based upon their failure to explain the holding back of some documents that PCA did not intend to be bound by the transactions. There is a substantial body of evidence that supports the conclusion that within PCA it did consider that it was bound by the transactions. I refer to the trade ticket procedure and also the preparation, consideration and delivery of draft confirmations between the parties.

  1069. In my opinion there are a number of factors which show that despite the leisurely pace with which the parties considered the confirmations the fact was that PCA intended to be bound by the transactions concluded in November 1997.

  1070. First, the pattern of trading between the parties showed a leisurely attitude to finalising confirmations. In respect of most of the undisputed contracts, the confirmations came after performance commenced. Sometimes many weeks after the period had commenced.

  1071. Secondly, the parties prior to 29 May 1998 had a very good relationship based on trust and fair dealing. This is exemplified by the "validity period" in relation to offers. As a matter of law, in the absence of any consideration paid to keep an offer open, PP could have ignored the validity period and withdrawn offers. But it did not do so. The fact was that the parties traded on a basis of mutual trust. Hence, there was never any disagreement with respect to any confirmation. There were queries about and errors in confirmations, but these were always resolved without difficulty.

  1072. Thirdly, PP was responsible for preparing the confirmations. It in fact did prepare all confirmations save for the first one or two which were exchanged in late 1996/early 1997. At times PP was dragging its feet. Mrs Sim on occasions required Mr Trethewy to hasten the sending of the confirmation and did offer to assist.

  1073. Fourthly, in the period March to the end of May 1998 PCA did seek to vary the terms of contracts with respect to the question of linking of contracts 40 and 41 and also varying terms. These attempts held back the finalisation of confirmations because PCA was seeking to vary the transactions. In my view they were not to be viewed as steps of negotiation nor any evidence of an intention not to be bound.

  1074. Fifthly, the four hedge contracts and particularly 39, 40 and 41 did not rely upon any particular retail contract. The hedges were to support many retail contracts. It was not a question of holding back for a particular retail contract.

  1075. Sixthly, the spot price was of little moment in my opinion to the decision made by PCA with respect to the transactions. The prices were fixed on the date of the transaction. The movement of prices during the first five months of 1998 was of little relevance to contracts covering long periods commencing well into the future. For example, contracts 35, 40 and 41 were to start in 2001.

  1076. Seventhly, the evidence of PCA's internal administration showed that the trade tickets were filled out on the day the transaction was completed or very soon thereafter. The trade system ticket system was to record the transaction and provide the basis for the confirmation document and settlement. This evidence cogently supports the intention of PCA to be bound by the contracts when made.

  1077. Eighthly, on many occasions between December 1997 and the end of May 1998 PCA raised the issue of completing the confirmation documents.

  1078. Ninthly, the evidence establishes that Mrs Sim responded promptly to any queries raised concerning the confirmations and also referring errors back to Mr Trethewy.

  1079. Finally, the evidence concerning contracts 39, 40 and 41 and their confirmations show that there were reasons for the delays which do not amount to an intention on PCA's part not to proceed or be bound. The confirmations were sent for consideration in mid March by Mr Trethewy to Mr Treacy. Thereafter queries were raised as to errors, it was necessary to check the details, in late March there was agreement with respect to flex rules and procedure and annual load shape reviews and in mid April 1998 PCA made a decision to hold up the confirmations because of a review of overall load shapes which was expected to take three weeks. On 29 April the question of linking contracts 39, 40 and 41 was raised. On 4 May Mr Trethewy forwarded to Mrs Sim the confirmation of contract 41 with amendments requested by Mrs Sim some two months previously. Mrs Sim the next day pointed out that there was an error in relation to the pricing. On 8 May in a discussion between Mrs Sim and Mr Trethewy she informed him that the confirmations were being held up and she gave evidence that she was waiting on a linking letter. By mid May Mr Murphy had confirmed that the procedure for linking was appropriate for contracts 40 and 41 and Mr Treacy stated he would get a letter to combine the contracts. On 20 May 1998 Mr Treacy raised the question of varying the price in contract 39. On 28 May Mrs Sim sent two facsimiles to Mr Trethewy stating that she was still waiting for the confirmations for Visy and Smorgon. There were further discussions between Messrs Treacy and Murphy as to contracts 39 and 40 at the end of May.

  1080. The various comments made on the documentation within PCA in my view are explicable in relation to the events that were occurring at the time. I do not accept the submission that the evidence demonstrates that PCA was holding back finalising confirmations because it was still negotiating, that it did not think it was bound and did not intend to contract.

  1081. None of the evidence leads to a conclusion that PCA was still negotiating or did not intend to be bound and insofar as there were further discussions concerning terms in relation to some of the contracts I am satisfied that what was being sought was a variation of contracts already concluded.

  1082. Further, none of the evidence rebuts the presumption established by the Master Agreement that the parties intended to be bound when they agreed to the terms of the transaction. None of the evidence in relation to any transaction shows that the parties did not intend to be bound by their agreement to the terms.

  1083. The list of outstanding contracts prepared by Mrs Sim on 17 June 1998 is a record prepared by her of her understanding of the position. I am satisfied, that in relation to some aspects that she was mistaken as to what the true position was. I do not accept that that document represents a considered position of PCA and amounts to evidence that PCA did not intend to be bound.

  1084. Finally, I refer to the conversation between Mr Trethewy and Mr Treacy on 12 June 1998. It is clear from that conversation that Mr Trethewy whose function it was within PP to prepare and finalise confirmation documents was of the opinion that all matters in relation to all the disputed contracts had been agreed and there were no problems. In other words he did not draw the inference from the events of the first five months of 1998 that PCA and PP were still negotiating or that PCA did not intend to be bound by the transactions.

  1085. PP further submit that irrespective of the evidence relating to the alleged holding back there was other evidence which would lead to the conclusion that the parties did not intend to be bound by any of the disputed contracts.

  1086. These submissions must be considered in the context of what the parties agreed to in the Master Agreement concerning intention, the finding that the parties agreed to be bound once all the terms of the transaction had been agreed, and that the confirmation was a record only of a pre-existing agreement. Further, the actions of both parties up to 29 May 1998 objectively manifested an intention to be bound by the November agreements.

  1087. In considering this submission I put to one side the argument concerning authority. I will deal with this separately. Either the PP employees had the authority or they did not.

  1088. PP submits that the period of this lengthy litigation demonstrates a lack of objective intention. It was also argued that neither party objectively could have intended to enter into contracts by facsimiles and telephone calls in circumstances where there were so many disputes as to whether the contracts were concluded and binding. This is an interesting and novel argument but I reject it. The length of the litigation can be explained on many grounds, none of which are relevant to the question of intention. I do not accept that the parties dealing in transactions of this type did not intend to enter into contracts by reason of the fact that they negotiated per telephone, at meetings and through the medium of facsimiles. It is said that the length and the complexity of negotiations show that the parties did not intend to be bound until a formal document was entered into.

  1089. I do accept that the type and complexity of negotiations may lead to the conclusion that parties did not intend to be bound until they had formally recorded all the terms in a document. Each case must depend upon its own circumstances. Here the pattern of trading, the type of transaction involved and the terms of the Master Agreement all lead to the conclusion that the contract was concluded when the terms were agreed and the fact that they were lengthy negotiations involving complex matters does not in my view provide any evidence that the parties did not intend to be bound until all the terms were agreed in a formal document. The fact that the negotiations were over a long period also in my view does not show that the parties did not intend to be bound.

  1090. The undeniable facts are that the parties used the verbiage of contracting not only in oral discussions but in writing, and facsimiles were sent by PCA confirming what had been agreed which were not questioned by PP. Mr Trethewy's letter of 13 January 1998 established that the parties had agreed and intended to be bound.

  1091. Putting aside the terms of the Master Agreement and considering the totality of the evidence it is my opinion that the contracts made in November 1997 were intended by the parties to bind them in law and the parties are bound.

  1092. None of the evidence established a variation of the Master Agreement with respect to the term concerning intention to be bound. The parties agreed as to when they intended to be bound in respect to each transaction. Their agreement must be upheld.

  1093. It follows that each of the 11 disputed contracts was a contract concluded and binding in law.

    1094. PCA's reliance on estoppel

  1094. In its amended reply PCA pleaded that in the event the court found that the contracts were not concluded and binding until the letters of confirmation were signed and exchanged, PP was estopped from denying that the contracts were concluded and binding prior to the exchange of the signed confirmation.

  1095. PCA relies upon the principles of equitable estoppel to establish this plea.

  1096. Because of the finding I have made, that the contracts were binding when made and prior to the exchange of signed confirmation documents, it is strictly unnecessary for me to consider the plea.

  1097. However, because of the prospects of the proceeding going on appeal I propose to briefly state my reasons with respect to the plea.

  1098. The plea is found in paragraph s 6C, D and E of PCA's amended reply and defence to counterclaim.

  1099. The court raised the adequacy of the pleading in its original form and on 1 June 1999 approximately halfway through the trial the plaintiff was granted leave to amend, inter alia, the pleading concerning estoppel.

  1100. No complaint was made by PP to the adequacy of the pleading until the issue was raised during the course of final address.

  1101. The plea is not a model of drafting and fails to properly allege each constituent element of estoppel. Further, the particulars provided not only in the pleading but in the further and better particulars only compound the uncertainty as to the way the estoppel is to be established.

  1102. In paragraph 6C it is alleged that PP "well knew or ought to have known that PCA would enter into commitments with third parties and would act otherwise to its detriment on the faith of such contracts being concluded prior to the letters of confirmation being signed." (Emphasis added)

  1103. The reference to "contracts" is a reference to the disputed contracts.

  1104. It is noted that the allegation of the assumed state of affairs is that a contract came into being prior to a letter of confirmation being signed.

  1105. Despite the terms of paragraph 6C, in paragraph 6D it is alleged, inter alia, that PCA acted to its detriment "by entering into agreements with third parties in reliance upon" and thereafter it is alleged reliance was based first, upon offers made and communicated, second, acceptances of offers made and communicated to PP and finally, upon the said contracts.

  1106. When it was pointed out during final address that there was an inconsistency between paragraphs 6C and 6D, Mr Myers QC informed the court that the basis of the plea was that PCA entered into agreements with other parties in reliance upon "the existence of the said contracts" ie the 11 disputed agreements.

  1107. The plea is not based upon offers made or the acceptance of offers made. The reliance is on the hedge contracts binding the parties before confirmation.

  1108. It is also noted that PCA alleged it suffered detriment by refraining from entering into hedging agreements with other parties to support retail agreements between PCA and third parties in reliance upon the existence of the disputed hedge contracts.

  1109. In general terms the primary purpose of estoppel is to prevent an injustice to a party which has proceeded on a certain basis as a result of an assumed state of affairs existing between the parties as a result of the other party withdrawing from the assumed state of affairs.

  1110. As Brennan J said in Waltons Stores case [1988] HCA 7; (1988) 164 CLR 387 at 419 the fundamental purpose of estoppel is to provide "protection against the detriment which would flow from a party's change of position if the assumption (or expectation) that led to it were deserted".

  1111. PCA relies upon the principles established in the Waltons Stores case and the decision of the New South Wales Court of Appeal in Austotel Pty Ltd v Franklins Self Serve Stores Pty Ltd (1989) 16 NSWLR 582.

  1112. Waltons Stores is an important decision in the development of equitable estoppel in Australian law. It had the effect of changing the pre-existing law in three important ways. First, the court held that the requirement that the parties be in a pre-existing legal relationship was unnecessary; secondly, that the estoppel could be used as a cause of action; and finally, the determining criterion for the exercise of the jurisdiction is "unconscionability".

  1113. Priestly JA in the Austotel Pty Ltd case, supra, summarised the modern statement of the principle when he said at p.610 -

    1114. "For equitable estoppel to operate there must be the creation or encouragement by the defendant in the plaintiff of an assumption that a contract will come into existence or a promise be performed or an interest granted to the plaintiff by the defendant, and reliance on that by the plaintiff, in the circumstances where departure from the assumption by the defendant will be unconscionable."

  1114. PCA has the burden of establishing the constituent elements of the estoppel.

  1115. The plea of estoppel relied upon by PCA raises the following four matters for consideration and determination by the court -

    1116. (i) At the time of each alleged hedge contract did PCA believe that the contract was binding without the execution and exchange of a confirmation document?

    (ii) Did PP make a representation or do something or fail to do something, which created an assumption that a binding contract came into existence prior to the execution and exchange of the confirmation document?

  1116. Any representation or any act or omission to act which leads to an assumed state of affairs must clearly and unequivocally result in the alleged assumed state of affairs. If the result is ambiguity or uncertainty or multiple states of affairs the estoppel fails. See Legione v Hateley [1983] HCA 11; (1983) 152 CLR 406 at 455-7and Woodhouse AC Israel Cocoa Ltd SA v Nigerian Produce Marketing Co Ltd (1972) AC 741 at 757.

  1117. The assumed state of affairs may be founded upon a representation of fact or mixed fact and law and arguably purely on a legal state of affairs. I have already discussed this issue with respect to the plea by PP of estoppel by convention.

    1118. (iii) Did PCA rely thereafter on the assumed state of affairs and do or not do anything as a result?

  1118. This is the reliance factor and PCA must demonstrate that it did rely upon the assumed state of affairs and that it was reasonable in all the circumstances to do so. Whether or not it was reasonable to do so will depend upon all the circumstances of the case including factors such as PCA's knowledge, bargaining position and the circumstances in which the alleged representation was made or assumed state of affairs arose.

  1119. Evidence that PCA would have acted in much the same fashion irrespective of its state of belief is usually telling evidence that there was no reliance.

  1120. It is important in considering the question of reliance to take into account the context in which a representation is made or an assumed state of affairs is created.

    1121. (iv) Would it be unconscionable to allow PP to deny the binding nature of the contract until the confirmation was executed and exchanged?

  1121. This involves consideration of the detriment which would flow from the desertion from the assumed state of affairs and the conduct of PP. In Commonwealth v Clark [1994] VicRp 66; (1994) 2 VR 333 Ormiston J said at p.377 -

    1122. "I would concede that a reason for insisting upon proof of detriment is the need to ensure that detriment results from acts or inaction in reliance on the assumption and to exclude proof of detriment flowing merely from the breach of the promise or departure from the assumption. ....... In my opinion what is essential to the establishment of equitable estoppel is proof of acts or circumstances flowing from reliance on the alleged assumption by the party claiming an estoppel, which are likely to produce material detriment to that party if departure from the assumption is permitted."

  1122. His Honour concluded at p.379 by saying

    1123. "But the critical factor in each case is proof that the party relying on an estoppel has acted, or failed to act in reliance on the relevant assumption. Then, either with or without further evidence it should be possible to determine what detriment is likely to flow. Having assessed the potential detriment the question will remain whether it would be unconscionable to allow the other party to depart from the assumed state of affairs or promise or whether some other course should be adopted."

  1123. On the question of proof his Honour stated that acts, facts and circumstances must be established from which it can be inferred -

    1124. "(1) Detriment to the party claiming estoppel is more likely than not to occur if the other party is permitted to depart from the assumption relied upon; and (2) That detriment of that kind will derive from the first party's proven acts or inaction in reliance on the assumption." See at p.380.

  1124. The test as to unconscionability has been expressed differently by members of the High Court in Verwayen's case, but the judgments recognise the creation of an equity which arises by reason of the assumption of affairs and the reliance upon it to conduct one's own affairs. Deane J in Commonwealth v Verwayen (1990) 170 CLR 394 at 436 said that the equity created is "to preclude departure by the other party from the assumed state of affairs if departure would, in all the circumstances, be unconscionable." In other words what do the requirements of good conscience demand in all the circumstances?

  1125. Deane J went on to say at p.445 -

    1126. "Ultimately however the question whether departure from the assumption would be unconscionable must be resolved not by reference to some preconceived formula framed to service a universal yardstick but by reference to all the circumstances of the case, including the reasonableness of the conduct of the other party in acting upon the assumption and the nature and extent of the detriment which he would sustain by acting upon the assumption if departure from the assumed state of affairs were permitted."

  1126. A fact relevant to this question is whether the other party intended or clearly knew that the other party would be induced by his conduct to act on the assumption.

  1127. PCA called evidence that it relied upon the contracts made with PP as hedge contracts to support retail contracts for the distribution of electricity and also with respect to on-selling the commodity derivative transactions to others in the wholesale market.

  1128. PCA prepared and presented its case to show not only that it relied on the hedge contracts but also on offers which had been made and were still on the table. Cross-examining counsel quickly established that the link between retail contracts and the offers could have been withdrawn. The reliance on offers was abandoned in final address. Hence much of the evidence is irrelevant.

  1129. The hedge contracts relied upon by PCA can be divided into two broad categories, namely, the disputed contracts entered into in November 1997 which number six and the five disputed contracts entered into on 29 May 1998.

  1130. I have found that PP unequivocally repudiated the agreements made on 29 May 1998 on 10 June 1998.

  1131. PCA submit that the reality is that the May contracts were in fact transacted in three separate deals on 8 April, 20 May and 29 May. But the contracts were in fact concluded on 29 May 1999. The offers could have been withdrawn or varied or re-negotiated. In my opinion the question of estoppel with respect to the second category of contracts has to be considered during the period between 29 May to 10 June 1996.

  1132. As a general proposition once a party is disabused of its state of belief then it could hardly be said that thereafter it relied upon any representation or assumed state of its affairs the deserting of which would be to its detriment and accordingly would be unable to prove that it was unconscionable for the other party to resile from that position thereafter.

  1133. One of the difficulties facing PCA in the presentation of evidence in this case arose out of the pleading which asserted a reliance not only on completed contracts but also on offers that had been made. However, in presenting the case no distinction was drawn between offers made and contracts concluded. The evidence showed that in determining the retail price for the sales team to use, the offers made in respect of proposed hedge contracts were used.

  1134. I have no doubt that within PCA's organisation it was confident that offers that had been made by PP would be accepted but nevertheless as a matter of law - and this was understood by PCA - the offers could have been withdrawn at any stage. Once PP had made it clear on 10 June 1998 it repudiated the contracts of 29 May 1998 PCA could not rely on those hedge contracts thereafter as hedges in respect of retail electricity contracts. If it did then it did so at its own risk.

  1135. Of the 994 retail contracts alleged to have relied on the hedge contracts made on 29 May 1999, only 80 were retail contracts concluded in the 12 days' period.

  1136. The evidence revealed that PCA established products from time to time with prices for specified periods. However, it is clear that the pricing model was based on hedge offers and contracts from a number of sources including PP and also other data. Some products were said to rely solely on PP's offers and hedges but doubts arose in cross-examination. Further, in attempting to establish the link in pricing between the product established and the particular hedge, it became apparent that PP's price for hedge offers and contracts was different to the retail price calculated by PCA raising doubts about the reliance on PP's contract.

  1137. The evidence of reliance suffers from inconsistency between hedge prices and retail product prices, the fact that the pricing product was produced prior to the hedge contracts made 29 May, the fact that offers were being used and that other hedge offers and contracts were available from other counterparties which were also being used to establish the product price within PCA.

  1138. I am not satisfied that during the 12 days' period PCA entered into retail contracts solely based on the PP hedge contracts made on 29 May. The evidence suggests that the products were produced prior to 29 May. More importantly, I am not persuaded by PCA that during this period it would have not traded in the retail market if it had known that the contract was not binding until confirmation. In my opinion PCA would have entered into the retail contracts in this period irrespective of whether it had hedge contracts with PP. During this period Mr McGarry admitted, retail prices were less than wholesale prices and it was difficult and competitive to do business in the retail market at this point in time.

  1139. PCA failed in proving reliance and detriment in respect of the contracts made 29 May 1999 and accordingly failed to establish estoppel to preclude PP from asserting that no contract came into being until the confirmation document was signed and exchanged in respect to the contracts made on 29 May 1998.

  1140. This brings me back to the first group of agreements. As I have already said, the witnesses called by PCA to prove this part of their case were given a task which was very difficult because a lot of the evidence showed PCA relied upon offers rather than concluded contracts.

  1141. As a result, both of the main witnesses, being Messrs Telford and McGarry gave unsatisfactory evidence and were often placed in an impossible position to maintain that PCA had relied upon offers that could have been withdrawn in order to establish the actual product.

  1142. The first question concerns PCA's belief that a contract was a binding one prior to the execution and exchange of the confirmation document. The relevant dates are 13 November 1997 in respect of hedge contract Nos. 33 and 35 and 28 November 1997 in respect of contract Nos. 38-41 (inclusive).

  1143. In my opinion the evidence establishes that PCA did believe that the contract was binding once made and prior to exchange of the signed confirmation document.

  1144. In support of this finding I refer to evidence that Mr Treacy in the correspondence in November 1997 uses the language of contract, he responded to offers from Mr Murphy prior to the validity date expiring and the trade ticket system established within PCA which recorded it had concluded the transaction. The trade ticket system was based upon the trade date which invariably corresponded to the date when Mr Treacy believed that he had contracted. Mrs Sim prepared schedules of contracts that were made in November on 3 December 1997. In addition, the conduct of the parties prior to 13 November 1997 with respect to contracts commencing prior to confirmations being exchanged and settlements taking place after performance had commenced, strongly supports the assumed state of affairs.

  1145. In relation to the second question whether PP represented a fact or played some part in the establishment of the state of affairs, I am satisfied it did. The course of dealing between the parties prior to 13 November 1997 established that in respect to a large number of the 32 undisputed contracts, the contracts were commenced prior to the exchange of the confirmation documents.

  1146. This brings me to the third question.

  1147. It is important in considering the issues of reliance, detriment and unconscionability to bear in mind the core business of PCA was to sell electricity to retail consumers. It competed for the big customers with other suppliers and the number of consumers who PCA could do business with, was increasing. On any view, during 1997 and 1998 competition between electricity suppliers was fierce and in order to contract with the big consumer it was necessary to offer very competitive terms. Its business was selling electricity.

  1148. I did raise the question with PCA's Mr McGarry, what came first, the hedge or retail contract? The answer seemed to be that whilst it is preferable to finalise both types of contract simultaneously where the retail customer is a large customer, the commercial reality is that it is more important as the supplier of electricity to sign up the big consumer. Having said that a hedge contract is important to minimise a risk, especially in long term contracts but the fact is that there is only one potential large customer and many counter-parties available to transact hedge agreements. PCA's focus was on the retail business.

  1149. The evidence relating to reliance, detriment and unconscionability was given on behalf of PCA by Messrs McGarry and Telford and in affidavit form by Andrew John Reid who exhibited three schedules. He was not cross-examined.

  1150. Clearly much thought and preparation went into the presentation of evidence.

  1151. Proof of estoppel requires an identification of a disputed hedge contract and reliance on that hedge contract to either conclude retail contracts for the supply of electricity or the on-sale of the subject matter or a hedge of portion of it in the exempt futures market to another wholesaler.

  1152. The evidence was all in the camp of PCA. If there was a reliance it would not be difficult to produce evidence to support it. I agree with the submission of Mr Maloney on behalf of PP that the evidence was unsatisfactory, vague and unconvincing.

  1153. Part of the problem was that PCA in establishing its retail product relied upon offers and pattern of trading as well as concluded contracts.

  1154. PCA was confident that a hedge contract would be concluded based on offers made.

  1155. But the reality was, offers could be withdrawn at any stage, varied or re-negotiated. Once a concession was made in final address that PCA's case rested on concluded contracts much of the evidence that had been called was of little value.

  1156. The evidence led to the conclusion that PCA was concerned to sell electricity than wait for a concluded hedge contract. It traded in the main comforted by the fact that PP had made offers which PCA expected would end up as satisfactory hedge contracts. However, PP's contribution to the total hedge contract port folio of PCA was about 30%. This meant that PCA entered into commodity derivative contracts through other counter-parties. This is a factor of some importance when considering the question of reliance and detriment.

  1157. Mr McGarry, who holds an economics degree majoring in econometrics, was employed by PCA as the principal pricing analyst reporting directly to Mr Duvall. His job was concerned primarily with the retail side of PCA's business and he supported a retail sales team of about 40 persons to whom he gave information concerning prices and volumes to enable them to sell the retail electricity.

  1158. PCA held a formal strategy group meeting each Tuesday comprising Messrs Johnson, Telford, Daley, Treacy, Duvall and Dr Nelson. In addition, Mr McGarry held informal meetings with Mr Treacy and others concerning wholesale and retail prices.

  1159. Mr McGarry prepared from information that he obtained from various sources the price analysis model and Mr Treacy was the major source of the wholesale price information that he used. This model was to provide information to the retail staff. In addition, Mr McGarry obtained information from the retail staff which he passed on to Mr Treacy.

  1160. One of the purposes of the strategy meeting was to determine the prices that would be offered to customers.

  1161. Mr McGarry during the material period established a product which set out the prices, volumes and any particular terms based upon a hedge contract. This would be made available to the retail staff. He would make up a file code if a new product was required. The necessity from time to time to establish a new product came about for a whole host of reasons, the main ones being that there has been a change in prices in the market place and the risk position of PCA may have changed.

  1162. It was important to provide to the retail staff flexibility with respect to long term deals. Mr McGarry stated that in late 1997 customers were advised by the PCA retail sales team that it would be a good time to think about long term deals because of the prices at that stage.

  1163. It was PCA's case that the November hedge contracts were used for retail sales and also for on-selling as hedge contracts. Mr Telford dealt with the on-selling.

  1164. Mr McGarry gave evidence by reference to his products that he created from time to time. He would establish a product which would be then made available to the retail team. By way of example he created a product known as 711GP which was a product created in November 1997 (hence the 711) and was for generic pricing, hence the use of the letters "GP". This product had a four year span and the prices for peak, shoulder and off-peak were set out. He gave evidence that he would determine the prices by reference to offers and hedge contracts.

  1165. His cross-examination revealed that many of his products were based upon offers that were made by PP and not concluded contracts. Further, it was demonstrated that his prices did not necessarily match the offers that had been made. Some of the retail products appear to have been based upon other hedge contracts.

  1166. Schedule A1, which is Exhibit AR1 to the affidavit of Mr Reid, sets out 162 alleged retail contracts relied upon in respect of the 28 November contracts. The schedule reveals that of the 162 retail contracts only 25 retail contracts were actually concluded after 28 November and prior to 11 June 1997. Between 11 June and 27 June four more contracts were concluded so that a total of 29 contracts were concluded after 28 November 1997 and prior to the date of repudiation.

  1167. Hedge contract 33 is alleged to have supported two retail transactions, namely, Visyboard and Air Liquide. The hedge contract involved a substantial volume of energy and approximately half the volume was to support the Air Liquide retail contract.

  1168. With respect to the Visy portion the evidence showed that PCA negotiated an agreement with Visy and reached a point in March 1997 that they had an agreement in principle. At that stage PCA was in the early throes of negotiating the hedge contract with PP in relation to Visy. By October 1997 there was a dispute between PCA and Visy as to whether there was a binding contract, Visy asserting there was and PCA denying it. No final contract was entered into between Visy and PCA until early 1999. The evidence revealed that PCA was providing Visy with electricity and had been doing so from about March 1997 pursuant to a short term agreement.

  1169. I reject the evidence of PCA that its retail contract with Visy relied upon the 13 November 1997 hedge contract with PP. By the time the retail contract was signed PP had repudiated the hedge agreements. PCA proceeded with the retail contract. The evidence leads to the conclusion that irrespective of whether there was a hedge or not a hedge contract PCA intended to enter into a retail contract with Visy. Further, even if PCA had not entered into a hedge contract with PP in my opinion it would have still proceeded with the transaction and if necessary would have obtained hedge support elsewhere. The fact was the Visy retail contract was not concluded until well after the hedge contract was repudiated on 26 June 1998.

  1170. With respect to Air Liquide the evidence shows that as at 29 May 1997 the parties had negotiated a retail contract which was "subject to contract and board approval". The terms in that agreement in principle are identical to those contained in the ultimate contract which was signed in late December 1997, at least for the relevant period. In my opinion the evidence leads to the conclusion that irrespective of whether there had been a hedge contract with PP, PCA was determined to enter into a contract with Air Liquide to supply retail electricity.

  1171. In the circumstances PCA has failed to prove reliance, detriment or lack of conscionability in PP refusing to be bound by contract No. 33 of 13 November 1997.

  1172. The Smorgon retail contract was finally agreed between PCA on 21 November 1997. This was eight days after the hedge contract No. 35 was concluded.

  1173. Prior to August 1997 PCA and Smorgon had been negotiating an agreement for some months and by mid August 1997 the parties had practically reached agreement. On 28 August 1997 Mr Treacy informed Mr Murphy that the PCA sales team believed that the contract would be resolved shortly.

  1174. On 13 November Mr Treacy informed Mr Murphy that the Smorgon contract had been approved by the board and PCA wished to enter into the hedge contract.

  1175. In my opinion, again, PCA fails to prove reliance and detriment in respect to this hedge contract. PCA was determined to obtain Smorgon's electricity contract. The reality is the retail contract came first, followed by the hedge.

  1176. PCA submits, however, that the court should infer that between 14 November 1997 and 26 June 1998 if PCA had known that PP was not going to honour the hedge contracts until the confirmations were signed it lost the opportunity to enter into a hedge contract elsewhere bearing in mind that the hedge contract period was to commence on 1 August 2001. Reference is made to the fact that spot prices dropped during the period down to April 1998 at which time they commenced to rise again.

  1177. I am not prepared to draw the inference that PCA would have attempted to support the retail contract by hedging elsewhere during that period on establishing reliance and detriment. There was no evidence from PCA that if it had been apprised of the refusal by PP to honour the contract, that it would have entered into a hedge contract during this period. Indeed, the evidence of falling prices and the fact that the first year of the hedge contract was not until 2001 in the absence of direct evidence from the party which has the burden of proof I would not be prepared to draw the inference that during that period it would have gone out and sought further hedge contracts if it had been told PP would not honour the hedge until the confirmation was signed. Further, there is no evidence from any source that it could not have gone out and hedged the contract after 26 June. Nor is there any evidence to suggest that if it had done so it would have suffered any detriment .

  1178. On 28 November 1997 PCA entered into six contracts with PP. They are numbered 36-41 (inclusive). There is no dispute between the parties as to the hedge contracts numbered 36 and 37.

  1179. The evidence clearly establishes that PP was aware that PCA was entering into the derivative contracts to hedge their risk in relation to the retail market.

  1180. After completing the deal PCA prepared a summary setting out the long term contracts and the allocation of the customer loads against the PP November hedges. A report was prepared for the head office in Portland, Oregon to explain what had been done and to indicate the customers provided for in respect to the hedges.

  1181. I am satisfied on the evidence that the pricing and volumes in the hedge contracts were used by Mr McGarry to prepare his products for use by the retail sales team.

  1182. Mr Maloney on behalf of PP submits that many of the products created by Mr McGarry pre-date 28 November 1997, that Mr McGarry was unable to establish the link between the prices in his products and the prices in the hedge contracts and that there were inconsistencies in his evidence. He also highlighted that there were other hedge contracts entered into by PCA and some of the retail products relied upon by PCA were created based upon other hedge contracts.

  1183. I am satisfied on the evidence that Mr McGarry did use the hedge products when he created his products for the retail sales team. However, in respect of a substantial number of those that were relied upon in evidence I am not prepared to conclude that there was the link established between the retail product and the hedge contract.

  1184. I do have some difficulty understanding the evidence of Mr McGarry due to the fact that he does not draw a distinction between products that rely upon the November 1997 contracts and those made at the end of May 1998. It is clear from his cross-examination that some of the products created in March 1998 relied upon offers that were subsequently accepted by the contracts of late May. Further, there is evidence to support the conclusion that some of the products relied upon non-disputed contracts 36 and 37.

  1185. PCA has the burden of proof. It had all the evidence in its camp. It should have placed evidence - if this was the situation - before the court which would show what retail contracts were linked back to PP's hedge contract. In a general way the evidence has been adduced to show that the hedge products were used as a basis for the retail product. I accept that. But on the other hand the bulk of the evidence shows that offers were used as a basis, non-disputed contracts 36 and 37 were also used, and that in respect of the retail products relied upon, there was real doubts about the pricing being linked back to the particular hedge contracts. In the few where one could see a linking the retail product preceded the hedge contracts.

  1186. I am not persuaded on this evidence that any detriment would be suffered by PCA if PP was permitted to deny the existence of the contracts of late November 1997 until the exchange of signed confirmations. I am not persuaded that there is any link which would lead to a detriment being suffered. I accept that there is some evidence that the information was used in relation to some retail products but there are so many other factors which intrude into the equation which leaves the court in doubt.

  1187. The same may be said about Mr Telford's exercise in relation to the on-selling of the hedge contracts. Again, no effort is made to draw a distinction between the November and May contracts. Indeed, the evidence establishes that most of the on-selling hedge contracts entered into by PCA were in relation to the May contracts. Again, the evidence is unsatisfactory and I would not be prepared to find that PCA would suffer any detriment in relation to these hedge contracts that it allegedly entered into if PP was entitled to deny the November hedge contracts until confirmation.

  1188. I do not accept the submission of PCA that one can rely upon equitable estoppel in some general way. It is necessary to establish an equity and the relief that is granted is moulded to give effect to that equity. It is impossible to say in the way the evidence is before the court what equity PCA has established. Unless there is some link between retail contracts or wholesale contracts and particular hedge contracts then it is not possible to say what detriment PCA would suffer if PP is permitted to deny the binding nature of the November contracts until confirmation.

  1189. It follows that PCA has failed to establish its plea of estoppel if the court had found that PP was not bound by the disputed contracts until the exchange of signed confirmations.

    1190. Authority

  1190. The person who negotiated the 11 disputed contracts on behalf of PP was Mr Murphy. Mr Trethewy had some involvement in relation to a few of the contracts but in the final analysis it was Mr Murphy who finalised the deals. Anything that Mr Trethewy did was clearly subject to Mr Murphy's supervision.

  1191. When he wrote his letter on 29 May 1998, in which he used a number of reasons for refusing to recognise any of the contracts made on 28 May 1998, nothing was said by Mr Murphy concerning the question of authority. Prior to this letter the parties through Messrs Treacy and Murphy had negotiated many contracts in an atmosphere of good faith, honesty and trust. This letter sent tremors through PCA. Despite its terms Mr Murphy was prepared to discuss matters on a friendly basis during the following days. However, on 10 June 1998 he sent another letter and his opening paragraph read -

    1192. "RE: Contract Status I refer to your fax of 29th May 1998. The terms and conditions of these proposed contracts have not in fact been agreed yet, nor have the details so far agreed between us been approved by anyone in Pacific Power or Powercor having the legal capacity to do so. Thus we do not yet have a contract, only a sound basis for further negotiation." (Emphasis added.)

  1192. After setting out a number of reasons why there were no contracts he concluded the letter as follows -

    1193. "Most of the above considerations also apply to the proposed contracts 38, 39, 40 and 41."

  1193. This was the first occasion on which PP had suggested that there was any lack of authority in Mr Murphy to transact dealings between the parties. Further, he raised the point that Mr Treacy on behalf of PCA did not have the "legal" capacity to approve.

  1194. It is to be noted that he makes no mention of authority to agree. He stated, "authority to approve".

  1195. PP in this proceeding has denied that Mr Murphy had any authority to transact the agreements that were made in November 1997 and May 1998. PCA disputes this contention and asserts that Mr Murphy did have the necessary authority, that in the alternative if he did not he was held out as having apparent authority and finally, he had actual or ostensible authority to communicate on behalf of PP offers and receive acceptances of offers made by PP.

  1196. PP also contends that Mr Treacy did not have authority to conclude the 11 contracts on behalf of PCA. PCA asserts he did so and in the alternative relies upon the principle of ratification.

  1197. Both parties had the necessary authority and power to enter into the 11 disputed contracts. There is no question of PCA and PP not having the authority. The issues come down to the authorities of the employees to bind the employer.

    1198. A. The Contentions

  1198. The parties are corporate legal entities. PCA is a large company wholly owned by an American parent; PP is a statutory corporation established by Act of the New South Wales Parliament.

  1199. The parties, being artificial legal entities, act through human agents. But not every employee and agent of a corporation has the authority to conclude contracts on behalf of the corporation and thereby bind it.

  1200. This was known, understood and appreciated by the personnel of each party who were involved in negotiating the contracts between the parties from the very outset of their relationship. PCA and its employees and in particular Mr Treacy were aware at all relevant times of the general proposition that those who were employed by PP may have limits on their authority. Not only did Mr Treacy accept that that was so but within PCA's organisation there were limitations on the authority of its various employees.

  1201. The statement of claim pleaded that certain personnel employed by PP including Mr Murphy had authority to perform telephone dealings on the part of PP and the same persons were authorised to confirm telephone dealings. PP admits this but pleads that the telephone dealing authorities were varied by correspondence between the parties and the exchange of trading authorities up to 11 September 1997. PP also contends that the 11 disputed contracts were not telephone dealings and hence the Telephone Dealing Supplement had no application.

  1202. PP further alleged that Mr Murphy did not have authority to agree on behalf of and bind PP in respect of the disputed contracts.

  1203. PCA's reply put in issue the question whether the written trading authorities relied on were actually provided to it by PP, alleged that Mr Murphy did have authority to contract and in the alternative pleaded that Mr Murphy had ostensible authority to bind PP. PCA also alleged that Mr Murphy had specific authority to approve and execute all contracts.

  1204. By an amendment to the reply PCA alleged Messrs Murphy and Trethewy had actual or ostensible authority to make and communicate offers and accept offers on behalf of PP.

  1205. It can be seen that whilst the question of authority was originally tied to the Telephone Dealing Supplement forming part of the Master Agreement, the issues are wider and raise questions which are not tied to the Master Agreement and the Telephone Dealing Supplement.

  1206. In simple terms the real issues concern the authority of Messrs Murphy and Treacy to contract on behalf of, and bind their employer.

  1207. The issue of authority has to be considered and determined as at 13 November 1997 being the date of the first disputed contract, and thereafter up to and including 4 June 1998.

  1208. By 13 November 1997 the parties had executed the Master Agreement, varied it, signed the Telephone Dealing Supplement appendices, exchanged written trading authorities and importantly, had negotiated 29 contracts, most of which were negotiated by Messrs Murphy and Treacy.

  1209. All these matters, including the pattern of the trading conduct, will have to be considered when deciding the authority of Messrs Murphy and Mr Treacy as at 13 November 1997 and thereafter.

    1210. B. Principles Concerning Authority

  1210. An agent is a person duly authorised to act on behalf of another. The authority of the agent may be actual or apparent (ostensible). It is important to distinguish between the two concepts.

  1211. Actual authority is entrusted to an agent where a principal has clothed the agent with authority to perform some act on his behalf. Actual authority may also arise by implication. One may add to the category of actual authority, that conferred by subsequent ratification. That is, a person purports to act on behalf of another without actual authority and the other ratifies the acts subsequent to the performance with the result that authority is retrospectively conferred upon the agent back to the date of so acting.

  1212. On the other hand, apparent authority (also known as ostensible authority) is established when a principal holds out the agent as having authority and the person dealing with the principal relies on that person's apparent authority. The foundation of apparent authority is estoppel.

  1213. The law recognises that an authority given carries with it all powers which are necessary and usual to enable the agent to perform his authority to full effect. Sometimes the express authority carries with it all incidental powers justified by the usage of a trade. Sometimes the agent has additional incidental powers because of the type of authority entrusted to him. In addition, the law recognises implied authority in a particular class of agents. For example, a solicitor acting under a general retainer.

  1214. PP is a statutory corporation. Its existence, powers and authorities are to be bound within the four corners of the statute which established it. In addition to the express powers and authority found in the Act there may be implied power and authority.

  1215. In Northside Developments Pty Ltd v Registrar General [1990] HCA 32; (1990) 170 CLR 146 Brennan J at 171 said -

    1216. "A company, being a corporation, is a legal fiction. Its existence capacities and activities are only such as the law attributes to it. The acts and omissions attributed to a company are perforce the acts and omissions of natural persons. A company is bound by an act done when the person who does it purports thereby to bind the company and that person is authorised to do so or the doing of the act is subsequently ratified. ... Authority for the purpose is derived either directly from the constitution of the company or from some antecedent act typically, a resolution of the governing body which is itself binding on the company."

  1216. Nevertheless authority may be established by a course of conduct.

  1217. In PP's case its constitution is the Act.

  1218. In Freeman and Lockyer v Buckhurst Park Properties (Mangal) Ltd (1964) 2 QB 480, Diplock, LJ exhaustively considered the principles of law concerning agency and in particular apparent authority.

  1219. At p.502 he said -

    1220. "It is necessary at the outset to distinguish between an 'actual' authority of an agent on the one hand, and an 'apparent' or 'ostensible' authority on the other. Actual authority and apparent authority are quite independent of one another. Generally they co-exist and coincide, but either may exist without the other and their respective scopes may be different. ....

    An 'actual' authority is a legal relationship between principal and agent created by a consensual agreement to which they alone are parties. Its scope is to be ascertained by applying ordinary principles of construction of contracts, including any proper implications from the express words used, the usages of the trade, or the course of business between the parties. To this agreement the contractor is a stranger; he may be totally ignorant of the existence of any authority on the part of the agent."

  1220. In Heley-Hutchison v Brayhead Ltd (1968) 1 QB 549 at 583, Lord Denning, MR said -

    1221. "It is there shown that actual authority may be express or implied. It is express when it is given by express words, such as when a board of directors pass a resolution which authorises two of their number to sign cheques. It is implied when it is inferred from the conduct of the parties and the circumstances of the case, such as when the board of directors appoint one of their number to be managing director. They thereby impliedly authorise him to do all such things as fall within the usual scope of that office." (Emphasis added.)

  1221. If a document is signed which authorises an agent, it is to be construed like any other contractual document and the extent of his authority is to be determined by considering all the circumstances. It is a question of fact.

  1222. The Privy Council said in Ashford Shire Council v Dependable Motors (1961) AC 336 at 349 -

    1223. "The extent of an agent's authority, if in doubt, must be determined by inference from the whole circumstances."

  1223. Some contracts of agency contain an express limitation on the agent's authority. PP contends that is the case with Messrs Murphy and Trethewy. When this is the situation, it is necessary to distinguish between the relationships of principal and agent and principal and third parties. The questions of authority may be different in each case. In the first case the true limit of authority will be marked out by the express authority limitation or instructions given to the agent. In the second case, the true limit of the agent's power to bind the principal will be the apparent authority with which the agent is invested.

  1224. But apparent authority is subject to knowledge in the contracting third party of the limitation on the agent's authority. If he knows, he cannot rely on the estoppel.

  1225. Even though the principal has expressly authorised an agent to act in a prescribed way with a limitation on his authority, nevertheless the agent may be held out as having greater authority than is the fact. It is said in those circumstances he has apparent or ostensible authority which may exceed his actual authority. The modern statement of the law can be traced back to the judgment of Diplock, LJ in the Freeman v Lockyer case supra. The judgment has been quoted often and always with approval both in Australia and in England.

  1226. See Crabtree-Vickers Pty Ltd v Australian Direct Mail Advertising and Addressing Co Pty Ltd [1975] HCA 49; (1975) 133 CLR 72 and Northside Developments Pty Ltd v Registrar-General [1990] HCA 32; (1990) 170 CLR 146 at 159-60, 172 et seq and 198-9. See Healy-Hutchisons case, supra.

  1227. In the Freeman v Lockyer case at p.503 Diplock, LJ said -

    1228. "An 'apparent' or 'ostensible' authority, on the other hand, is a legal relationship between the principal and the contractor created by a representation, made by the principal to the contractor, intended to be and in fact acted upon by the contractor, that the agent has authority to enter on behalf of the principal into a contract of a kind within the scope of the 'apparent' authority, so as to render the principal liable to perform any obligations imposed upon him by such contract. To the relationship so created the agent is a stranger."

  1228. His Lordship stated that the representation operated as an estoppel and it was irrelevant whether the agent had actual authority to enter into the contract.

  1229. Later, His Lordship said -

    1230. "The representation which creates 'apparent' authority may take a variety of forms of which the commonest is representation by conduct, that is, by permitting the agent to act in some way in the conduct of the principal's business with other persons."

  1230. His Lordship went on to underline the fact that in order to create an estoppel between the corporation and the contractor the representation as to authority which creates the apparent authority must be made by some person who has actual authority from the corporation to make such a representation.

  1231. His Lordship went on to state what the commonest form of representation by a principal was, namely, by permitting the agent to act in the management or conduct of the principal's business.

  1232. His Lordship then summarised the position as follows with respect to apparent authority the onus of which is upon the party alleging that the agent had ostensible authority -

    1233. "It must be shown:

    (1) that a representation that the agent had authority to enter on behalf of the company into the contract of the kind sought to be enforced was made to the contractor;

    (2) that such representation was made by a person or persons who had 'actual' authority to manage the business of the company either generally or in respect of those matters to which the contract relates;

    (3) that he (the contractor) was induced by such representation to enter into the contract, that is, that he in fact relied upon it; and

    (4) that under its memorandum or articles of association the company was not deprived of the capacity either to enter into the contract of the kind sought to be enforced or to delegate authority to enter into a contract of that kind to the agent."

  1233. The reference to memorandum or articles of association in point (4) is to the constitution of the corporation. In the present case the reference is to the statute which created PP and which sets out its rights, duties and powers.

  1234. It has been well established and for many years that if the contracting third party has notice that the agent is acting in violation of his authority then the rules concerning apparent authority have no operation. It is well established that a principal cannot by some secret limitation of an agent's apparent authority free himself from any liability under contracts made by the agent based on that authority, yet the rule has no operation where the contracting party has notice that the agent is acting contrary to his instructions. See Howard v Braithwaite 1 Ves and B 209, and Armagas Ltd v Mundogas S.A. [1985] UKHL 11; (1986) AC 717 at p.777 where Lord Keith said -

    1235. "Ostensible general authority can, however, never arise where the contractor knows that the agent's authority is limited so as to exclude entering into transactions of the type in question, and so cannot have relied on any contrary representation by the principal: Russo-Chines Bak v Li Yau Sam (1910) AC 174."

  1235. Lord Keith also touched on common cases of ostensible authority. He said supra at p.777 -

    1236. "Ostensible general authority may also arise where the agent has had a course of dealing with a particular contractor and the principal has acquiesced in this course of dealing and honoured transactions arising out of it."

  1236. PCA by amendment to its reply, has raised the question whether a person, even though that person cannot conclude a contract binding on his employer, can nevertheless have authority either actual or apparent to communicate an offer from his principal which may be accepted and also to accept an offer on behalf of his principal thereby bringing into existence a contract.

  1237. I drew the attention of the parties to the English Court of Appeal decision of First Energy (UK) Ltd v Hungarian International Bank Ltd (1993) 2 Ll.L.R. 194 which is authority for the proposition that an agent may be authorised to communicate both offers and acceptances on behalf of his principal thereby bringing into existence a concluded and binding contract even though he does not have actual authority to contract.

  1238. The leading judgment was delivered by Steyn, LJ.

  1239. His Lordship commenced his judgment by again reminding the profession and the commercial world that the objective of the law of contract is that the reasonable expectations of honest men must be protected and not frustrated. In enforcing and construing contracts between commercial men the court should approach the task based on the premise of giving effect to what commercial men agree.

  1240. The facts can be briefly summarised. The plaintiff specialised in the replacement of old-fashioned heating systems by a new form of space heating. In order to make the new systems financially attractive the plaintiff devised a scheme whereby it would install the heater and the owner would pay over a number of years. The plaintiff needed credit facilities and approached the defendant, the Hungarian International Bank Ltd. It is a merchant bank which specialised in commercial lending to trading companies and its head office was in London. It also had an office in Manchester and the plaintiff dealt through the Manchester office. The man at the bank who did the negotiations was Mr Jamison who was a senior manager and in charge of the Manchester office.

  1241. Discussions took place and Mr Jamison told the plaintiff that he had no authority to sanction a loan facility and indeed Mr Jamison was not held out as having any authority to sanction any particular size facility.

  1242. The defendant bank sent a facility letter to the plaintiff signed by its managing director and an assistant director. The letter set out in some detail the terms relating to the facility and the final paragraph provided that the letter did not constitute an offer but detailed the terms upon which the bank was prepared to consider making facilities available and invited the plaintiff to sign the terms if they were acceptable and to forward a cheque for £2,500. It was also stated that the signing by the defendant of one of the copies would only "then create a binding contract between us".

  1243. The plaintiff signed the facility letter and deposited a cheque for £2,500 with the bank.

  1244. Despite the fact that the facility letter did not come into force the parties nevertheless entered into a binding agreement in respect of some equipment to be installed. This involved a hire purchase arrangement and the bank proceeded with the transaction and it was performed.

  1245. Further discussions took place about other supplies and installation of heating. The facility letter was not signed and yet the parties continued to do business with respect to hire purchase agreements for the installation of the heating. Having done that the senior management of the defendant decided they were no longer interested in the plaintiff's business. The plaintiff then issued proceedings against the bank.

  1246. The trial judge found that a letter which was written on 2 August concerning the further hire purchase agreements was an offer which could be accepted. The trial judge then found that Mr Jamison had no ostensible authority to enter into the transaction but nevertheless held that he had the authority to communicate an offer by somebody within the bank who did have authority and once the offer was accepted then the defendant was bound.

  1247. Having found that the trial judge was correct in construing the letter as an offer which was accepted Steyn LJ then considered the question of ostensible authority.

  1248. Steyn, LJ said at p.201 -

    1249. "In order for a plea of apparent authority to succeed it is necessary for First Energy to establish not only that HIB through its appropriate organs held out Mr Jamison as having such authority but also that First Energy relied on that fact in entering into the transaction. In context the letter was calculated to convey to First Energy that Mr Jamison had obtained the approval of the transaction at the appropriate level at head office. The evidence of Mr Croft was that he relied on that fact. That evidence was supported by the inherent probabilities. The judge found that the element of reliance was established."

  1249. The issue on the appeal was whether the bank had held out Mr Jamison as its agent for the purpose of conveying the approval of the offer. His Lordship said -

    1250. "It is common ground that a plea of apparent authority can only be based on a holding out, or representation, as to authority of the agent by the principal sought to be held bound by the particular Act. Our law does not recognise in the context of apparent authority, the idea of a self-authorising agent ... Is it possible to narrow down the issue of simple authority which arises in the present case? A principal may clothe an agent with apparent authority in more than one way. The present case falls into a category which in Armagas Ltd v Mundogas S.A. was described by Lord Keith as follows: 'In the commonly encountered case the ostensible authority is general in character, arising when the principal has placed the agent in a position which in the outside world is generally regarded as carrying authority to enter into transactions of the kind in question.' This type of apparent authority is often described as the usual authority of an agent. But it is important to remember that the idea of usual authority is used in two senses. First, it sometimes means that the agent had implied actual authority to perform acts necessarily incidental to the performance of the agency. Secondly, it sometimes means that the principal's conduct in clothing the agent with the trappings of authority was such as to induce a third party to rely on the existence of the agency. The issue in the present case is one of usual authority in the second sense."

  1250. His Lordship stated the principle as to making a representation of fact at p.204 when he said -

    1251. "It seems to me that the law recognises that in modern commerce an agent who has no apparent authority to conclude a particular transaction may sometimes be clothed with apparent authority to make representations of fact. The level at which such apparent authority could be found to exist may vary and generalisation will be unhelpful."

  1251. As the reasoning of Steyn, LJ demonstrates it is a question of looking at the particular person, the position that he is placed in and his conduct in that position to determine whether or not the principal has held out that he would have the necessary authority to make offers on behalf of a corporation and accept offers on behalf of a corporation.

  1252. His Lordship said at p.204 -

    1253. "In agreement with the judge I have come to the conclusion, that on the particular facts of this case, that Mr Jamison's position as senior manager in Manchester was such that he was clothed with ostensible authority to communicate that head office approval had been given to the facility set out in the August 2 letter. Although his status was below that of a managing director or general manager, it was nevertheless considerable. And, in the circumstances of this case, the idea that First Energy should have checked with the managing director in London whether HIB had approved the transaction seems unreal. This factor is, of course, not decisive but is relevant to the ultimate decision."

    [My emphasis]

  1253. The other two members of the court delivered judgments agreeing with Steyn, LJ.

  1254. Although the decision has been criticised - see Bowstead on Agency 16th Ed at p.372, each case must depend on its own facts. The decision represents a commercial common sense approach which avoids frustrating the doings of honest commercial men in circumstances where it can be inferred the employee is held out as having the authority to communicate an offer or accept an offer.

  1255. Once PP raised lack of authority in Mr Murphy PCA has the burden of establishing authority. See Pole v Leask (1864) 33 LJ Ch 155 at p.162.

  1256. It is now necessary to consider the facts concerning the authority of Mr Murphy.

    1257. C. Authority of Mr Murphy

    I. Actual authority

  1257. It is appropriate at this point to make a number of observations concerning Mr Murphy's position and his role in negotiating commodity contracts on behalf of PP.

    1258. * At all times Mr Murphy occupied a very senior position at PP. In March 1996 he held the position of Manager/Pricing and Strategy and from 1 April 1997 held the position of Manager/Energy and Marketing. He headed the Energy Marketing Group.
