Those persons who traded and were responsible for administration, in the Energy Marketing Group were answerable to Mr Murphy.

    * The hierarchy at PP comprised the Board of Directors, the Chief Executive of PP who to all intents and purposes under the Act was PP, and immediately below him were a number of general managers one of whom was Dr Lang who held the position of General Manager/Development and since January 1998 the position of General Manager/Operations and Marketing. Immediately below Dr Lang's level were a number of managers one of whom was Mr Murphy.

    On any view of the evidence Mr Murphy held a senior responsible position with PP, was answerable to Dr Lang but in his day to day activities made many important decisions without reference to Dr Lang.

    * When Dr Lang was absent Mr Murphy occupied his position and physically his office. At that point it put him on the rung immediately below the Chief Executive Officer.

    * Mr Murphy at all times had considerable knowledge of, and experience in, commodity derivative trading.

    * At all relevant times he was familiar with the ISDA Master Agreement and its purpose. He served on a committee which considered its form for the Australian exempt futures trading in electricity prior to May 1996.

    * At the first meeting on 13 August 1996 between PP and PCA to explore possibilities of doing business, Mr Murphy and Mr Petch who is also a manager, attended on behalf of PP and met with Messrs Treacy and Telford of PCA. At this first meeting PP's representatives indicated

    that they wished the ISDA documentation to be entered into

    between the parties and form the basis for future dealings.

    * Thereafter Mr Murphy was the person who dealt with Mr Treacy in relation to most matters concerning their dealings from consideration of the ISDA documentation, exchange of authority mandates, questions of volume rebate agreement to negotiating commodity derivative contracts.

    * He and Mr Treacy were the two persons who negotiated most of the 35 undisputed agreements which followed the pattern of negotiations, agreement and preparation, delivery, signing and exchanging confirmations. Often the contracts commenced prior to the exchange of the signed confirmation.

    * He and Mr Treacy negotiated the 11 disputed agreements. Even though Mr Trethewy played some small part in the May contracts I am satisfied that Mr Murphy was the one who transacted and agreed the deal on behalf of PP.

    * The reality of the trading relationship between PP and PCA is that Mr Murphy was PP and Mr Treacy was PCA when transacting and agreeing to the deals.

  1258. This is the context in which Mr Murphy decided that PP would not proceed with the May contracts. When he and Dr Lang made the decision to send the letter of 10 June 1998 one of the grounds upon which PP denied the contracts was that Mr Murphy did not have the authority to approve the contracts.

  1259. The words of Steyn LJ immediately come to mind. His Lordship in First Energy (UK) Ltd v Hungarian International Bank Ltd, supra, after referring to the philosophical theme that underpins the law of contract, namely, "the reasonable expectations of reasonable men must be protected" stated that the philosophy applied in an agency situation and made the following observations -

    1260. "If we were to accept the implications which the appellants have placed on the observation in the House of Lords in Armagas Ltd v Mundogas SA [1985] UKHL 11; (1986) 1 A.C. 717, our decision would in my view frustrate the reasonable expectations of the parties. Moreover, our decision would have to be based on an unreal premise as to the way in which commercial men transact business of the particular kind involved in this case." - at p.196. (Emphasis added.)

  1260. His Lordship's words are apposite to the present matter.

  1261. What authority Mr Murphy had as a senior employee of PP depended upon his contract of employment. What was he engaged to do? What was he authorised to do in the course of his employment? What was he permitted to do or what was he prohibited from doing?

  1262. His contract of employment like any other contract can be varied not only by express agreement but by the conduct of the parties. No doubt it can be terminated and replaced by another contract again by express agreement or by the conduct of the parties. A contract of employment can be an ever-changing one as an employee is promoted or entrusted with different duties and responsibilities. Often the changes would take place without either party giving any thought to varying the contract. The fact is that by reason of the conduct of the parties an employee may be given greater and greater authority. In some circumstances the parties may enter into an entirely new agreement.

  1263. The employee is authorised to perform his duties in the course of his employment. It is implied into the contract that he has all the necessary incidental powers to enable him to perform his task completely and in a satisfactory manner. Further, an employee appointed to a particular position in the absence of any prohibition has all the authority expected of a person in that position to enable him to carry out his duties.

  1264. The relationship between the employer and employee may establish an implied authority to perform certain acts on behalf of the employer and which binds the employer.

  1265. The question of implied authority was discussed by the Court of Appeal in Healy-Hutchinson v Brayhead Ltd, supra.

  1266. In that case the chairman of a company acted as its defacto managing director. He was the chief executive who made the final decision on any matters concerning finance. He often committed the company to contract without the knowledge of the board and reported the matter afterwards. The board knew of and acquiesced in those actions.

  1267. The chairman signed two documents on the company's paper, purporting to bind the company to indemnify a person against loss.

  1268. The question arose as to whether he had authority to do so. The trial judge held that he had ostensible authority but no actual authority.

  1269. On appeal the Court of Appeal held that he had actual authority.

  1270. At p.586 Lord Wilburforce said -

    1271. "I agree of course, that Mr Richards had no actual express authority to enter into the two agreements ... But the question remains whether he had implied authority to do so. Now when one is considering whether he had implied authority, one asks first: from what is the implication to be drawn? The suggestion was made that his authority might be implied from the mere fact of his holding the office of director and Chairman of Brayhead at the relevant time. The judge dealt with that and held that, merely by virtue of his position as chairman he would not have the necessary authority to enter into these agreements. I agree with that: but the question as to implication does not stop there. I quote some words in this connection from Diplock LJ's judgment in Freeman and Lockyear v Buckhurst Park Properties(Mangal) Ltd. He says: 'An actual authority is a legal relationship between principal and agent created by a consensual agreement to which they alone are parties. Its scope is to be ascertained by applying ordinary principles of construction of contracts, including any proper implications from the express words used, the usages of the trade, or the course of business between the parties.' I think therefore that it is legitimate to go on and consider over and above the powers he had as chairman, what the actual circumstances of the relationship between him and the board of directors may show." (Emphasis added.)

  1271. His Lordship at p.487 referred to the fact that Mr Richards was allowed to deal with general financial and policy questions acting in the role of chief executive without having to consult the members of the board and the evidence shows "that Mr Richards with the consent and acquiescence of the board was allowed to act as chief executive and to make decisions relating to these financial questions." (Emphasis added.)

  1272. His Lordship also referred to the fact that evidence of Mr Richards' dealing with a particular company was also relevant to the question of his implied authority.

  1273. Lord Denning said this at p.584 -

    1274. "The judge held that Mr Richards had ostensible or apparent authority to make the contract, but I think his findings carry with it the necessary inference that he had also actual authority, such authority being implied from the circumstances that the board by their conduct over many months had acquiesced in his acting as their chief executive and committing Brayhead Ltd to contracts without the necessity of sanction from the board." (Emphasis added.)

  1274. It follows that an employer may by his conduct extend the powers of an employee by implication. Clearly if an employee performs a task on behalf of his employer who acquiesces in what he does, endorses it and gives effect to what he did, the employer is thereby authorising him to carry out that task. If this is permitted over a period of time then the clear inference is that he has that power within his employment to carry out that task. Indeed, the employee may have been expressly forbidden at some point to perform the task in question but if thereafter the employer with knowledge adopts the transaction and gives effect to it then in the absence of any contrary evidence one would be confident in drawing the inference that the employee's contract of employment has been varied authorising him to perform similar type tasks.

  1275. The course of dealing between employer and employee may establish that the employee's authority is being increased.

  1276. In considering Mr Murphy's authority it is necessary to consider his contract of employment and in particular his relationship with his employer, what he was asked to do and did, and whether his employer acquiesced in what he did.

  1277. This raises the question who was Mr Murphy's employer? Who in effect was PP? The answer to that has to be found in the Act which creates and governs PP.

  1278. As stated PP was established by the Electricity (Pacific Power) Act 1950.

  1279. By s.5 PP was established as a corporation and for the purposes of any Act is a statutory body representing the Crown.

  1280. Section 5(3) provides -

    1281. "(3) Pacific Power has such functions as are conferred or imposed on

    it by or under this or any other Act or law."

  1281. The parties did not refer me to any other Act or law which was relevant to the functions of PP and accordingly the general principle applies, namely, that the express and implied rights, powers, duties and obligations of PP are to be found within the four corners of the statute which created it.

  1282. One of the principal objectives of PP is "to be a successful business and to this end" to inter alia, "operate at least as efficiently as any comparable business". - See s.5A(1)(a).

  1283. Three attributes of an efficient commodity derivative transaction business are expedition, finality and certainty. These are put in jeopardy if a senior employee who negotiates the trade is denied the authority to conclude the business.

  1284. Section 5B established a Board of Directors which comprised the Chief Executive and at least three but not more than five part-time directors appointed by the government and another part-time director appointed on the recommendation of a committee. It is noted that all directors are part-time. This accords with the scheme of the Act leaving the management and conduct of the business to the Chief Executive who is also a board member. - See s.5F.

  1285. Section 9 sets out the functions of PP which are the generation and supply of electricity, the provision of works to generate electricity, the supply of electricity to consumers, purchasing electricity and with the approval of the Minister, any other business arising out of its core activities.

  1286. There is no dispute that PP had the power and authority to agree the 11 disputed contracts.

  1287. Whilst the Board has the function of determining policy and strategic plans for PP it is clear from the Act that the Chief Executive manages and controls Pacific Power in accordance with the said policies and plans and subject to any directions of the Minister. It is noted that anything done by him or with his authority is taken to have been done by PP.

  1288. He is in effect, PP.

  1289. This is made clear by s.5C which provides -

    1290. "(1) The affairs of Pacific Power are to be managed and controlled by the Chief Executive in accordance with the policies and strategic plans determined by the Board, but subject to any directions of the Minister.

    (2) Any act, manner or thing done in the name of, or on behalf of, Pacific Power by, or with the authority of, the Chief Executive is taken to have been done by Pacific Power."

    (Emphasis added.)

  1290. Section 5G is concerned with delegation of functions. It is relied upon by PP. It is important to observe it is concerned with delegating functions and not authority.

  1291. It provides -

    1292. "(1) Pacific Power may delegate to an authorised person any of its functions, other than this power of delegation.

    (2) The delegate may sub-delegate to an authorised person any function delegated by Pacific Power if the delegate is authorised in writing to do so by Pacific Power."

    (Emphasis added.)

  1292. "Authorised person" means, inter alia, a member of staff of Pacific Power. See s.5G(3).

  1293. PP relies on this section as being the only power given to PP to delegate authority. It submits the actual delegations which were made to employees preclude any contention that Mr Murphy had power to conclude the 11 contracts on behalf of PP. The delegations it is said were limited and given pursuant to this section.

  1294. It will be necessary to come back to this argument.

  1295. PP in its defence raised the lack of authority in Mr Murphy and accordingly has the evidentiary burden of establishing facts to support its contention. In the final analysis the ultimate burden rests on PCA to prove its contract with PP and since the issue of authority has been raised it involves PCA proving that Mr Murphy had actual authority to bind his employer.

  1296. PCA also has the burden of establishing ostensible authority.

  1297. However, in weighing up the evidence it is important to take into account that on the question of actual authority the focus is on the contractual relationship between Mr Murphy and PP and accordingly the bulk of the evidence is within the knowledge of PP. Accordingly, it behoves the court to closely scrutinise the evidence and secondly, in weighing the evidence to take into account that PCA has limited access to the evidence concerning the relationship between Mr Murphy and his employer.

  1298. Whilst the issue is concerned with the relationship between PP and its employee, the trading between PCA and PP is relevant to the issue insofar as it bears upon the issue of Mr Murphy's actual authority. What he does within the course and scope of his employment provides some evidence of his actual authority.

  1299. Mr Murphy is an employee. As such he is an agent but different to agents engaged to perform a specific task. The question of authority depends upon the contract between PP and Mr Murphy.

  1300. In a long term employment the duties and authorities of an employee are likely to change and as a result work a variation to the contractual terms. In many cases this will not be documented, not adverted to and will come about because of the conduct of the parties. The task of the court is to determine from all the relevant circumstances what authority an employee had at a particular time to perform work on behalf of, and binding, the employer.

  1301. That is the position here.

  1302. Mr Murphy's employment with PP goes back to March 1980 when he commenced as a cadet engineer. When the New South Wales State electricity market commenced on 10 May 1996 his position was Manager/Pricing and Strategy and in April 1997 he was appointed to the position of Manager/Energy Marketing. His responsibilities were the same in both positions. He was in charge of a number of employees. He was directly answerable to Dr Lang.

  1303. No evidence was placed before the court as to his terms of employment. No written contract was produced and somewhat vague evidence was given by Mr Murphy with respect to his duties based upon a document, evidently in his computer, described as "Position Description". I will return to this document later.

  1304. Dr Lang gave no evidence with respect to the terms of the employment contract between Mr Murphy and PP.

  1305. More importantly no Chief Executive was called to give evidence.

  1306. There is no direct evidence of the terms of his employment. However, there is a substantial body of evidence of what he did in the course of his employment.

  1307. It is appropriate at this stage to consider who is PP when it trades. It is a statutory body.

  1308. In my opinion it is clear from the Act that the Chief Executive is PP. This conclusion is based upon s.5F.

  1309. The Chief Executive manages and controls PP's affairs and anything done by him or with his authority is "taken to have been done by PP".

  1310. It is clear in order for PP to perform its functions it requires human agents and the Chief Executive in accordance with his statutory obligation to manage and control PP has established a work force to carry out its business activities. There is a hierarchy of employees all of whom are ultimately responsible to him. By employing a person and requiring him to perform a task for PP the Chief Executive is authorising that person to do the task for and on behalf of PP. In so performing the task the employee is PP and he derives his authority from the Chief Executive. When performing the task on behalf of PP and within the scope of his authority he binds PP.

  1311. The Chief Executive is to perform his management and control duties in accordance with the policies and strategic plans determined by the Board but there is no evidence of any policies and strategic plans of the Board which restrict the Chief Executive in the way he manages and controls PP and employs employees and requires them to carry out the functions of PP.

  1312. The Chief Executive has the power to employ a person and authorise him expressly or impliedly to do any act, matter or thing on behalf of Pacific Power. When so authorised the act matter or thing "is taken to have been done by Pacific Power". See 5F(2) of the Act.

  1313. In the absence of any contractual document between PP and Mr Murphy as to his duties and authorities it is necessary to consider all the circumstances to ascertain what his actual authority was.

  1314. As a general proposition and in the absence of any prohibition, an employee who is placed in a certain position to perform duties on behalf of his employer has all the authority in order to properly carry out his duties and all implied and incidental powers in order for him to effectively perform his task.

  1315. According to Dr Lang, from the beginning of March 1996 onwards Mr Murphy was to act as a market trader in the electricity contracts market which was to come into operation in May 1996 and to provide for future hedging of quantities of electricity. Dr Lang said

    1316. "His credentials for that position, in addition to his academic qualifications and career path with Pacific Power and its predecessors were enhanced by the fact that, to my knowledge, he had participated in working groups which developed the market rules and hammered out the policies and procedures to apply in the hedging market including use of the standard form ISDA documentation."

  1316. Mr Murphy was the manager in charge of market trading dealing with hedge contracts and he had a number of employees who were answerable to him.

  1317. By placing Mr Murphy in charge of the department the Chief Executive authorised him to do all things that were necessary to conduct that part of PP's business which was dealing in commodity derivative contracts over the counter in the exempt futures market.

  1318. Since April 1996 PP has entered into many over the counter commodity derivative contracts and it was the main part of Mr Murphy's duties to negotiate and agree the transactions on behalf of PP. Unless expressly provided it would be implied from his position, his duties and the nature of the transactions which require expedition, finality and certainty, to agree to contract on behalf of PP.

  1319. The evidence from PP as to Mr Murphy's duties and the terms of his employment is indeed sparse.

  1320. Mr Murphy gave evidence that his duties were set out in a "Position Description" document which was prepared on 26 March 1997 by which time he had been trading on behalf of PP with the authority of the Chief Executive in commodity derivative contracts for a period of approximately nine months.

  1321. By that time PP had entered into 14 contracts with PCA, a majority of which were negotiated between Mr Murphy and Mr Treacy. The trading followed a pattern of negotiation, agreement and subsequently an exchange of signed confirmation. This was in accordance with the Master Agreement procedure. Mr Murphy determined the prices and terms and made the agreement. He was not authorised to sign the confirmation and did not do so except when acting in Dr Lang's office. Often the performance of the contract commenced before the confirmation was signed. At no stage prior to 26 March 1997 was Mr Murphy's authority questioned within PP nor is there any evidence that what he did in the course of his employment, being the subject of question or criticism. The evidence demonstrates that his employer acquiesced in what he did.

  1322. The fountainhead of all authority in PP is the Chief Executive. He is responsible for employing the work force which carries out the functions of PP. Mr Murphy was employed in the senior position of manager of market trading and up to 13 November 1997 negotiated commodity derivative contracts with PCA and other counter parties which subsequently became the subject of confirmation documents under the Master Agreement. In performing his tasks he was clearly doing so with the authority of the Chief Executive. Further, the commodity derivative contracts that he negotiated were for periods greater than one month. Any possible restriction on PP's ability to trade in commodity derivative contracts confined to a certain period did not apply in 1997. There is some suggestion in a Board resolution in 1996 that there may have been a restriction.

  1323. The evidence revealed not only in relation to the undisputed contracts but also the disputed contracts that Mr Murphy made decisions about price, volume, period and terms of each derivative contract, did not have to and did not consult Dr Lang in respect to these important issues and was the one who made offers and accepted counter-offers.

  1324. Dr Lang played no role in these matters. On the few occasions Mr Murphy consulted Dr Lang, Mr Murphy raised the query and sought advice. A decision would be made but it can be fairly said that Dr Lang did not in any way supervise Mr Murphy's work.

  1325. Once the agreement was reached Mr Murphy either prepared the confirmation document himself or instructed Mr Trethewy to do so. Again, no input from Dr Lang. Having prepared the confirmation document it was sent to the other counter party, including PCA for consideration and finalisation. Once the parties agreed that the document confirmed the contract reached between them Mr Murphy took the confirmation to Dr Lang to have it executed. I accept the evidence of Mr Murphy that Dr Lang did not check anything in detail, did not ask to look at any documents and left it up to Mr Murphy to ensure that the confirmation properly recorded the agreement.

  1326. In my opinion the evidence overwhelmingly leads to the conclusion that within his department Mr Murphy was supreme and made all decisions in relation to the particular transactions he was negotiating. He expressed his offers in the language of contract and he received acceptances of offers made by him.

  1327. He clearly took it upon himself to perform his duties without concerning Dr Lang or the Chief Executive. On any view the Chief Executive and Dr Lang acquiesced in what Mr Murphy was doing.

  1328. Mr Ross Bunyon was Chief Executive up to 30 June 1996 and Mr Peter Graham occupied that position until 26 February 1999. Neither was called as a witness. No explanation was given for their absence from the witness box.

  1329. The inference is that neither could give evidence to assist PP's case.

  1330. The inference which is open from the evidence and which is reinforced by the failure to call the Chief Executive Officer is that by 13 November 1997 Mr Murphy was authorised by PP to do all matters, acts and things on behalf of PP to effectively perform that part of its business which was involved with transacting commodity derivative contracts. Many of the contracts that he agreed to were in excess of one month.

  1331. Because of the nature of the transactions, the circumstances, and the fact that at no stage did a Chief Executive Officer or Dr Lang prohibit Mr Murphy from what he was doing, the inference is that as at 13 November 1997 he had the authority within his employment to contract hedge contracts in excess of one month thereby binding PP.

  1332. PP submitted that Mr Murphy did not have authority to conclude contracts exceeding one month on behalf of PP thereby binding it and referred to evidence relating to Board policies, written delegations of authority and the job "Position Description" document.

  1333. These facts must be considered in the light of the events which occurred up to 13 November 1997 and thereafter up to 29 May 1998 and with reference to the ISDA documentation.

  1334. All the evidence leads to the conclusion that Mr Murphy was permitted to do everything with respect to negotiating and transacting the contracts on behalf of PP and he did this with the knowledge and apparent authority of the Chief Executive. The latter clearly acquiesced in what he did.

  1335. PP submits that by reason of s.5G of the Act that PP could not authorise any member of staff to contract on behalf of PP unless PP delegated the authority to that person.

  1336. I reject that argument. Section 5G is concerned with PP delegating to any authorised person "any of its functions".

  1337. Section 9 sets out the main functions of PP. There is no dispute that as part of its functions PP could enter into commodity derivative contracts on the exempt futures market. The Chief Executive manages and controls the business. He is PP for the purpose of conducting that function and he authorises employees to carry out that business for PP.

  1338. If PP decided to delegate that function it would be necessary to delegate the function in accordance with the Act. But this is not the same as the Chief Executive authorising staff to perform the function. There is no question of delegation in those circumstances.

  1339. Clearly, it is a matter for the Chief Executive in managing and controlling PP's affairs to determine and authorise who should have the necessary authority to carry out the function.

  1340. Section 5G is concerned with delegating a function, not a question of authorising a person to carry out a function.

  1341. PP submitted that the court should take into account the definition of "function" in s.8A but that definition only applies to that division.

  1342. "Function" in my opinion in s.5G bears its ordinary meaning, namely, the special kind of activity which PP is empowered to perform.

  1343. I agree with the submission of Mr Myers QC that it was unnecessary for the Board of PP to become involved in any decision concerning the commodity derivative trading and that it was not necessary under the terms of the Act for the Board to authorise any person to carry out that part of PP's business. The Chief Executive clearly has power under s.5F once it is accepted that PP is entitled to engage in such business, for him to manage and control how that business was to be carried out by PP.

  1344. In other words, the Chief Executive, by reason of his statutory position, was the person who could make all decisions concerning the trading of PP in this new area. Nevertheless, as things happened the Board became involved and it is unnecessary for me to decide whether or not s.5C(1) did empower the Board to pass certain resolutions concerning the trading.

  1345. The fact was that it did. The question is what effect, if any, did the Board's actions, the delegations and sub-delegations and the job specification have upon the terms of Mr Murphy's contract of employment by 13 November 1997?

  1346. I reiterate what I have already said that because of the nature of his contract of employment Mr Murphy's terms of employment would vary as further duties were entrusted to him and by reason of the conduct of the Chief Executive and/or Dr Lang in acquiescing in what he did thereby approving his actions.

  1347. In considering the evidence it is also necessary to take into account the context in which PP commenced trading in this area and in particular the fact that it made a decision at an early stage that all its commodity derivative transactions should be governed by the ISDA Master Agreement.

  1348. In considering this evidence it is important to bear in mind that there is no direct evidence from PP that Mr Murphy was prohibited from entering into commodity derivative contracts with PCA.

  1349. The Exempt Futures Market commenced on 3 May 1996. It was in anticipation of PP trading in derivative contracts that the issue of authority arose. It arose in the context of the ISDA documentation because it is clear that PP intended that any trading would be performed subject to the documentation.

  1350. Under the ISDA Master Agreement there is a difference between contracting and recording the agreement in a document, that is the confirmation. These are two discrete steps. Agreement followed by confirmation. Different personnel could be involved in the two steps.

  1351. On 18 April 1996 Mr Bunyon, the Chief Executive, and Dr Lang signed a report to the PP Board. It was titled - "Electricity Sales Contracts for Non-Vested Quantities."

  1352. The report discussed the question of entering into what was described as contracts for differences which are a commodity derivative contract. It was recommended to the Board and resolved by the Board "that the Chief Executive or in his absence the General Manager/Development be authorised to negotiate and execute contracts with the parameters described herein".

  1353. The reference to "parameters described herein" covered the types of contracts involved which included an ISDA-type contract, contract durations which could be anything up to five years, contract size and price.

  1354. It is noted in the report to the Board the following -

    1355. "The market is expected to be extremely competitive, and it is also expected the contract negotiations will be undertaken frequently in order to manage daily changes in market position. It is therefore essential that PP be able to respond to contract proposals in a timely fashion." (Emphasis added.)

  1355. The report is stating the obvious, namely, that in negotiating commodity derivative contracts in a volatile market it is absolutely essential that contracts be concluded quickly and with finality and certainty.

  1356. On the same day, 18 April 1996, the two men also signed a report which was described as - "ELECTRICITY TRADING POLICY".

  1357. Unlike the first report this was concerned with what was described as short term contract sales and purchases. It was noted that PP in order to reduce its risk in supplying electricity might wish to purchase short term contracts and also to sell short term contracts. It is noted that all generators and retailers in New South Wales were to use the ISDA Master Agreement.

  1358. The report goes on to state -

    1359. "Opportunities for sales or requirements for purchases of short term contracts will arise at very short notice."

  1359. The Board on 24 April 1996 also resolved that -

    1360. "The Chief Executive or in his absence the General Manager/Development be authorised to approve of and execute short term contract sales and purchases as described herein, and be authorised to delegate such authority to appropriate senior officers." (Emphasis added.)

  1360. It is clear from the report and the resolution that the purpose of the resolution was to enable PP to take advantage promptly of opportunities for sales and purchases at very short notice in respect to short term type transactions. The authority related to short term deals. It did not purport to deal with long term deals.

  1361. Dr Lang was the General Manager/Development at the time.

  1362. The question is whether either of these two resolutions in any way prohibited Mr Murphy from entering into the types of contracts that he did on 13 November 1997 and 29 May 1998?

  1363. In my opinion neither resolution did so. The first resolution empowered the Chief Executive and the General Manager to "negotiate and execute contracts". The second resolution authorised the two men to "approve of and execute short term contract sales and purchases and the authority to delegate that authority to a senior officer".

  1364. There is a distinction between negotiating and executing, and approving and executing.

  1365. In the context of the ISDA documentation the approval of and execution of a contract is the confirmation step.

  1366. But there is nothing in either of the two resolutions which prohibits Mr Murphy from entering into the types of agreement that he did on behalf of PP in November 1997 and May 1998.

  1367. What the second resolution did was to authorise Dr Lang in the absence of the Chief Executive to approve short term contracts.

  1368. Side by side with these resolutions was the power in the Chief Executive pursuant to s.5F of the Act in conducting the affairs of PP to authorise any officer within PP to do an act on behalf of PP and having given the authority such act would as a result of s.5F(2) the Act, be done by PP.

  1369. On 3 May 1996 Dr Lang signed a sub-delegation which gave -

    1370. "authority to approve of and execute on behalf of Pacific Power short term contract sales and purchases for operation in the New South Wales electricity market in respect of the output of Eraring Power Station." (Emphasis added.)

  1370. One of the persons authorised was Mr Murphy.

  1371. It is noted that the authority is "to approve of and execute ... short term contract sales and purchases" and is confined to the NSW electricity market.

  1372. It says nothing about authority to make an agreement but is concerned with approval and execution. Further, it is confined to short term contracts which were for one month. This sub-delegation did not prohibit him from performing his duties which involved trading in hedge contracts which exceeded one month. That is what the Chief Executive required him to do in the course of his employment.

  1373. After representatives of PP and PCA met on 13 August 1996 negotiations took place between the parties concerning the contents and execution of the ISDA Master Agreement. Before it was executed the parties entered into a contract which was a two-way swap for a substantial supply of electricity for a period of one year on 4 November 1996 and the confirmation document was signed and exchanged on or about 8 November 1996. The contract commenced on 1 January 1997.

  1374. The contract was negotiated between Messrs Murphy and Treacy and there was no question of any authority being raised. The confirmation was signed by Dr Lang.

  1375. The parties did raise however during the course of negotiations concerning the Master Agreement the question of authority of employees to negotiate agreements and execute confirmation documents.

  1376. The schedule to the Master Agreement which was at the time the subject of negotiation between the parties contained a term which stated -

    1377. "For the purposes of s.4(a)(i) and (ii) of this agreement, each party agrees to deliver the following documents, as applicable:

    (a) ...

    (b) Other documents to be delivered are: a list of authorised signatories for the party and evidence satisfactory in form and substance to the other party of the authority of the authorised signatories of the party to execute this Agreement and any Confirmation on behalf of the party."

    (Emphasis added.)

  1377. The Master Agreement when executed contained the same term. In my opinion it obliges both parties to forward a list of authorised persons who have the authority to execute the agreement which was the ISDA Master Agreement and any confirmation document. The clause makes no mention of authority to contract.

  1378. When considering the evidence relating to exchanging authority documents in November/December 1996 it is necessary to bear in mind this provision in the Schedule and also the Telephone Dealing Supplement which set out authorised persons.

  1379. Discussions took place concerning questions of authority. On 21 November 1996 at a meeting between Messrs Treacy and Murphy, the topic of authorised signatories was raised and also the completion of the telephone appendices to the Telephone Dealing Supplement was discussed.

  1380. On 27 November 1996 Mr Treacy sent to Mr Murphy a document under cover of a facsimile in which Mr Treacy wrote -

    1381. "As requested last week we now provide details of the persons who can enter into contracts for energy trading. The original will be forwarded by post. Would you also provide reciprocal information?"

  1381. It will be necessary to return to this mandate when considering the authority of Mr Treacy.

  1382. On or about the same day Mr Murphy forwarded to Mr Treacy under cover of a facsimile what he described as trading authorities "delegated by the board of Pacific Power to the Chief Executive and the General Manager/Development together with sub-delegations."

  1383. What he sent was a photostat of the two board resolutions of 3 May 1996 which authorised the Chief Executive or in his absence the General Manager/Development to negotiate and execute contracts and authority to approve of and execute short term contracts and authority to delegate. He also sent the sub-delegation authority signed by Dr Lang on 3 May 1996.

  1384. The first resolution passed on 24 April 1996 authorised the Chief Executive or in his absence the General Manager "to negotiate and execute contracts within the parameters described herein."

  1385. As I have said, this was an unnecessary conferring of authority because the Chief Executive had the authority to conduct PP's business. But the important thing to notice is that the Chief Executive is to negotiate and execute contracts. Of course, he never did. He had a staff to carry out the PP's business. The other resolution was concerned with approval of and executing short term contract sales and the right to delegate authority. That was the authority which was sub-delegated to a number of persons, including Mr Murphy. A distinction is drawn within PP between negotiating and executing, and approving and executing. It was the latter which was sub-delegated. On the other hand the Chief Executive did not negotiate and execute contracts. This was left to Mr Murphy. That is what Mr Murphy did.

  1386. A conversation took place on 28 November 1996 between Messrs Treacy and Murphy in which questions of doing deals and what short term meant were discussed. There is some dispute about this conversation but it is unnecessary for me to resolve it because on 28 November 1996 Mr Murphy sent a facsimile to Mr Treacy in which he stated that only the Chief Executive and General Manager had authority to sign deals of any kind in Victoria and that the reference to short term in New South Wales was defined "in Pacific Power's trading policy as being one month or less."

  1387. At the end of November Mr Telford sent to Mr Murphy a draft of the Telephone Dealing Supplements completed by the parties for consideration. These Telephone Dealing Supplements which were subsequently executed set out who had the authority to do deals over the telephone.

  1388. Some time on or after 2 December 1996 Mr Murphy had a conversation with Mr Telford in which Mr Telford raised the question of the Board of PP placing limits on the authorities of traders and requested how that should be dealt with in the Telephone Dealing Supplement. Mr Murphy said there was no real scope in the document to do that but one should proceed on the basis that the mandate document covered the situation.

  1389. There is some dispute concerning this conversation. However, I am satisfied that some discussion was had along these lines. By a facsimile dated 9 December Mr Treacy requested from Mr Murphy an update on the current status of - "persons authorised to sign contracts for Vic on behalf of Pacific Power".

  1390. It was in those circumstances that the Master Agreement was executed by the parties.

  1391. A query was raised by PCA as to the restriction in relation to the sub-delegation and on 31 December 1996 Dr Lang executed another sub-delegation which removed the limitation to the New South Wales market.

  1392. On 15 August 1997 Dr Lang executed another sub-delegation in the same terms as that of 31 December 1996 but added the name of Mr Trethewy.

  1393. On 2 October 1997 the then Chief Executive, Peter Graham, executed a sub-delegation but I am satisfied on the evidence that the sub-delegation related to trading on the Sydney Futures Exchange and is not relevant to the present issue.

  1394. PP submits that the sub-delegations make it clear that Mr Murphy did not have the authority to enter into the 11 disputed contracts because the periods involved were all in excess of one month.

  1395. As against that evidence there is the substantial body of evidence of what Mr Murphy did in the course of his employment and within the scope of his authority as a general manager. That evidence leads to the conclusion that he did have actual authority to enter into contracts extending beyond one month.

  1396. The sub-delegations of 3 May 1996, 31 December 1996 and 15 August 1997 gave authority to Mr Murphy and others to "approve of and execute on behalf of Pacific Power short term contract sales and purchases".

  1397. The authority extended to "purchases" as well as "sales" and was granted in accordance with the report to the Board dated 18 April 1996. The authority is to be read in the context of that report. The authority was given to enable short term contract sales and purchases to be transacted quickly. The authority was granted to respond to that need. The authority was confined to short term. It says nothing about contracting let alone contracting long term deals.

  1398. The later authorities follow on from the 3 May 1996 one.

  1399. In my opinion the delegated written authority does not restrict Mr Murphy's authority which is entrusted to him by virtue of his office and duties within PP to conclude long term contracts on behalf of PP.

  1400. The wording of the delegations do not say so nor do the duties which he was obliged to perform demonstrate any restriction on his authority.

  1401. I am reinforced in that conclusion by the terms of the Master Agreement. This was the charter regulating the trading of the parties. PP executed the agreement. By so doing the Chief Executive authorised the staff at PP to transact business in accordance with the Master Agreement.

  1402. As I have already held, the procedure under the Master Agreement involved agreement, whether oral or in writing, followed by the recording of the agreement in a written confirmation which was to be executed by the parties.

  1403. In my opinion there is a difference between negotiating on the one hand and approving and executing the contracts on the other hand under the Master Agreement.

  1404. The sub-delegation does not concern negotiating a contract but approving and executing a contract. In my opinion this refers to the procedure after agreement has been reached.

  1405. The whole procedure of trading in commodity derivative contracts is based upon speed, finality and certainty. It is of the utmost importance that the person who does the trade has the authority to conclude the transaction. This was known and appreciated by both parties.

  1406. Once PP executed the Master Agreement which was thereafter to regulate the transactions and import terms into the transaction, PP, through the Chief Executive, authorised the officers to trade accordingly.

  1407. Mr Murphy was entrusted to trade. He did so. He agreed to the contracts.

  1408. In my opinion his terms of employment authorised him to agree to the transactions and no prohibition was placed upon him in negotiating and concluding a binding agreement in accordance with the Master Agreement.

  1409. The exchange of delegations between both parties in December 1996 and January 1997 was in accordance with the obligations under the Master Agreement. The schedule to the Master Agreement made it clear that the authorities were to relate to executing the Master Agreement and any confirmation pursuant to it - see clause 4(a)(ii).

  1410. The issue is whether as at 13 November 1997, 28 November 1997 and 29 May 1998 Mr Murphy was authorised by his employer to conclude the commodity derivative contracts which he did on behalf of PP and bind it?

  1411. I am satisfied on the whole of the evidence that the terms of his employment gave him the authority to make contracts on behalf of PP and none of the material relied upon by PP shows that he did not have the authority which one would expect he had by reason of his position, the duties he performed and what he did.

  1412. What he did was done with the knowledge and acquiescence of the Chief Executive and Dr Lang.

  1413. My conclusion is reinforced by the terms of the Telephone Dealing Supplement.

  1414. I have already held that the Supplement only relates to transactions done over the telephone. PP agreed that the type of transactions covered by the Supplement were transactions involving the NSW and Vic electricity market.

  1415. There is no restriction on Mr Murphy who is authorised, in the document as to the period of the transaction or the volume of electricity.

  1416. The Supplement contemplates the transaction being finalised, followed by a method of confirming the transaction. The failure to confirm did not in any way negate or vary the transaction. The Supplement and the Appendices prevailed over any inconsistency in the Master Agreement.

  1417. PP executed the appendix and the employee who was permitted to enter into the transaction covered - "anyone who answers when one of the above telephone numbers or dedicated lines rings, OR" and thereafter appeared three names, including Mr Murphy. The appendix goes on to set out who was authorised to confirm the transaction and again one of those was Mr Murphy.

  1418. The significance of the appendix is that there is no limitation placed upon the authority of any employee who answered a particular phone number or Mr Murphy in entering into a transaction, whatever length of time it may be or for any amount of electricity. No limit was placed on Mr Murphy's authority.

  1419. In considering the terms of Mr Murphy's employment it is relevant to take into account the fact that the Chief Executive Officer permitted Mr Murphy to enter into any transaction of any length and size over the telephone.

  1420. The trading policies of PP were reviewed by the Board on 24 April 1998 and on 7 May 1998 and it adopted the policy much in line with what it had done in April 1996. I note the reference in the report to long term and short term contracts but again the emphasis is on approval as distinct from entering into the contract. But in any event the report is contrary to what in fact happened in relation to the trading and confuses the difference between contracting and recording by confirmation.

  1421. Nothing in that report in any way contradicts what I have found were the terms of Mr Murphy's contract.

  1422. PP also relied upon a number of other matters which it was submitted showed that it was not part of his duties to agree a binding contract.

  1423. The first matter concerns Mr Murphy's "Position Description". He stated in evidence-in-chief that it was prepared on 26 March 1997 but the computer print-out shows that the document was created in August 1993 and last revised on 26 March 1997. Who actually prepared the document and what its status was, was not addressed in evidence.

  1424. One of the roles of his position was described as -

    1425. "? Negotiate the sale of electricity hedging contracts for the output of Pacific Power's generating port folio, in order to earn income for Pacific Power and to reduce the variability of the income stream. Sales should meet Pacific Power's budget insofar as possible, and must be in accordance with Pacific Power's trading policy."

  1425. There is a heading - "Accountability" and underneath that is another heading - "Independent Decisions".

  1426. Underneath those headings are three dot points which state that he can make independent decisions for prices and quantities for short term contracts and daily prices for the operation of PP's generating port folio.

  1427. Then appears a heading - "Decisions Referred for Approval".

  1428. Then appears the following -

    1429. "? Prices and quantities for long term contracts are recommended by the position and approved by either the Chief Executive or General Manager/Development. Contracts are prepared by the position and executed by either the Chief Executive or General Manager Development. Income from long term contracts is approximately $200m p.a."

  1429. This document is dated 26 March 1997 and although one is not surprised that prices and quantities should be approved the fact was that Mr Murphy never sought any approval for prices and quantities. The evidence clearly demonstrated that he did not, and that he made the decisions. No question arose as to whether or not he should have sought the approval with respect to prices and quantities. Mr Murphy took it upon himself to do so and it is clear by the conduct of the parties that what he did was accepted by the Chief Executive and Dr Lang.

  1430. There is nothing in the Position Document which constitutes a term in Mr Murphy's contract of employment restricting his authority to contract on behalf of PP. Indeed, he is authorised to negotiate the sale of electricity hedging contracts and the only requirement placed upon him was that he was to obtain approval for prices and quantities. He was not required to seek approval in relation to any other terms of such a contract. All the evidence shows that Dr Lang and the Chief Executive acquiesced in him setting the prices and quantities, agreeing terms not seeking approval and transacting the business in accordance with the Master Agreement.

  1431. PP rely upon the job analysis of Mr Trethewy and the same observations can be made. There is nothing in that document which in any way affects Mr Murphy's contract of employment.

  1432. PP relied upon a report dated 4 September 1997 prepared by Mr Trethewy and signed by Dr Lang which was placed before the Board at a meeting on 18 September 1997 in which there is a reference to the earlier delegations concerning approval. There is nothing in that report which in any way provides evidence of any restriction in the contract of employment of Mr Murphy in relation to contracting on behalf of PP.

  1433. Another matter relied upon by PP concerning the nature of Mr Murphy's contract of employment and his authority concerns an incident which occurred in May 1997.

  1434. The parties were discussing a hedge contract and the question arose as to whether PP was prepared to transact an off-peak deal at prices suggested by Mr Treacy. On 16 May 1997 Mr Murphy informed Mr Treacy that PP was not prepared to do the business, a discussion occurred about certain volumes and Mr Murphy indicated that PP would be prepared to transact at that volume. Subsequently, Mr Murphy told Mr Treacy that PP was not prepared to do the deal and withdrew the offer. This caused some consternation to PCA as the offer which Mr Murphy was prepared to make had a validity period and the offer was withdrawn before the validity period expired. Mr Murphy told Dr Lang that Mr Cornelius of PCA was unhappy that the offer had been withdrawn before the validity period had expired. Dr Lang said he would speak to him. Dr Lang stated that he spoke to Mr Graham the Chief Executive about the problem and Mr Graham said the deal could proceed subject to two provisions, one of which was that Dr Lang was to remind PCA that Mr Murphy did not have "the authority to bind PP in long term deals". Dr Lang spoke to Mr Cornelius. Mr Cornelius said that PCA had a problem because it had locked in retail customers, Dr Lang alleged he said -

    1435. "I appreciate your position. However, we are not legally bound to proceed with these arrangements because Mr Murphy doesn't have the authority to bind Pacific Power. However, I am prepared to do a deal on this occasion but only subject to provisos."

  1435. He went on to say that he emphasised to Mr Cornelius that PCA had to understand the approval processes and they were - "that only the Chief Executive and I have the authority to execute long term contracts and Mr Murphy does not". He said he repeated what Mr Graham had said.

  1436. Dr Lang said that Mr Cornelius asked him how they were to avoid the problem happening again and Dr Lang said - "In the future we will clarify our expiry dates but you have to understand our approval processes". He alleged Mr Cornelius asked him - "Does that mean that you have to approve expiry dates in the future?" Dr Lang alleged he said - "Yes, if you want a firm option open for a specific date, then that's an option that will require my authority and execution".

  1437. I have some difficulty with the last piece of evidence. The evidence of trading between Messrs Treacy and Murphy shows that Mr Murphy made the decisions as to validity periods and according to Dr Lang was authorised to withdraw the one he did in May 1997. Both before and after 19 May 1997 Mr Murphy made decisions without recourse to Dr Lang. Later on one occasion Mr Murphy agreed to a legal option ie. one where a fee was to be paid, without recourse to Dr Lang. The contract term was in excess of one month. The evidence before and after the alleged statement by Dr Lang contradicts the necessity for him to have said what he alleges he said. I do not accept that part of his evidence.

  1438. In considering this evidence it is necessary to bear in mind the Master Agreement procedure. This involved a binding contract followed by a confirmation. The evidence shows that Dr Lang and the Chief Executive were the ones who were to sign the confirmations. That is the approval process.

  1439. The alleged conversation concerns "authority to execute long term contracts". The phrase is ambiguous. In the light of the Master Agreement it means the confirmation process and not authority to contract.

  1440. This evidence has to be compared with the job description of Mr Murphy dated 26 March 1997 which did not limit him in the way as stated by Dr Lang and the Master Agreement procedure which does not limit Mr Murphy's authority. Further, the real issue in question in May 1997 did not concern Mr Murphy's authority to do anything but concerned the withdrawal of an offer before the end of the validity period.

  1441. The evidence both pre and post this incident demonstrated a trading relationship based upon good faith and trust. Validity periods were always stated by PP and were often extended. Even though on one view PP was not legally bound to not withdraw the offers the fact is that in practice it never did.

  1442. However, in any event I am not prepared to accept the versions of Mr Murphy or Dr Lang insofar as either says the question of Mr Murphy's authority to actually agree was discussed either within PP or between PP and PCA.

  1443. Dr Lang was cross-examined at some length on this alleged conversation and the circumstances concerning the issues.

  1444. I reject the evidence for a number of reasons. First, because of the way Mr Murphy and Dr Lang prepared their evidence, much of which was reconstructed based upon documents. There is no document or note which addresses the issue of authority being raised or discussed.

  1445. Secondly, I was not impressed by the demeanour of either witness when cross-examined on the matters. The evidence was far from persuasive. Thirdly, Dr Lang is a meticulous note taker but no mention is made of the issue authority in his diary notes. Fourthly, the real issue between the parties was the withdrawal of an offer. Dr Lang said Mr Murphy had the right to do this. Clearly he had the authority to fix validity periods. He had in the past and the later evidence showed he did thereafter. The real point of contention was whether as between PCA and PP an offer could be withdrawn prior to the expiration of the validity period. Dr Lang's diary noted - "In future will clarify expiry dates and processes". The topic did not involve authority to contract.

  1446. The evidence pre and post 19 May 1997 shows that Mr Murphy traded in the same way.

  1447. Finally, the alleged conversation involving Mr Graham was not included in Dr Lang's witness statement. No credible explanation was given for its omission.

  1448. Mr Cornelius was not called as a witness and accordingly there is no direct evidence denying what Dr Lang alleged was said in the conversation. Dr Lang's notes do not refer to the question at all. There is no correspondence that supports Dr Lang. I accept Mr Treacy's evidence that Mr Murphy did not raise the topic with him. The clear focus was on withdrawing the offer and whether PP should be allowed to do so.

  1449. Whilst Mr Treacy did not have any memory of any authority question being raised despite references in documentation thereafter, I am satisfied that the issue was in relation to withdrawing the offer. The authorisation matter raised in documents related to Mr Trethewy and the question of his authorisation.

  1450. I do not accept the versions of Dr Lang or Mr Murphy in which they say that they raised with Messrs Cornelius and Treacy the authority of Mr Murphy.

  1451. PP places weight on the fact that Mr Cornelius was not called as a witness. No explanation was given for his absence although it is noted he now resides overseas. The failure to call a witness does not supply evidence. I do not accept the evidence of Dr Lang or Mr Murphy insofar as they state the question of Mr Murphy's authority to contract was raised.

  1452. I find that Mr Murphy had the authority to transact and agree the 11 disputed contracts and by doing so had the authority to bind his employer PP.

    1453. Ostensible authority

  1453. If I am wrong on the question of actual authority, PCA contends that PP held out Mr Murphy as having ostensible authority to contract and bind PP.

  1454. In Freeman and Lockyer v Buckhurst Park Properties (Mangal) Ltd, supra, Diplock LJ at pp.505-06 summarised the four conditions that had to be fulfilled in order to establish apparent authority.

  1455. The first condition concerned the representation that the agent had authority to enter into the contract. His Lordship said at p.506 -

    1456. "In each of the relevant cases the representation relied upon as creating the 'apparent' authority of the agent was by conduct in permitting the agent to act in the management and conduct of part of the business of the company."

  1456. The second condition concerns the question of the person who had actual authority making the representation.

  1457. One goes back to s.5F of the Act. As I have said the fountainhead of power is in the Chief Executive.

  1458. The next step is the Master Agreement. The parties agree that it will be a charter for their negotiations. It provides for agreement, written or oral, and the recording of the agreement by confirmation. The parties discuss authority. Documents are exchanged relating to approval. The Telephone Dealing Supplement appendices are signed. No limit is placed on Mr Murphy in the latter document.

  1459. It must be inferred that all those steps were with the authority of the Chief Executive.

  1460. Mr Murphy was occupying a very senior management position. It was his duty to negotiate the commodity derivative contracts. He did so. He transacted a number of dealings on behalf of PP which subsequently were recorded in confirmations, signed and exchanged. The parties settled on the contracts each month.

  1461. Whilst from time to time Mr Murphy stated that he had to think about a proposition, or had to refer it elsewhere, the fact was that in most cases he transacted the business without reference to anybody and did not suggest that he was required to do so.

  1462. The confirmations were executed by Dr Lang and on occasions by Mr Murphy.

  1463. I am satisfied that PP did hold out Mr Murphy as having the authority to conclude the 11 disputed contracts and that the representation that he had such authority was made by or on behalf of the Chief Executive by placing Mr Murphy in the position he was consequent upon the Master Agreement being executed.

  1464. I am satisfied that PCA was induced by such a representation to enter into each contract. In other words, PCA did rely upon Mr Murphy having the necessary authority to transact the business and that there was nothing in the constitution of PP which precluded it from entering into such contracts.

  1465. PP submits that PCA fails to prove apparent authority by reason of the fact that PCA was aware that he had limited authority.

  1466. I do not agree.

  1467. The starting point is the Master Agreement and the sub-delegations which were exchanged. Under the Master Agreement there is a two step procedure, contract and recording. The sub-delegations addressed the second point. Further, the Telephone Dealing Supplement gave unlimited authority. I do not accept the submission that the sub-delegations limited Mr Murphy's authority to contract. They were concerned with approval and execution of the confirmation. I have already rejected the evidence of Dr Lang and Mr Murphy concerning the May 1997 incident insofar as they state the topic was raised that Mr Murphy did not have the necessary authority. But in any event the subsequent events up to 13 November 1997 have to be considered and they show that in this period he was held out as having the necessary authority. I have closely considered all the evidence and the documentation but I am not persuaded that the evidence shows that PCA was made aware that Mr Murphy could not enter into a contract greater than one month. The Master Agreement is to the contrary.

  1468. PCA also rely upon the option agreement concerning Alcoa/SECV In this contract an option fee was paid. And then followed a letter from Dr Lang. Mr Murphy made the agreement without reference to anybody. He did not for one minute indicate to Mr Treacy that he was not authorised to make the option agreement. The agreement was greater than one month. I accept Mr Treacy's evidence in relation to the conversations with Mr Murphy. The documentary evidence reveals that on 27 August 1997 Mr Murphy made an offer in the form of an option in relation to what was a very large contract and on the same day Mr Treacy accepted the offer and said he would forward a cheque for payment of the option fee. I accept the evidence of Mr Treacy that he did not request any letter from PP in relation to this matter. Further the letter written by Dr Lang on 28 August 1997 is an acknowledgment of an agreement already made.

  1469. Mr Murphy alleges that on 28 August 1997 a telephone conversation took place between he and Mr Treacy wherein Mr Treacy is alleged to have raised the question of the authority of Mr Murphy to agree. It is said that Mr Treacy requested a letter from Dr Lang because of the alleged lack of authority. Mr Treacy denies this conversation. Mr Murphy was cross-examined on this matter. In my view he has reconstructed what he thinks happened.

  1470. This is the only letter in which Dr Lang has acknowledged the acceptance of an offer despite all the other contracts entered into between the parties. This was the first of the very large contracts and I do not accept the evidence of either Mr Murphy or Dr Lang that this was written because of the concern about authority.

  1471. I do not accept that in the context of the Master Agreement which provided for agreement followed by confirmation and the trading between the parties and the wording of the sub-delegations which were handed over in any way lead to knowledge within PCA that Mr Murphy was not authorised to conclude the disputed contracts.

  1472. In my opinion PCA has established ostensible authority that PP held out Mr Murphy as having and PP is estopped from denying that he had the authority to contract the 11 disputed contracts.

    1473. Authority to communicate offers and acceptances

  1473. The case of First Energy (UK) Ltd v Hungarian International Bank Ltd, supra, is an example of a party to a contract being bound by an employee having the authority to communicate offers and accept offers.

  1474. The Master Agreement provided for agreement once the terms had been agreed. Confirmation was the recording of the agreement. In my opinion Mr Murphy had actual authority to communicate offers and acceptances. His job description in March 1997 authorised him to negotiate hedge contracts and required him to seek approval in relation to prices and quantities. The evidence shows that he did not seek such approval. Despite his failure to do so agreements were transacted and confirmations signed by Dr Lang. The senior personnel whose job was to supervise Mr Murphy acquiesced in what he did. That is Dr Lang and through him the Chief Executive. That is an inference that is clearly open. The Chief Executive was not called to give evidence.

  1475. PP executed the Master Agreement. Accordingly, trade was to be done in accordance with its terms. Mr Murphy made offers. He was authorised to do so. He was authorised to accept counter-offers. Once these steps were put in place then a contract came into being. That is what happened.

  1476. Further, in my opinion PCA has established ostensible authority to make and accept offers. Mr Murphy was held out as such. Offers and acceptances were made and contracts came into being.

  1477. If I am wrong in relation to actual authority to contract and ostensible authority to contract then in my opinion there was actual and ostensible authority to accept offers made and to make offers. A contract came into being when the terms were agreed.

    1478. Authority of Mr Treacy

  1478. PP in its defence contends that Mr Treacy did not have authority to bind PCA in relation to the 11 disputed contracts.

  1479. According to the particulars to paragraph 6D of the amended defence, PP relies upon two letters dated 1 August 1997 and 18 November 1997 and alleges that Chris Treacy was authorised to enter into energy trades with PP having a term of up to five years but no longer.

  1480. As the case proceeded the investigation by PP widened to probing the evidence as to what Pacificorp, the parent of PCA had authorised PCA to do and in particular what Mr Duvall was permitted to do.

  1481. Time was spent at trial demanding discovery of all types of documents relating to Pacificorp's directions, instructions and authorities concerning PCA, the trading policy of PCA, and Mr Duvall's position to authorise a transaction in excess of five years.

  1482. In Smith v Littlewood's Organisation Ltd [1987] UKHL 3; (1987) AC 241 at 280 Lord Gough of Chieveley described the judicial function when he said -

    1483. "The judicial function can, I believe, be epitomised as an educated reflex to facts."

  1483. He finished his judgment by stating what the courts seek to achieve which was "practical justice founded upon legal principle".

  1484. My immediate reaction to the defence that Mr Treacy was not authorised by PCA to contract the 11 disputed contracts was that it had no substance.

  1485. Let me explain why. First, the parties had over a period of some 18 months negotiated contracts, signed confirmations, performed contracts and had traded in an atmosphere of mutual trust and good faith. The expectation of both parties was to transact business to their mutual benefit. To suggest after nearly two years of trading that Mr Treacy was not authorised by his employer to conclude the 11 disputed contracts flies in the face of the commercial reality and the reasonable expectations of Messrs Murphy and Treacy. Secondly, at no stage prior to the letter of 10 June 1998 did PP ever raise the question of the authority of Mr Treacy. The tone of the letter of 10 June is one of finding excuses to renege on agreements. Thirdly, if the boot was on the other foot and PP was seeking to enforce the contracts against PCA any defence of lack of authority would be met with a fairly strongly case of apparent authority on the part of PCA holding out Mr Treacy as having the necessary authority.

  1486. The issue is whether at 13 November 1997 and following Mr Treacy had the authority and all the evidence of trading and negotiating up to that point would strongly support an argument that he was held out as having apparent authority.

  1487. Fourthly, it was clear from 10 June 1998 onwards that PCA wished to affirm the contracts which PP repudiated. That was PCA's decision. It accepted that Mr Treacy had authority.

  1488. This is very obvious from a telephone conversation had between Mr Treacy and Mr Trethewy on 12 June 1998. I also refer to the facsimile of Mr Treacy to Mr Murphy dated 15 June 1998.

  1489. The writ was issued on 21 August 1998. It seeks specific performance of the 11 disputed contracts.

  1490. On any view there is a very strong case of ratification even if Mr Treacy did not have the authority to contract on behalf of PCA.

  1491. At the very outset of this trial I did raise with Mr Downes this defence and whether it had any merit, especially bearing in mind the fact that PCA had pleaded ratification.

  1492. Having heard all the evidence and considered the submissions, my initial educated reflex to the facts remains the same. The defence has no merit.

  1493. Let me now refer to matters of legal principle.

  1494. First, the issue is whether Mr Treacy had the authority to conclude the disputed contracts on the part of PCA.

  1495. Secondly, that issue has to be considered as at the relevant dates, namely, 13 November 1997, 28 November 1997 and 29 May 1998.

  1496. Thirdly, the question of Mr Treacy's authority to bind his employer depends upon his contract of employment. As I have already stated in relation to the question of Mr Murphy's authority, contracts of employment are continually in a state of flux due to the fact that employees are from time to time requested to perform different duties and the employer accepts and adopts what the employee has done and acquiesces in the performance. These changes work a variation to the contract of employment and result in an employee obtaining more and more authority.

  1497. Another important aspect is that Mr Treacy negotiated the agreements dealt with by Mr Murphy and was the one who informed PP of the acceptance of offers. He was the one who signed the facsimiles. He was the face of PCA. But the facts were that he attended meetings presided over by Mr Duvall, questions of offers being accepted were discussed with Mr Duvall and decisions were made by Mr Duvall in consultation with Mr Treacy. Mr Treacy in turn informed PP of the decision. The fact was that Mr Duvall made the decisions and authorised Mr Treacy to conclude the deals. Mr Treacy did so.

  1498. Mr Duvall occupied an important and senior position with PCA. He was the one who ultimately gave the authority to Mr Treacy to proceed.

  1499. PP roamed over many documents, demanded that PCA discover and produce more documents and cross-examined at length witnesses of PCA, in particular Mr Duvall in respect to trading policy and authority. At times the real issue got lost in the theories and speculations. Mr Downes QC spent much time trying to show that Pacificorp, the parent body of PCA, was controlling PCA and PCA was not permitted to enter into particular types of contracts because of the trading policies of Pacificorp.

  1500. But that was not the issue that was pleaded. It was accepted by PP that PCA could enter into the disputed contracts. The issue as pleaded was whether Mr Treacy had the authority to conclude the contract, thereby binding PCA. PP contended he had no authority to conclude contracts for a period in excess of five years.

  1501. Turning to contracts 33 and 35, these had been negotiated between the parties over a long period of time. No question was raised within PCA that Mr Treacy was not permitted to negotiate contracts of the length, price and quantities in respect to these two contracts.

  1502. Mr Duvall who is employed by the parent company, Pacificorp, was sent to Australia and on 14 July 1997 took up his position as manager of energy trading. He replaced Mr David Cornelius. He was sent to Australia to set up PCA as a commodity trader. Mr Duvall managed the trading floor and was the person in charge.

  1503. Each Tuesday there was a strategy meeting which involved a number of persons, including Mr Treacy. Mr Duvall chaired those meetings, discussions took place as to trading strategies and pricing, and discussions were had and decisions made with respect to contracting.

  1504. Mr Treacy was not on a frolic of his own when negotiating the deals with Mr Murphy in 1997 and whatever he did was done with the authority of Mr Duvall.

  1505. The fact was that Mr Treacy was the man entrusted by PCA to transact the deals. His deals were done with the knowledge of Mr Duvall. Mr Duvall authorised him to go ahead with the deals. The deals were done. On any view, Mr Treacy had authority to bind PCA. Trade tickets were completed and lodged with administration very soon after contracts 33 and 35 were agreed. This was part of the system within PCA. No query was raised that Mr Treacy was not authorised to transact the deals. He completed the transactions, recorded them as concluded and reported to Mr Duvall. Senior management acquiesced in what he did.

  1506. There is no evidence that any senior executive said that Mr Treacy did not have authority. The clear implication is that he was authorised to do what he did when he did it. What he did was done with the knowledge of the senior personnel of PCA who acquiesced in what he did. The nature of his employment and what he did is powerful evidence of his authority in the absence of any evidence of prohibition.

  1507. The point is made by PP that he was only permitted to agree to contracts of five years or less.

  1508. It will be necessary to consider the mandates which were sent by PCA to PP insofar as they bear upon this question, but in the end it is a matter of the contract between PCA and Mr Treacy.

  1509. I am satisfied that he had the necessary authority to transact the contracts number 33 and 35.

  1510. This brings me to the contracts made on 28 November 1997. First of all, contracts 38 and 39 were for periods less than five years.

  1511. They fall outside the defence.

  1512. One thing is very clear and that is that the decision to proceed with these November contracts was made by Mr Duvall.

  1513. I accept his evidence that after Mr Murphy said he could not extend the offer beyond the end of November 1997 that a meeting took place involving Messrs Treacy, Daley and Duvall. After considering various matters a decision was made to proceed. I accept the evidence of Mr Duvall that he telephoned Mr Sickels, Vice President of Pacificorp at his home to discuss what was proposed and after some discussion Mr Sickels agreed that PCA should proceed.

  1514. Mr Treacy was then instructed to accept the offers which was done.

  1515. In my opinion the evidence establishes beyond doubt that Mr Duvall made the decision, he instructed Mr Treacy to communicate the acceptance and in those circumstances I am satisfied that Mr Treacy was authorised to communicate the acceptances of the offers and these acceptances constituted the contracts.

  1516. In my opinion Messrs Duvall and Treacy had the authority to bind PCA with respect to these contracts.

  1517. Turning to the contracts made on 29 May 1998, contracts 43, 44, 45 and 47 are for five year periods. They fall outside the defence.

  1518. If Mr Treacy's authority was limited to five year contracts then these were within his authority.

  1519. The only contract which was in excess of five years is contract 46.

  1520. Again, the question as at 29 May 1998 was whether the contract of employment between PCA and Mr Treacy authorised him to make the contract? The most cogent evidence of this, is what he did in the course of his employment which was accepted by his employer without demur. Two substantial contracts for long periods were the Alcoa and the BHP contracts which were in excess of five years. They were transacted in 1997. Confirmations were signed. No issue arose within PCA that Mr Treacy did not have the authority to transact the business.

  1521. The obvious reason for this was because Mr Treacy consulted with Mr Duvall and decisions were made to proceed. The fact was that negotiations took place, contracts were agreed, confirmations were signed and exchanged and within PCA no question was raised as to Mr Treacy having the authority to do what he did. PP did not raise any issue.

  1522. He had clear express authority to enter into contracts in excess of five years. This is clear from the events leading up to 29 May 1998 and I am satisfied that as at that date, namely, 29 May, he had the necessary actual authority to enter into the agreements and bind his employer.

  1523. PP's case depends to a large degree on PCA's mandates which were executed by Mr Spalding the Chief Executive and handed over to PP. These documents were exchanged in compliance with the obligations under the Master Agreement to provide the necessary authorities.

  1524. The first mandate which was signed and forwarded to PP was dated 15 November 1996 and was provided to comply with the provisions of the proposed Master Agreement.

  1525. It was signed by Mr Spalding who was Chairman and Chief Executive Officer.
