It provided that "energy trading transactions" were defined as all transactions of "up to five years" and certain persons were authorised to enter into energy trades with PP and they included Mr David Cornelius who was Manager Energy Trading, and Mr Treacy who was described as Manager Energy Purchasing (NSW). The document went on to provide that certain persons were authorised to "complete and sign a documentation confirming energy trades". The four persons named included Mr Cornelius and three very senior executives of PCA. It was noted that the same person could not undertake an energy trade and also confirm it.

  1526. The mandate responds to the Master Agreement documentation in that it recognises a difference between concluding an agreement and signing the documentation concerning the agreement.

  1527. A similar document was executed on 1 August 1997 to remove Mr Cornelius and replace him with Mr Greg Duvall who was described as - "Assistant Vice President - Energy Trading". Mr Duvall was authorised to enter into energy trades and was also authorised to sign a confirmation. A similar document was executed on 18 November 1997 to add two further persons to the trading team.

  1528. PP submits that these documents are of no effect because there is no evidence that Mr Spalding had express actual authority to execute these mandates.

  1529. I must say I am surprised by this submission because if that is so then there are no written authorities which could possibly in any way provide an argument that Mr Treacy's authority was limited. However, I reject the submission. It lacks reality. The presumption of regularity would be sufficient to found the authority. Further, Mr Spalding occupied the very senior position of Chairman and Chief Executive Officer of PCA. The issue was not raised during the trial.

  1530. The real issue does not depend upon mandates passing between PP and PCA as to authority but what the agreement was between Mr Treacy and PCA. It is said that the mandates show that Mr Duvall's authority was limited and accordingly he could not give any permission to Mr Treacy to bind PCA. I have already found that Mr Duvall sought permission of Mr Sickels and the November agreements were made after discussion with a senior person with Pacificorp.

  1531. Mr Treacy's authority depended upon his contract of employment and the facts show that PCA through various senior personnel required Mr Treacy to contract on behalf of PCA and PCA adopted what he did. I am satisfied that these mandates which passed between the parties did not mean that within PCA Mr Treacy lacked the necessary authority to convey an acceptance on behalf of PCA of PP's offers and thereby bind PCA or contract on its behalf. What was done by Mr Treacy was done with the knowledge of the senior executives of PCA and in my view there can be no question that he had the necessary authority.

  1532. PP also submitted that there were documents within PCA's possession which raise considerable doubts about the authority of either Mr Duvall or Mr Treacy to enter into the disputed contracts. These documents arguably raised an issue whether PCA could enter into the contracts in question. This was not the issue pleaded.

  1533. Reference was made to internal documents of PCA, guidelines from Pacificorp, memoranda from employees at Pacificorp, resolutions of the Board of Pacificorp and a directive from Pacificorp which imposed a limit of US$1M daily earnings at risk for Australian electricity trading. In addition, statements made in reports prepared by two firms of accountants, Arthur Andersen and Deloittes, were relied upon to found an argument that PCA's authority and hence its trader's authority were limited. In considering these statements it must not be overlooked that they are hearsay and do not bind unless adopted by PCA or represent a statement made by a PCA employee authorised to make it. The fact was that the statements in the various reports were the work of employees of the accountants.

  1534. Mr Duvall was cross-examined at length to suggest that PCA was not authorised to enter into the disputed contracts and reference was made to these various documents. The starting point was a document called an Energy Trading Risk Management Policy which was drafted for reference to PCA's Board in early 1997 but I am satisfied on the evidence that the document was never adopted by the Board.

  1535. Mr Downes QC sought to prove that the Energy Trading Risk Management Policy was in fact the trading policy of PCA and that this clearly showed that the company was not able to undertake hedge transactions in excess of five years and hence there was a limit on the traders entering into long term contracts. He referred to many other documents which tended to support this inference and he also relied on draft reports prepared by Arthur Andersen and Deloittes, accountants engaged to report on PCA's trading system. He also emphasised the fact that the disputed contracts involved large sums of money with substantial risks and therefore there had to be a limit.

  1536. I have closely considered the documents and the arguments but I am not prepared to draw the conclusion that they show that nobody within PCA could enter into contracts in excess of five years. Much of what Mr Downes sought to prove is speculation and hypothetical.

  1537. I accept the evidence of Mr Duvall which is to the effect that PCA did have the necessary authority to enter into contracts in excess of five years and it must follow that if Mr Treacy is requested to finalise a deal, that he had the necessary authority to do so.

  1538. The fact was that the commercial reality was that PCA decided to enter into these contracts and Mr Treacy was the man who was employed to finalise the deal and clearly had the authority to do so.

    1539. Ratification

  1539. If I am wrong in respect to the finding that Mr Treacy had the necessary authority to enter into the 11 disputed contracts and thereby bind PCA, in my opinion the evidence is clear that PCA ratified any lack of authority.

  1540. The evidence shows that from early June 1998 PCA was evincing the intention to proceed with the contracts. When Mr Murphy raised doubts about PP completing the contracts all the endeavours of PCA was to ensure that the contracts were enforced. This was so after the letter of 10 June which repudiated the May contracts and the letter of 26 June which made it clear that PP repudiated the 11 disputed contracts. The writ was issued on 21 August 1998 and I have no doubt by that date PCA had ratified any lack of authority.

  1541. It has been the law for many, many years that under certain conditions a contract which at the time was entered into on behalf of a principal by an agent who lacked authority may, by the subsequent conduct of the principal, be ratified by him and the contract is treated as if the principal had previously authorised it.

  1542. The authority is conferred retrospectively and the contract is considered to have been authorised at the date when the unauthorised agent concluded it on behalf of his principal.

  1543. In order to constitute ratification it must be evidenced by clear adoptive acts or by acquiescence equivalent to clear adoptive acts. It may be implied from conduct. To constitute ratification there must be full knowledge of the circumstances by the principal and an unequivocal adoption after knowledge.

  1544. By instituting a proceeding to enforce the contract that is evidence of an unequivocal adoptive act.

  1545. By PCA instituting the present proceeding it was ratifying the actions of Mr Treacy and in accordance with the principle of ratification had the retrospective effect of giving authority to Mr Treacy at the time when he concluded each of the disputed contracts.

  1546. In my opinion PCA had by the 30 June 1998 evinced an intention to proceed with the disputed contracts and at that moment ratified any lack of authority in Mr Treacy if there had been.

  1547. Mr Downes QC submits that if the other party to the contract informs the unauthorised party that it is not proceeding with the contract prior to the act of ratification, the act of ratification is of no effect. To put it another way, if the offer is withdrawn before the act of contracting is ratified then the ratification can have no effect upon the lack of authority to make the alleged contract.

  1548. In the present case the contracts had been concluded by 4 June 1998 at the latest and PP raised the question of no-one in PCA having authority in its letter of 10 June 1998.

  1549. PP repudiated the agreements by 19 June 1998.

  1550. Mr Downes QC submits the contracts were terminated prior to ratification and therefore the act of ratification was of no effect.

  1551. There is a long standing authority standing in the way of the argument of Mr Downes QC.

  1552. In Bolton Partners v Lambert (1889) 41 Ch.D 295, an offer to purchase was made by the defendant to X who was the agent of the plaintiffs but who was not authorised to make any contract of sale. The offer was accepted by X on behalf of the plaintiffs and then the defendant withdrew his offer. After the offer had been withdrawn the plaintiffs ratified the acceptance of the offer by X and brought a proceeding for specific performance.

  1553. Kekewich J at first instance said at p.301 -

    1554. "The doctrine of ratification is this, that when a principal on whose behalf a contract has been made, although it may be made in the first instance without his authority, adopts it and ratifies it, then, whether the contract is one which is for his benefit and which he is enforcing, or which is sought to be enforced against him, the ratification is referred to the date of the original contract, and the contract becomes as from its inception as binding on him as if he had been originally a party."

    His Lordship held that the plaintiffs were entitled to a decree of specific performance.

  1554. On appeal, Cotton LJ said at p.306 -

    1555. "The rule as to ratification by a principal of acts done by an assumed agent is that the ratification is thrown back to the date of the act done, and that the agent is put in the same position as if he had had authority to do the act at the time the act was done by him."

  1555. He added at p.307 -

    1556. "I think the proper view is that the acceptance by X did constitute a contract, subject to its being shown that X had authority to bind the company. If that were not shown there would be no contract on the part of the company, but when and as soon as authority was given to X to bind the company the authority was thrown back to the time when the act was done by X and prevented the defendant withdrawing his offer because it was then no longer an offer but a binding contract."

    Lord Justices Lindley and Lopes in separate judgments came to the same conclusion.

  1556. The decision of Bolton Partners v Lambert has not escaped criticism. Mr Downes QC submitted that in the light of the criticism this court should decline to follow it. Of course it is not binding on me but nevertheless represents the considered opinions of four eminent judges.

  1557. Lord Lindley in delivering the advice of the Privy Council in Fleming v Bank of New Zealand (1900) AC 577 at 587 said this -

    1558. "The above view of the case renders it unnecessary to consider any question of ratification or to dwell on the decision of Bolton Partners v Lambert and its application of the facts of this case. The decision referred to presents difficulties: and their Lordships reserve their liberty to reconsider it if on some future occasion it should become necessary to do so." (Emphasis added.)

  1558. Lord Lindley was one of the judges in the Court of Appeal decision.

  1559. It is important to note what his Lordship said. Its application to the facts in the Privy Council decision could have raised difficulties. He is not saying it is wrong.

  1560. In Davison v Vickeries' Motors Ltd (in liq) [1925] HCA 47; (1925) 37 CLR 1, Isaacs J who dissented traced the history of Bolton Partners v Lambert and its consideration by various courts and after referring to the Privy Council decision, supra, said this at p.16 -

    1561. "In my opinion those observations, especially from one of the authors of the decision, shakes it severely, and leaves the case entirely open for any superior court of the Empire subject to the revision of the Privy Council to consider the matter for itself. I cannot think it is required of this court at all events that it should blindly follow any decision of an English Court of Appeal that it believes to be wrong and more particularly a decision that the Privy Council itself has seriously doubted."

  1561. If I may say so with respect the Privy Council did not doubt the decision but stated that the decision could have presented difficulties in respect to the facts of that case.

  1562. His Honour then referred to the history subsequent to 1900 and concludes that it should not be followed.

  1563. Higgins J who also dissented said at p.31 -

    1564. "It is also unnecessary, in this case, for me to decide whether the case of Bolton Partners v Lambert is good law or not; for in that case the would be agent did clearly purport to act for the company in accepting the offer, and the ratification did relate back to the date of the unauthorised contract."

    None of the other judges made reference to it.

    The case has been referred to and considered in NM Superannuation Pty Ltd v Hughes (1991) 27 NSWLR 26 and on appeal see (1992) 29 NSWLR 653.

  1564. In considering Bolton Partners v Lambert it is important to determine what the Court of Appeal held. What was the ratio decidendi in its narrow sense, namely, what did it decide on the facts?

  1565. The facts demonstrate that both parties to the contract knew that the agent was purporting to act on behalf of a company. The contract was concluded. Later, the purchaser withdrew the offer before ratification. There was no question of any rights intervening or any prejudice suffered if the retrospective effect of the principle of ratification applied.

  1566. It was a decision based on those facts.

  1567. The learned judges in the Court of Appeal recognised that the rule as to ratification was subject to exceptions. See Cotton LJ at p.307 and also Lindley LJ at p.309 who said -

    1568. "I see no reason to take this case out of the application of the general principle as to ratification."

  1568. Difficulties will arise with respect to ratification if the interests of others are affected by the retrospective application of the doctrine.

  1569. It is well established that the time within which ratification can take place must be a reasonable time and that the principle does not apply if ratification prejudices the interests of third persons.

  1570. There are a number of examples found in the cases which establish these propositions.

  1571. The problem with the case of Bolton Partners v Lambert arises if one seeks a principle of law of general application, with the result that the retrospective effect causes prejudice.

  1572. The question arises where the application of the principle would prejudice the interests of others. Would it be unjust in the circumstances to apply the principle?

  1573. Mr Downes QC was unable to refer to any matter which showed that the application of the decision in the present case prejudiced the rights of either PP or others or caused an injustice.

  1574. The facts of the present matter are indistinguishable from the facts in Bolton Partners v Lambert. There is no question of any prejudice to the rights of others if the principle of ratification applies.

  1575. In my opinion the principle does apply which means that once PCA ratified the acts of any of its employees who did not have authority at the relevant time to contract on its behalf, the ratification operated retrospectively and at the relevant time the particular employee had the necessary authority.

  1576. PP submitted that ratification must take place within a reasonable time and ratification can never take place after the time when the contract commences. See Managers of Metropolitan Asylum Board v Kingham (1890) 6 TLR 217. In that case Fry LJ said that ratification cannot take place after the time of commencement of the contract. That is a wide proposition and one that could work an injustice. I decline to follow it. So did Parker J in Bedford Insurance Co Ltd v Institutio De Resseguros Do Brasil (1985) QB 966.

  1577. PP refers to contract 33 and contracts 43 - 47 which commenced on 1 July 1998. I do not accept that ratification cannot take place after the contract commences. Ratification operates retrospectively.

  1578. But in any event I am satisfied on the facts that it was made quite clear to PP prior to 1 July 1998 that PCA wished to proceed with the contracts made. That is ratification occurred before the contracts commenced.

  1579. It was submitted also that there must be clear communication of ratification and I accept that as a general proposition. In my opinion there was clear communication of an intention to be bound by the end of June 1998. Further, it must be pointed out that the issue then raised by PP did not raise the question of no authority to conclude a contract but only raised the question of approval by somebody in PCA as to details already agreed and in a general vague way the suggestion was that the same point was made in relation to contracts 38 - 41. The letter of 19 June 1998 only referred to Mr Murphy's lack of authority. In my opinion the facsimile of 15 June 1998 from Mr Treacy to Mr Murphy made it quite clear that PCA wished to proceed with the contracts.

  1580. In my opinion the principle of ratification applies in the present proceeding and if Mr Treacy or indeed Mr Duvall did not have the necessary authority to enter into the contracts made in November 1997 and May 1998 thereby binding PCA I find that at the latest by 30 June 1998 PCA had ratified the actions of the employees of PCA, the principle applied and accordingly lack of authority was retrospectively rectified.

    1581. Mistake

  1581. PP contends that in respect of some of the disputed contracts the term which required that the load shape be not less than a certain percentage was expressed in a way which was mistaken.

  1582. PP pleads as a defence that at the time the parties entered into contracts numbered 39 - 41 (inclusive), namely, 28 November 1997 and contracts numbered 43 - 46 (inclusive), namely, 29 May 1998 ("relevant contracts"), the parties either were labouring under a common mistake or in the alternative it was labouring under a mistake and as a result the written contracts did not accord with the true agreement made between the parties.

  1583. PP has pleaded mistake also in relation to contracts numbered 33, 35 and 47 but in final address did not contend that the terms of those contracts were affected by any mistake concerning the obligation of the load shape to meet a certain minimum.

  1584. PP claims rescission of the relevant contracts but in final address confined that relief to contracts numbered 41, 43 - 46 (inclusive).

  1585. In its counterclaim it claims rectification in respect of each of the relevant contracts.

  1586. PP alleges that there was a mistake in each of the relevant contracts in the term concerning the obligation of the load shape to meet a minimum percentage of the load.

  1587. It was a term of each of the relevant contracts that the load factor was to be -

    1588. "a minimum annual peak load factor of 38%."

  1588. Each of the disputed contracts involves a notional volume of electricity over the period of the contract.

  1589. As the price is fixed for each half hour it is necessary that the volume of notional energy to be supplied at that moment in time is also specified.

  1590. If the supply every half hour is the same throughout the supply period, then the load is described as a flat load, that is, 100% load factor.

  1591. However, the load may change during the peak period, ie 7.00 a.m. to 11.00 p.m. in Victoria, so that the volume every half hour may be different. In the normal course of events the demands during the peak hours for actual electricity do change.

  1592. Hence it was agreed between the parties that the actual load shape could be sculptured by PCA so that the specified volume during the period of supply would vary from half hour to half hour. However, PCA was not left to its own devices. The parties agreed to a formula. It was agreed that the particular load shape had to meet a certain minimum percentage of the whole. This was expressed, as I have stated, by the formula which prescribed -

    1593. "a minimum annual peak load factor of 38%."

  1593. There is no dispute between the parties as to how the load factor is to be calculated in accordance with the agreed minimum factor.

  1594. In relation to a flat load Dr Nelson gave evidence that if it was flat and the total volume for the year was 200Gwh both peak and off-peak then a flat load would translate to 200Gwh over the year, divided by the number of hours 8,760. This produces a constant energy volume of 22.83 Mgw per hour. In the case of such a contract, in every hour the energy demand (22.83 Mgwh) is applied to the contract and the floating price is calculated by reference to the prevailing pool price. The difference between the spot price and the final price will result in a payment from one party to the other.

  1595. One compares the flat load with the requirement that the minimum load factor be agreed at, say, not less than 50%. That was a term of the first undisputed contract entered into between the parties in November 1996. PCA sculptured, ie prepared the load shape to meet the agreed load factor.

  1596. The actual load shape comprises attachment 16 to Dr Nelson's witness statement.

  1597. Part 3 of the load shape is concerned with the notional quantity of electricity supplied during the week days. A reference to the document shows that for the first six hours of the day the notional quantity is 4.5MWh and the price is fixed at $19.70/MWh and then for the period from 6.00 a.m. through to 5.00 p.m. the quantity is 22MWh at a price which varies from $19.70 through to $45.30.

  1598. The notional quantity and fixed price are fixed for each half hour of the year. It is then necessary to determine whether the load as sculptured by PCA met the requirement that the minimum load factor was to be not less than 50%.

  1599. In order to determine this an agreed formula was used to do the calculation.

  1600. The formula is -

    1601. The notional volume for the year, ie 186GWh is multiplied by 1,000 to convert to MWh and this figure is divided by 24 hours by 365 days by the maximum demand.

  1601. By looking at the load shape as set out in the attachment to confirmation No. 1 one can readily see what was the maximum demand at any point in time.

  1602. Taking the example of 186GWh the calculation is as follows -

    1603. 186 x 1,000 = 0.419655 24 x 365 x 50.596MW (the maximum demand) In other words it translates at 41.96%.

  1603. PP alleges that the term included in the relevant documents did not express the true agreement between the parties which should have been -

    1604. "Minimum load factor of 38%".

  1604. It is noted that there are two words added to the term of each relevant contract, namely, "annual peak". It is the word "peak" which makes a substantial difference to the load because if the requirement is based upon "peak" this means that the formula is only concerned with peak hours instead of all hours.

  1605. There is a substantial difference to the calculation depending on whether all hours are used or only peak hours are used.

  1606. It is the contention of PP that "peak" in the term refers to peak volume and not peak hours and therefore there is no difference between the two terms but if this is wrong then there is a difference. In my opinion "peak" means "peak hours".

  1607. PCA denies that there was any mistake. It says that in the negotiations from beginning to end the term was always "minimum annual peak load factor of 38%". It says there was no mistake.

  1608. Although PP pleaded that it was entitled to rescind the relevant contracts for common mistake, it did not seek to establish the case for rescission based on common mistake and in final address confined the relief for common mistake to rectification and sought in respect of unilateral mistake, rescission and in the alternative, rectification.

    1609. Common mistake

  1609. In a claim for relief based upon common mistake, the party seeking the relief has the burden of establishing the following -

    1610. (i) That the parties had agreed to a term prior to recording it in writing;

    (ii) that the parties in stating the term in the written version mistakenly expressed their true common intention;

    (iii) that during the time after the term was agreed until the recording of it, it was the continuing intention of the parties that the term was as agreed;

    (iv) that if rectified as claimed the written contract would give effect to the common intention of the parties.

  1610. The burden of proof is on the party who alleges mistake and the standard is high. It has been described as "strong irrefragable evidence" as early as 1784 - see Countess of Shelburne v Earl of Inchiquin [1784] EngR 38; (1784) 1 Bro. C.C. 338 at 341 per Lord Thurlow and in 1939 as a "high degree of conviction" - per Greene MR in Crane v Heggeman-Harris Co (1939) 4 All E.R. 68 at 71.

  1611. In some cases the mistake is obvious. It can be traced with confidence from the negotiated agreed term to the error in the written document. These cases present little difficulty.

  1612. But in cases such as the present where there is a real dispute between the parties as to the alleged mistake, the standard of proof must remain high. There are many good reasons for this high standard.

  1613. First, in some cases - and this is an example - it is alleged by one party that there has been a mistake made by the party. The evidence often comes down to one person stating what his intention and state of mind was. In the absence of any other evidence the court is faced with a person stating that he was labouring under a mistake. Evidence, easy to assert, nearly impossible to disprove and very difficult to test.

  1614. Secondly, a party is bound by the terms of the contract made whether he read them or understood them. This is a general proposition subject to some exceptions but it is a very important starting point when considering the issue. See Watkins v Rymill (1884) 10 QBD 178. Sometimes a party may misunderstand a term, may not appreciate what it means but it is important that contracts solemnly and bona fide entered into should be enforced. Often parties do not read or read carefully the contract or misunderstand the language. Nevertheless the party has agreed and the other party relies on the apparent agreement. It would be indeed mischievous and destructive of certainty in dealings if a party could assert mistake to avoid an obligation without being put to strict proof.

  1615. Thirdly, in construing a contract, the objective of the court is to determine the meaning according to the common intention of the parties. This is an objective test. The intentions, hopes, aspirations and understandings of the parties are not relevant to the exercise.

  1616. Fourthly, a contracting party may misunderstand what the contract means, but it is important that a contract is upheld giving effect to the common intention of the parties determined on an objective basis. To do otherwise creates uncertainty in contracting which is detrimental to the expectations of contracting parties, the administration of law and certainty.

  1617. PP's defence and claim for rectification is based upon Mr Murphy's evidence.

  1618. The evidence is mainly based upon his state of mind and understanding with respect to the term. As I have stated PCA denies any mistake. Accordingly, PP carries a very heavy burden of persuading the court that a mistake has been made in the recording of the agreement between the parties.

  1619. The relevant date for contracts 39 - 41 is 28 November 1997 and for contracts 43 - 46, 29 May 1998. They were the dates when the contracts were made.

  1620. At the time when the contracts were made on 28 November 1997 two other contracts were agreed, confirmations were signed and exchanged and performance commenced on 1 January 1998. These are contracts 36 and 37 and each contained the term that the load factor was to meet the requirement - "minimum annual peak load factor of 38%".

  1621. The load profiles which were attached to the confirmations and which had been prepared by PCA met that requirement.

  1622. Mr Murphy checked the load profiles of these contracts when they were sent to PP in January 1998. He did the calculations to determine whether the load shapes met the requirement and thought they did. If in fact the load shape had to meet the requirement of a minimum load factor of 38% then clearly they did not. Mr Murphy did not ascertain any error in making the calculations. With hindsight he admitted he may have been careless. Another factor of some importance is that at no stage has any complaint been made concerning the alleged mistake to PCA and further, Mr Murphy did not appreciate, so he said, the difference between the two phrases until about October 1998 when in the course of preparing the documents for discovery and himself to give evidence, he appreciated the difference between the two terms.

  1623. Given those facts the burden resting upon PP of proving mistake justifying relief becomes heavier.

  1624. The term in its original form had been discussed in early 1997. In negotiations in May 1997 Mr Murphy in relation to long term pricing indicated a minimum load factor of 38% in each year.

  1625. The same term was used in further negotiations in June, July and August 1997.

  1626. At the end of June 1997 Mr Treacy commenced negotiations for a hedge contract in relation to a possible retail contract PCA was interested in for the company New Crest at Cadia in central New South Wales. Discussions took place and one component of the proposal was the 38% minimum load factor requirement.

  1627. In late August 1997 Mr Treacy indicated to PP that it was unlikely that PCA would proceed with the Cadia proposal.

  1628. Despite the fact that the Cadia matter was put on hold, PCA was anxious to use the proposal as a basis for further negotiations.

  1629. The summary of the proposal prepared by PCA on 15 July 1997 and sent to PP did refer to "a minimum load factor of 38%".

  1630. On 28 August 1997 Mr Treacy sent to Mr Murphy a copy of this proposal and wrote -

    1631. "We now wish to use this as a basis of a draw down product with the load details advised by 30/3/98. Please advise which aspects of the proposal are causing a concern to you."

  1631. It is noted that the proposal was to be used as a basis of the product.

  1632. Mr Daley within PCA prepared a document entitled "Ten Year Draw Down".

  1633. The opening words of the summary are -

    1634. "The components of this ten year draw down product have been based on the New Crest at Cadia previously agreed. The proposed features are:-"

    and thereafter are set out over two and a half pages the various details. Under the heading of "2.0 Volume of Energy and Load Shape" is a sub-clause 2.3 which read -

    "Minimum annual peak load factor of 38%."

  1634. There was a footnote at the end of that line which referred back to correspondence dated 15 July 1997 and 28 August 1997 "New Crest at Cadia".

  1635. Again, it is important to note that the components of the Ten Year Draw Down were "based on the New Crest at Cadia previously agreed". It formed the basis but was not alleged to be the same.

  1636. It is important to note that this is the opening proposal by PCA with respect to a Ten Year Draw Down Product which was to be used as a basis for future dealings.

  1637. This product was sent to Mr Murphy and the facsimile requested Mr Murphy to review the proposal and then stated - "Can we discuss, please?"

  1638. Mr Murphy received the document and said he read it. The date was 17 September 1997.

  1639. A meeting took place on 24 September 1997 at which Mr Daley and Dr Nelson, Messrs Murphy and Trethewy were present.

  1640. I accept the evidence of Mr Daley as to what happened at the meeting. He produced a copy of the draft which he had sent Mr Murphy a week earlier and commenced to go through it in order to outline each matter but having shortly commenced the exercise Mr Murphy enquired whether the document was the same that had been sent earlier. Mr Daley said "Yes, it was," and then Mr Murphy replied by stating that he had read it and he did not have any problems with the proposal. He expressly declined the invitation to go through each aspect of it.

  1641. Mr Murphy gave evidence that prior to this meeting he had read the document and when he saw clause 2.3 he thought that it had the same meaning as a minimum load factor of 38% which had been discussed in the past. He may have thought that but the fact was that the terms were expressed in different language. I do not accept he could have read the new term as meaning the same.

  1642. Further discussions took place and on 12 November 1997 Mr Treacy forwarded to Mr Murphy a facsimile covering a number of issues and attached a draft of the Ten Year Product "revised to take into account our previous discussions at our last meeting".

  1643. This product differed to the draft of 17 September 1997 and referred to the fact that it was based upon the previously agreed product dated 17 September 1997. It no longer contained any footnotes to any previous correspondence and also had attached to it a number of attachments with respect to prices, volumes and a meet the market clause.

  1644. At no stage was there any discussion concerning clause 2.3 or any query raised about it by Mr Murphy or anybody else from PP.

  1645. In oral evidence Mr Murphy said that when he read the first draft, namely, 17 September 1997 he noted the footnotes and went back to the previous correspondence.

  1646. I reject that evidence. It was unnecessary for him to do so. What was being put up was a proposal based on a previous proposal. There was no reason for him to do so. Further, he was familiar with the formula in the sense that it had been used before. If in fact he had have gone back to the previous proposal he would have observed the difference in the phraseology. Further, the evidence was not given in his written statement exchanged between the parties. He gave it orally.

  1647. At no stage did he ever raise the difference in phraseology with PCA.

  1648. The Draw Down Product was used as standard terms for contracts made on 28 November 1997 and 29 May 1998.

  1649. PP fails to establish common mistake. It does so on a number of grounds. First, I reject the evidence of Mr Murphy that he mistakenly believed after reading the Draw Down Product that the term was the same as the term expressed in the earlier proposal. Secondly, the fact was that PCA was not mistaken about the term. I accept the evidence of the PCA witnesses as to their understanding of what it meant. I also accept the evidence that they intended the term to apply to the annual peak hours. PCA was not labouring under any mistake.

  1650. PP fails to establish first that there was a common mistake and secondly, that there was any agreement prior to 28 November 1997 that the requirement concerning the load shape was to meet a minimum load factor of 38%.

  1651. The term which first appeared when negotiations commenced with the Ten Year Draw Down Product was "minimum annual peak load factor of 38%" and remained the same throughout all negotiations ultimately leading up to the adoption of the product and its incorporation in the disputed contracts and also contracts 36 and 37 which were performed in accordance with the term.

  1652. PP fails to establish any defence based upon common mistake or any basis for rectification of any of the relevant contracts.

    1653. Unilateral mistake

  1653. One starts with the general rule that there is no rescission or rectification of a contract where one of the parties is mistaken as to a term. This has always been the law. See Sells v Sells (1860) 1 Dr. and Fm at 42. However, there are exceptions to the general rule.

  1654. PP which carries a very heavy burden in order to establish any relief on the ground of unilateral mistake has to prove the following in a rectification claim -

    1655. "If (1) one party, A, makes an agreement under a misapprehension that the agreement contains a particular provision which the agreement does not in fact contain; and (2) the other party, B, knows of the omission and that it is due to a mistake on A's part; and (3) lets A remain under the misapprehension and concludes the agreement on the mistaken basis in circumstances where equity would require B to take some step or steps, depending on those circumstances, to bring the mistake to A's attention; then (4) B will be precluded from relying upon A's execution of the agreement to resist A's claim for rectification to give effect to A's intention." Per Kenny JA in Leibler v Air New Zealand Ltd (No 2) (1999) 1 VR 1 at p.14.

  1655. Proof of a unilateral mistake on the part of one of the contracting parties may also be the basis for rescission. In Taylor v Johnson [1983] HCA 5; (1983) 151 CLR 422 at 431 Mason ACJ, Murphy and Deane JJ said at p.432 -

    1656. "A party who has entered into a written contract under a serious mistake about its contents in relation to a fundamental term will be entitled in equity to an order rescinding the contract if the other party is aware that circumstances exist which indicate that the first party is entering into the contract under some serious mistake or misapprehension about the content or subject matter of that term and deliberately sets out to ensure that the first party does not become aware of the existence of his mistake or misapprehension. ... Moreover and perhaps more importantly, it is a principle which is best calculated to do justice between the parties to a contract in the situation which it contemplates. In such a situation it is unfair that the mistaken party should be held to the written contract by the other party whose lack of precise knowledge of the first party's actual mistake proceeds from wilful ignorance because, knowing or having reason to know that there is some mistake or misapprehension, he engages deliberately in a course of conduct which is designed to inhibit discovery of it." (Emphases added.)

  1656. The determinant is that there are circumstances which would make it unconscionable for the party seeking to uphold the agreement in its written form.

  1657. The cases support the proposition that one cannot rely upon unilateral mistake for any relief unless it is shown that the other party "had actual knowledge of the existence of the plaintiff's mistake at the time when the contract was signed". See Slade L.J. in Agip S.P.A. v Navigazione Alta Italia S.p. A. (1984) 1 Ll. Rep. 353 at 351.

  1658. Kenny JA in the Liebler case also stated the element as requiring proof of knowledge of the error.

  1659. In Commissioner for New Towns v Cooper (G.B.) Ltd (1995) Ch. 259 Stuart-Smith LJ stated that although the authorities supported that as a general proposition it may not be an exhaustive statement of the law because there may be circumstances where one party does not actually know but merely suspects that the other party is mistaken and takes advantage of it in some way. This may be a basis for relief.

  1660. Mr Murphy said that he made the mistake in that he believed the phrase in the Ten Year Draw Down Product meant the same thing as the earlier phrase in the earlier proposal.

  1661. I have already found that he did not refer back to the original proposal. I have little doubt that he was familiar with the original phrase because it had been used in a number of negotiations but I do not accept that he was of the opinion that the phrase in the Ten Year Draw Down Product was the same as the original phrase.

  1662. There are a number of features about Mr Murphy's conduct in the course of his trading with PCA which demonstrated a lack of care on his part. I do not believe that he thought the phrase was the same and was labouring under such mistake. The probabilities are high that he did not appreciate there was any difference at all because he did not turn his mind to it. This is supported by the fact that he did calculations in January 1998 in respect to contracts 36 and 37 and did not notice any difference, that contracts 36 and 37 had been performed and settled for many, many months prior to him realising in October 1998 that there was a difference in phraseology.

  1663. I reject his evidence that he believed that the load factor term in the Draw Down Product was the same as the term in the Newcrest at Cadia proposal and hence was labouring under any mistake.

  1664. PP fails to prove it was labouring under any mistake.

  1665. However, if I am wrong in that conclusion I am satisfied that PCA did not know or indeed even suspect that Mr Murphy was labouring under some misapprehension as to what the phrase meant. I accept the evidence of the PCA witnesses that it was their intention throughout that the requirement was "the peak load" and that they had no suspicion or inkling that Mr Murphy had been mistaken in any way.

  1666. This is supported by the objective evidence. The term in the first draft of the Draw Down Product in September 1997 was "peak load" and that term appeared throughout right all documentation through to 12 November 1997. Mr Daley and Dr Nelson were prepared to discussed the features of the product with Mr Murphy at the meeting on 24 September 1997. Mr Daley commenced to go through it and Mr Murphy interrupted him and said it was unnecessary, as he understood it.

  1667. Mr Murphy at no stage said, did or omitted to do anything which would have indicated to PCA's representatives that he was labouring under any mistake as to what the term meant. This is reinforced by the fact that the load shapes in respect of contracts 36 and 37 prepared and sent in January 1998 by PCA accorded with the peak load term and nothing was said about it. The contracts commenced.

  1668. PP failed to prove that anybody at PCA had actual knowledge of the alleged mistake or had a basis for suspecting that there was a mistake and accordingly fails to prove that important element of its proof. However, I would go one step further and say that I am satisfied that there was nobody at PCA who at any stage thought that Mr Murphy and PP were labouring under any mistake or indeed suspected that he or it may be labouring under any mistake. Further, PCA's representatives did not do anything which in any way misled or deceived Mr Murphy or anybody else at PP into believing that the term in the Draw Down Product was the same as the term which had been discussed in relation to previous negotiations.

  1669. PP fails in its defence based upon unilateral mistake and also fails in establishing any basis for rectification based upon unilateral mistake.

    1670. Counterclaim

  1670. PP alleged by way of counterclaim that in the course of their relationship, PCA made seven representations which contravened ss.52(1) of the Trade Practices Act 1974 and also that four also contravened s.53(g).

  1671. It claims, inter alia, damages for the alleged breaches.

  1672. In the course of final address it abandoned its causes of action based upon the second representation.

  1673. All told there are ten separate causes of action based upon the Trade Practices Act for damages and other relief.

  1674. This is not the first case and I have no doubt will not be the last case in which a defendant faced with a strong case against it trawls over some years of a relationship with the plaintiff and with the benefit of hindsight and a detailed consideration of all the facts comes to the view that what was stated at a particular time was arguably false or misleading and bases a cause of action on it.

  1675. Often the causes of action are nothing more than the desperate attempt by a defendant to make life difficult for a deserving plaintiff. Invariably they lead to energy, time and money being expended on a forlorn cause.

  1676. I have no doubt that the Legislature never intended that Part 5 of the Trade Practices Act should be used for such a purpose. Yet this case is another example.

  1677. PP, trawling over the facts, looking at documents, including the documents produced by PCA, fixes on a statement made on a particular day which arguably is false and then seeks to develop around that statement a cause of action based on an alleged breach of s.52 of the Act. The court quite rightly is sceptical and approaches such alleged causes of action with cynicism.

  1678. Part of the proof in a misrepresentation cause of action based upon s.52 is reliance upon the misrepresentation and detriment resulting from relying on misrepresentation. This involves a party giving evidence on a hypothetical basis of what it would have done if it had been told the truth at the time. Much time is spent by smart minds, usually the client with the lawyers, working out what the client would have done if it had been told the truth. In the circumstance of this case where the evidence reveals most of the causes of action were an afterthought, one cannot help but approach such evidence with scepticism.

  1679. Adopting what Lord Goff of Chieveley said in Smith v Littlewoods Organisation Ltd, supra, my educated reflex to this trawling operation in this case is that the alleged causes of action have little substance.

  1680. I have voiced my views in a previous decision in this court last year and I voiced them again to Mr Downes QC during the course of his address. However, as he pointed out, the legal practitioners have a duty to their client to present all arguable claims and defences. I agree, but in conducting litigation the lawyers must plant both feet firmly on the ground.

  1681. Having said that it is my duty to consider and determine each of the alleged causes of action.

  1682. At no stage did PP complain in writing prior to the delivery of the defence about the alleged misrepresentations, and Mr Murphy frankly conceded that he only appreciated the fact that there may have been false statements made in the last few months of 1998 when he was involved in considering the documents, including PCA's documents, and preparing himself to give evidence in the proceeding.

  1683. Mr Murphy gave the evidence concerning each of the alleged causes of action. The evidence followed a pattern. He stated the facts that allegedly constituted the misrepresentation and then stated what he would have done if he had known the true facts. The latter evidence followed a formula. He stated that if he had known the true facts he would have either further negotiated or broken off negotiations and entered into negotiations "with another facility provider".

  1684. I refer by way of example to paragraph 443 of his witness statement.

  1685. It provided in effect that if contract No. 33 contained a term that there was to be "a minimum annual peak load factor of 38%" then if he had appreciated there was a difference to a term expressed to be "a minimum load factor of 38%" "then I would have in further negotiations with Powercor stipulated as a term that there be a minimum load factor of 38%. In the event that Powercor had refused to accept a minimum load factor of 38%, then I would have entered into negotiations for Commodity Transaction Agreements with a total volume of 190 Gh per annum during the period from 1 July 1998 to at least 8 March 2007 with another facility provider."

  1686. He gives similar evidence in relation to the first alleged misrepresentation in relation to all the disputed contracts.

  1687. I am satisfied that in respect to the evidence in relation to the causes of action that Mr Murphy was assisted in preparing his evidence by PP's lawyers who in consultation with him devised the formula. Unfortunately for Mr Murphy the evidence contained obvious errors and he was the one who had to try and explain his evidence in cross-examination.

  1688. In relation to contracts 33 and 35 they did not contain a term that the load profile was to meet "a minimum annual peak load factor of 38%". His evidence in relation to these two contracts is totally irrelevant.

  1689. In respect to each of the alleged misrepresentations a formula is used in respect to each contract and one cannot but help be cynical when considering the evidence based upon what Mr Murphy would have done if he had known the true facts. His evidence contains many errors.

    1690. Section 52

  1690. Section 52 provides -

    1691. "(1) A corporation shall not, in trade or commerce, engage in conduct that is misleading or deceptive or is likely to mislead or deceive."

  1691. There is no dispute that PCA is a corporation and in dealing with PP it was engaged in trade and commerce.

  1692. PP has the burden of establishing each cause of action for breach of this section.

  1693. PP has to establish that PCA by its conduct, and that includes acts of commission and omission, PCA has made a misrepresentation to PP which was misleading or deceptive.

  1694. It is unnecessary for PP to prove that PCA intended to mislead or deceive.

  1695. The cause of action is not complete until the proof of loss or damage. The loss or damage must have been caused by the conduct in breach of the Act.

  1696. Normally, in order to establish the relevant nexus between the conduct complained of and the loss and damage the alleged victim usually seeks to prove that he relied upon the representation to his detriment. That is how PP puts its case.

  1697. A person who does suffer loss or damage by reason of the conduct of another person done in contravention of Part V of the Act may recover the loss or damage. See s.82.

  1698. Once the victim proves that he has suffered or is likely to suffer damage then this opens up the myriad of remedies available under s.87. The power is given to re-write contracts if necessary.

  1699. Although PP carries the burden of proving breach of s.52, nevertheless if a corporation makes a representation with respect to any future matter then the corporation has the burden of establishing that it had reasonable grounds for making the representation. See s.51A.

  1700. There is a dispute between the parties as to whether PP should have pleaded that it relied upon s.51A of the Act. There is nothing in its amended defence and counterclaim indicating that it was relying upon s.51A.

  1701. In accordance with the Rules of this Court, matters that could catch the other side by surprise should be pleaded, and it is my opinion that PP should have pleaded its reliance upon s.51A. This is reinforced by the fact that sometimes it is a difficult question to draw a line between a present fact and a future matter and the alleged wrongdoer should know how the case is being put. See Truth About Motorways Pty Ltd v Infrastructure Investments Ltd (1998) ATPR 41-633.

  1702. It is important in the conduct of litigation that parties are aware of the issues in the case but in my opinion in the present matter PCA is not prejudiced in any way by PP relying upon s.51A, if it in fact applies.

    1703. First representation

  1703. The first alleged misrepresentation relied upon by PP as a cause of action is pleaded as follows -

    1704. "That the words 'minimum annual peak load factor of 38%' in clause 2.3 of each document entitled 'Ten Year Draw Down' enclosed in the facsimiles dated 17 September 1997 from PCA to PP and 12 November 1997 from PCA to PP had the same meaning as the words 'minimum load factor of 38%' in the second clause 8 in the document entitled 'Proposal for New Crest at Cadia' enclosed in the facsimiles dated 15 July 1997 and 28 August 1997 from PCA to PP."

  1704. This cause of action raises similar factual issues to that relied upon by PP in its defence based on common mistake and unilateral mistake.

  1705. The issue comes down to whether the conduct of PCA leading up to the entry into the contracts on 28 November 1997 conveyed to PP, that is Mr Murphy, that the two terms were the same. He gave evidence that he believed that the term in the Ten Year Draw Down product meant the same as the term in the New Crest at Cadia proposal. I rejected that evidence in respect to the defence of mistake. But even if he did that evidence is of little moment.

  1706. The question is did PA represent that the two terms were the same?

  1707. On a plain meaning of both terms it is clear they are different.

  1708. PCA did not make the representation as pleaded.

  1709. It was submitted on behalf of PP that the footnotes in the first document, being the one dated 17 September 1997, made it clear that the clause 2.3 meant the same as the clause discussed in other negotiations including the New Crest at Cadia proposal.

  1710. In respect to this argument it is clear that the first draft of the Draw Down product dated 17 September 1997 was based on a proposal but it was not said to be the same proposal. Further, the phrase "minimum load factor of 38%" had been used on a number of occasions other than in the correspondence concerning New Crest at Cadia. The phrase in clause 2.3 is different.

  1711. There is no evidence that PCA ever represented the two phrases meant the same.

  1712. It is said that in the circumstances PCA had an obligation to point out the difference. I do not agree. The negotiations were between experienced parties who had negotiated many transactions. Each was seeking to maximise the benefit for itself under the contract. The wording was different. The new term was put forward in a proposal. PP was given every opportunity to study the proposal. It agreed to it.

  1713. In my opinion PP has not proven that PCA represented that the two terms had the same meaning. Accordingly the cause of action based on the first alleged representation fails.

  1714. PP has abandoned its causes of action based on the second representation.

    1715. The third representation

  1715. The third alleged misrepresentation is pleaded as follows -

    1716. "That during the period from approximately 18 August 1997 to 29 May 1998 PCA represented to PP that commodity transaction agreements incorporating the terms of the document entitled 'ten year draw down' enclosed in the facsimile dated 12 November 1997 from PCA to PP (the draw down product) would only be entered into with PP where PCA had entered into or was proposing to enter into back to back retail contracts for the supply of electricity." (Emphasis added.)

  1716. What PP is asserting is that PCA would only enter into contracts with PP incorporating the Ten Year Draw Down Product where it, PCA, had entered into or was proposing to enter into back to back retail contracts for the supply of electricity.

  1717. In other words it represented it would not on sell the hedge contract in the exempt futures market.

  1718. Mr Murphy stated in evidence that he only realised there may have been a misrepresentation when he saw the particulars delivered by PCA in support of its plea of equitable estoppel. These particulars showed that the hedge contracts were used to support a number of different retail contracts and in some instances other futures contracts.

  1719. There is an air of unreality about this alleged cause of action. There is no doubt from time to time PCA made reference to certain retail contracts which it was endeavouring to negotiate and in respect to which it was seeking a hedge contract from PP to minimise its risk.

  1720. PP complains that the hedge contracts were not only used for retail supply but were also used for the purpose of selling in the wholesale market, i.e. entering into a commodity derivative contract with another wholesaler, for example, a financier in respect of the hedge contract entered into with PP.

  1721. There is no doubt there is evidence that during the course of negotiations in the latter six months of 1997 and into the early months of 1998 that PCA represented that the commodity derivative contracts then under negotiation were to be used to hedge risks in relation to retail contracts. Indeed, that was the main case put by PCA in support of its claim for equitable estoppel.

  1722. The real issue comes down to whether or not it represented that it would exclusively use the commodity derivative contracts to support retail contracts and not use them for any other purpose. In other words, by its acts of commission and omission did PCA represent that the commodity derivative contracts would be used exclusively to support retail customers' contracts?

  1723. Mr Murphy accepted that he did not have any belief when the Draw Down Product was first discussed in August 1997 that the proposed contracts would only be used for retail contracts. The first draft of the Draw Down Product which is dated 14 September did not make any mention of confining customers to retail customers. Neither did the document dated 12 November 1997 which was the final form of the Draw Down Product. No distinction was drawn between retail and wholesale customers.

  1724. The representation pleaded is ambiguous. First, it fails to adequately identify the real complaint being made, in other words the gravamen of the misrepresentation, and secondly, the phrase "back to back retail contracts" fails to specify in what way the retail contracts were to be back to back.

  1725. The evidence of Mr Murphy including his cross-examination identified the real complaint and that is that PCA allegedly misrepresented to PP that the commodity derivative contracts entered into between the parties would only be used to hedge retail contracts for the supply of electricity and not to be used for on-selling in the wholesale market.

  1726. The phrase "back to back" was the subject of cross-examination of Mr Murphy. He stated that it meant the main feature of both contracts being the volume of electricity at any particular point in time would be the same. But he conceded that it was not expected the contracts would be exactly the same. To say the least, his cross-examination revealed uncertainty in his own mind as to what he really understood by the phrase "back to back".

  1727. Mr Murphy admitted that it was not until late 1998 when PCA delivered its reply that he first thought that PCA had made a misrepresentation. He then went through all the documentation discovered by both sides in order to ascertain the evidence which supported his belief. He drafted paragraph 456 of his witness statement on or about 4 March 1999. In this paragraph he asserted that as a result of reading facsimiles, the Ten Year Draw Down document and having conversations with Mr Treacy, Mr Daley and Dr Nelson he formed a belief.

  1728. He expressed that belief in his evidence-in-chief as -

    1729. "I believe that Powercor would only enter into commodity transaction agreements incorporating the terms of the Draw Downproduct where Powercor had entered into or was proposing to enter into back to back retail contracts for the supply of electricity."

  1729. He accepted that counsel for PP had assisted him in the wording of the paragraphs 456-465 concerning the third alleged misrepresentation.

  1730. He agreed in cross-examination that it was necessary to reconstruct his evidence after looking at all the documents and discussing it with the legal representatives for PP.

  1731. In answer to a question from the court as to when he formed the belief that the hedge contracts would only be used in respect of retail contracts he was somewhat uncertain but stated that it would have been at the latest 28 November and probably earlier. In later cross-examination he went back to what he said in his witness statement that he first began forming the belief on 26 August 1997. But the first draft of the Ten Year Draw Down document was not sent until 17 September 1997. His vagueness as to when he formed the belief, together with his uncertainty as to what was meant by "back to back" retail contracts and the fact that he reconstructed his evidence based upon all the documentation makes relying upon his evidence as to his belief fraught with danger.

  1732. But the issue comes down to whether or not PCA did convey by its conduct a misrepresentation that the hedge contracts made on 28 November 1997 and 29 May 1998 which incorporated the terms of the Draw Downdocument were to be only used to support retail contracts?

  1733. Another element of importance in proving the cause of action is the question of reliance and causation. Mr Murphy stated that he relied upon the truth and accuracy of the representation and said that if he had known it was false he would not have proceeded with the proposed hedge contract, but after some cross-examination in which he indicated some uncertainty, then sought in the alternative to establish that he would have negotiated better terms for PP in the transaction.

  1734. Another matter which adds to the scepticism about this cause of action is that part of the evidence relied upon by PP to prove falsity is the evidence given by Mr Reid and Mr Telford on behalf of PCA relating to its equitable estoppel claim. In so doing PP adopts a different stand to that taken when attacking the evidence which aimed at showing there was no link between the hedge contracts and any wholesale contracts. Indeed, as I have already held, the evidence provides at most a very tenuous link between the hedge contracts and wholesale contracts allegedly entered into in 1998.

  1735. In considering this cause of action it is important at the outset to state that there is no direct evidence that PCA made a representation at any time that the hedge contracts would only be used to support retail contracts. The topic was never raised by Mr Murphy with anybody from PCA. He accepted that he never raised the topic with Mr Treacy.

  1736. In addition, at no stage did Mr Murphy request a term in any of the 11 disputed contracts to the effect that the hedge contracts must only be used to support retail contracts.

  1737. What Mr Murphy seeks to rely upon is evidence drawn from various sources, namely, facsimiles, telephone and face to face conversations and the Draw Down Product to show that the inference was that the hedge contracts were to be used for retail contracts.

  1738. Whilst I accept that there is evidence which shows that the parties were talking about hedge contracts supporting retail contracts I am not satisfied that the evidence conveyed the representation that hedge contracts would only be used to support retail contracts and would not be used for on-selling in the wholesale market.

  1739. The pattern of trading which I have discussed earlier showed that by 28 November 1997 the parties had concluded 34 commodity derivative contracts, most of which were two-way swap contracts. Sometimes PCA identified the transaction by reference to a retail customer, for example, SECV, Visyboard, Air Liquide and Smorgon. Although the customers were named, the terms of the retail contracts were never disclosed. PP never requested and never received any information concerning the retail contracts and the fact was that what PCA did on its retail side was its business and not PP's business.

  1740. Further, the commercial reality was, as Mr Murphy conceded, that if a hedge contract was negotiated in respect to a particular retail contract there was always the possibility that the retail contract may not go through. In those circumstances it was open to PCA to on-sell.

  1741. As I have said on many occasions PP was in the market to sell commodity derivative contracts to minimise its risk in the supply of electricity and just as importantly to make money out of hedge contracts. It was happy to do business with PCA. It had transacted 34 concluded agreements. Taken in context that Mr Murphy knew PCA was involved in wholesale trading it lacks commercial reality to suggest that if Mr Murphy had found out that there was the possibility of a hedge contract or part of it being on-sold that he would have immediately ceased trading with PCA in respect of it. Indeed, when cross-examined in respect to this he shifted ground by saying that if that had happened he may have sought better terms.

  1742. In my opinion the evidence does not establish that PCA represented that the commodity derivative contracts which it concluded in November 1997 and May 1998 were only to be used in respect of retail contracts and not to be used for on-selling in the wholesale market.

  1743. In addition to the matters already referred to I rely in reaching that conclusion on a number of pieces of evidence.

  1744. The terms of the draft and final Draw Down Product do not confine the customers to retail contracts only. There is no term to that effect.

  1745. Secondly, Mr Murphy never raised with Mr Treacy the topic of only using the hedge for retail contracts. Thirdly, Mr Murphy was aware that Mr Johnson had come from the United States of America in July 1997 for the purposes of trading in the wholesale market. Fourthly, Mr Murphy knew by 13 November 1997 that PCA was in the wholesale market. This was reinforced on 11 March 1998. Fifthly, Mr Murphy in cross-examination accepted that the documents upon which he relied may have implied that the hedge would only be used for retail but there was no evidence that PCA would only sell by retail.

  1746. Sixthly, in my opinion, in considering the evidence upon which Mr Murphy relies, none of it conveyed to him that PCA would only use the eleven disputed contracts made in November 1997 and May 1998 to support retail contracts only.

  1747. Finally, Mr Murphy never discussed his belief with anyone in PP.

  1748. The reality is that this cause of action is an afterthought. I do not accept that Mr Murphy formed any belief at the relevant time as to what PCA was doing with the hedge contracts.

  1749. In my opinion PCA did not make the representation as pleaded.

  1750. Further, I am not persuaded by Mr Murphy's evidence that if he had been told what PCA may do in respect of some of the commodity derivative contracts by on-selling in the wholesale market that he would have done anything about it. The probabilities are he would have continued treading with PCA.

  1751. It follows that PP fails to prove its cause of action based upon the third representation.

    1752. Fourth representation

  1752. The fourth alleged misrepresentation pleaded by PP is as follows -

    1753. "That on 11 May 1998 PCA represented to PP that the option for the commodity transaction agreement it was seeking from PP related to a retail 'back to back' contract for the supply of electricity."

  1753. The cause of action is based upon a telephone conversation which occurred on 11 May 1998 and which was recorded.

  1754. In the course of that telephone conversation Mr Treacy raised the question of transacting a deal. The deal was concerned with disputed contract No. 12.

  1755. PCA has abandoned its claim in respect to disputed contract No. 12. It is clear from the evidence of Mr Murphy that if there was a misrepresentation it only caused damage if PP was bound by disputed contract No. 12.

  1756. Accordingly, the cause of action is no longer relevant. Mr Downes QC expressly abandoned it on the last day of the hearing.

    1757. Fifth representation

  1757. The fifth alleged misrepresentation is pleaded by PP as follows -

    1758. "That during the period from 26 August 1997 to 13 November 1997 PCA represented to PP that the meet the market clause in the retail contracts for the supply of electricity between PCA and Visy and Air Liquide which PCA had entered or was proposing to enter into would be back to back with the commodity transaction agreement then being negotiated with PP and was in substantially identical terms to meet the market clause in that agreement and that the review dates specified in that meet the market clause was or would be 1st March in each year from 2000 up to and including 2006." (Emphasis added.)

  1758. This cause of action concerns disputed contract 33 that is in respect to two retail contracts being the Visy and Air Liquide contracts.

  1759. Mr Murphy's evidence-in-chief is found in paragraph s 468 and 469 of his witness statement.

  1760. He said he formed a belief based on two facsimiles sent by Mr Treacy to him on 26 August 1997 and the Draw Down Product.

  1761. His belief was, first that the Meet the Market clauses in the proposed retail contracts were "in substantially identical terms to the meet the market clause in the Draw Down Product" and secondly, that the MTM clause review date in each retail contract "would be one of 1 January, 1 April, 1 July and 1 October of each year".

  1762. He stated that if he had been told the correct position he would have broken off negotiations and traded with other "facility providers".

  1763. The relevant period for the alleged misrepresentation is from 26 August 1997 to 13 November 1997 and during this period neither retail contract had been concluded. Each was the subject of negotiation. The Visy contract was concluded 15 months later on 18 February 1997 and Air Liquid six weeks later on 24 December 1997.

  1764. He admitted in cross-examination that paragraphs 468 and 469 of his witness statement were drafted with the assistance of counsel and that there were errors in relation to his evidence on the review date expected in the Meet the Market clause. He stated that what he meant to say was that the review date was 1 March in each year.

  1765. The first observation I make is to the uncertainty of what is meant by the phrase "was in substantially identical terms".

  1766. Contract 33 was concluded on 13 November 1997. It was known as the Visy/Air Liquide transaction because from the earliest negotiations PCA informed PP that it was to be a hedge contract for a large retail supply of electricity to the two companies named.

  1767. In fact, the negotiations between PP and PCA commenced in March 1997 and were well advanced by 18 August 1997 when PP forwarded a draft confirmation concerning the deal.

  1768. The initial discussions involved the inclusion of an MTM clause which PP was not prepared to include. Eventually it indicated it would do so and the terms were agreed in November 1997. As Mr Treacy stated in his evidence, "It was important to PCA that the MTM clause obtained from PP at least matched the obligations that PCA had to Visy."

  1769. Mr Murphy in his witness statement alleged that he formed the belief as set out in the pleaded representation based on a number of documents one of which was the Draw Down Product.

  1770. But this could not be so. The negotiations in respect of contract 33 and negotiations in respect to the Draw Down Product were separate and continued for a period side by side but were discrete. Contract 33 had its genesis in discussions held in March 1997. The Draw Down Product negotiations began in August 1997 and were treated separately from contract 33.

  1771. On 10 November 1997 Mr Treacy sent a facsimile to Mr Murphy which was concerned with "Visy Contract 0022" and no other negotiation. It set out the details and attached was a MTM clause which had been modified to take into account discussions had between Messrs Daley and Murphy.

  1772. On 12 November 1997 Mr Treacy sent a facsimile to Mr Murphy detailing the matters to be discussed at a meeting on 13 November 1997 and the Visy contract and the Ten Year Draw Down product were separately noted.

  1773. On 13 November Mr Murphy agreed to the Visy deal but the Draw Down Product was still under discussion on 13 November 1997. At that time the Visy contract and the Draw Down Product were treated separately. Whilst the MTM clause in contract 33 is in the same terms as that attached to the Draw Down Product the fact is that contract 33 was concluded separately from the Draw Down Product.

  1774. Insofar as Mr Murphy says he relied upon the Draw Down Product as a basis for forming his alleged belief I reject his evidence.

  1775. The contract contained its own term with respect to when it was to be exercised.

  1776. Further his evidence found in paragraph 469 concerning the review date being on one of four specified dates is wrong.

  1777. Based on his evidence PCA did not make the representation as pleaded.

  1778. However, if I am wrong I am for other reasons of the opinion that PP has not proved its cause of action.

  1779. As at 13 November 1997 PCA's retail staff were still negotiating the electricity contracts with the consumers.

  1780. In fact the Visy retail contracts were negotiated over the next 15 months and were not executed until 18 February 1999. All told there were three separate contracts. The Air Liquide retail contract was executed on 24 December 1997.

  1781. At no stage was PP informed as to the details of the retail contracts.

  1782. When the documents were discovered by PCA in late 1998 it was ascertained by PP that the MTM clauses in the retail contracts were different to the standard clause agreed to in the draw down products.

  1783. And so was born another s.52 cause of action. Mr Murphy and PP's legal team trawled through all the documents and events to seek support for what turned out to be the fifth alleged misrepresentation.

  1784. Mr Murphy has given evidence that he believed somewhere between 26 August 1997 and 13 November 1997 he formed the belief that the MTM clause in the retail contracts which PCA was proposing to enter into "was in substantially identical terms to the MTM clause in the Draw Down product". (Emphasis added.)

  1785. He then stated in his evidence-in-chief that "The review date specified in the MTM clause in the retail contract" would be one of 1 January, 1 April, 1 July or 1 October of each year. As I have stated Mr Murphy accepted the evidence was wrong.

  1786. Mr Murphy's belief is based upon a number of events. Whilst his evidence of what he believed PCA was representing to PP is relevant and admissible, in the end his belief is but part of the evidence and the question is whether as at 13 November 1997 PCA did represent what has been pleaded and whether it was false or misleading when represented.

  1787. A matter of substance against PP's claim is that there is no evidence of an express statement that the MTM clauses which would eventually be in the retail contracts then under negotiation would be "substantially identical" to the MTM clause in concluded contract 33.

  1788. It follows that PP has to establish facts which lead to the inference. Mr Treacy conceded that the MTM clause in the hedge contract should at least match the obligations under the retail contracts and he expected the review dates would match.

  1789. However, it must be pointed out that he was not responsible for negotiating the retail contracts. His job was to secure an adequate MTM clause to provide some measure of protection for PCA in its dealings with the retail consumers.

  1790. The alleged representation defies commercial reality. Contract 33, concluded on 13 November 1997, was to hedge substantial retail contracts then under negotiation. At that point in time there was no guarantee that the retail contracts would be concluded. No doubt PCA had high expectations that they would but as events showed Visy was not concluded for 15 months.

  1791. As at 13 November 1997 the reality was that the retail consumer could request any number of changes to the proposed MTM clause. There is no basis for thinking that as at 13 November 1997 the MTM clause in contract 33 would be totally or substantially in the terms in a MTM clause in any contract that PCA may enter into thereafter in respect to retail supply.

  1792. It is in this context that the court has to determine whether the representation was made and if so whether it was false at that date.

  1793. The evidence relied on comprises two facsimiles from Mr Treacy to Mr Murphy and a telephone call, all dated 26 August 1997.

  1794. The first facsimile of 26 August 1997 from Mr Treacy referred to "various issues", one of which was expressed as -

    1795. "* MtM - Wanted to ensure that if one of the customers who have a MtM clause, does trigger it, then it automatically triggers the MtM with PP. We will link the customer's name to the contract. This is to ensure that the differential in pricing between the Retail and Wholesale does not mean that Retail is triggered while Wholesale is not. This would need to cover all contracts that have a MtM clause in place."

  1795. A conversation took place between the two men after the receipt of a facsimile. It will be necessary to return to this conversation.

  1796. Subsequently to the conversation Mr Treacy sent another facsimile confirming the discussion and he wrote -

    1797. "* MtM - Agreed to link the retail customer with the hedge so if the customer triggered the MtM clause then this would flow through to the wholesale hedge contract with PP. I will organise some words to wrap around this for the contracts in question."

  1797. In fact Mr Treacy never did draft any words to "wrap around" the contracts. As I have found PCA did not proceed with the automatic triggering concept.

  1798. According to his witness statement Mr Murphy also relied upon the Draw Down Product. It attached a standard MTM clause which provided for a review on the contract anniversary.

  1799. Clause 3.2 of the product provided -

    1800. "3.2 The annual Meet-the-Market review may apply for any of the following dates so as to accommodate varying customer contract start dates each customer with a Meet-the-Market (ie not all customers will be entitled to an annual review) will have one of the following dates nominated as a review date. The proposed dates have been set on a quarterly basis using 1 January, 1 April, 1 July or 1 October of each year."

  1800. In my opinion none of the documentary material relied upon by PP establishes the representation as pleaded by it. A perusal of the MTM clause attached to the Draw Down Product shows that it contained a variety of terms.

  1801. There is nothing in the material which would support the belief of Mr Murphy as stated in his witness statement or as pleaded.

  1802. However, in cross-examination when asked about his belief and the date when he formed it, Mr Murphy stated that in a conversation with Mr Treacy on 26 August 1997 that Mr Treacy had said that the MTM clauses in the Visy and Air Liquide retail contracts "would be in essentially the same terms as the Meet the Market clause in the Pacific Power/Powercor hedge contract".

  1803. This piece of evidence came out in cross-examination, was not included in his witness statement, was not given in evidence-in-chief and was not put to Mr Treacy.
